The Triangle Condition for the Marked Random Connection Model
Matthew Dickson, Markus Heydenreich

Abstract
We investigate a spatial random graph model whose vertices are given as a marked Poisson process on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Edges are inserted between any pair of points independently with probability depending on the spatial displacement of the two endpoints and on their marks. Upon variation of the Poisson density, a percolation phase transition occurs under mild conditions: for low density there are finite connected components only, whilst for large density there is an infinite component almost surely. Our focus is on the transition between the low- and high-density phase, where the system is critical. We prove that if the dimension is high enough and the edge probability function satisfies certain conditions, then an infrared bound for the critical connection function is valid. This implies the triangle condition, and thus mean-field behaviour. We achieve this result through combining the recently established lace expansion for Poisson processes with spectral estimates.



August 9, 2026

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020).
60K35, 82B43, 60G55.

Keywords and phrases.
Random connection model, continuum percolation, inhomogeneous percolation, lace expansion, mean-field behaviour, triangle condition, Ornstein-Zernike equation, Boolean percolation

1
Introduction

1.1
Phase transition in percolation

For percolation, each edge of the hypercubic lattice is retained independently with fixed probability
p\in[0,1]
. It is well-known that the model undergoes a phase transition: in any dimension
d\geq 2
there is a
p_{c}\in(0,1)
such that for
p<p_{c}
there is no infinite cluster and if
p>p_{c}
there is a unique infinite cluster. Behaviour of the model at (and near) the critical threshold
p_{c}
is a major theme in probability theory. Indeed, we have good control on the critical behaviour in two regimes:

•

if
d=2
, then planarity and the link with complex analysis allows a refined description of the critical behaviour (to some extent only on the triangular lattice), see
[
SW01
,
Wer09
,
BR06
]
and references therein;

•

if
d
is large (at least
d>6
), then Hara and Slade
[
HS90
]
established the famous triangle condition, which implies that various critical exponents exist and take on their mean-field values
[
AN84
,
BA91
,
Ngu87
,
KN09
,
KN11
,
HH17
,
Hut22
]
, see also
[
HM20
]
for a corresponding result for site percolation. While it is generally believed that
d>6
is sufficient, current proof techniques only allow the proof for
d\geq 11

[
FH17b
]
. Already
[
HS90
]
show it for
d>6
under an additional spread-out assumption.

The focus of the present paper is on the second case, which we coin the “mean-field regime”.
Indeed, it has been shown that certain mean-field critical exponents exist for a wide range of (discrete and continuous) models provided that the dimension is large enough
[
HHLM22
,
HHS08
]
. This therefore substantiates the postulated
universality
of critical exponents.
The principle workhorse in showing the mean-field critical exponents in Euclidean spaces is the establishment of the triangle condition, which has also been verified for non-amenable graphs under varying assumptions
[
Sch01
,
Sch02
,
Hut20b
]
.
However, all of the above papers crucially rely on the independence of edge occupation, and the question we put forward is:

To what extent can dependency of the edge occupation change critical exponents?

The present work establishes the triangle condition for a class of
dependent
percolation models, and thus shows that the critical exponents do not change. Mean-field bounds on some critical exponents were proven recently in the context of Boolean percolation
[
DM23
]
, but a proof of the triangle condition in such situations is new.

We prove an infrared bound for the marked random connection model whenever
d\geq d^{*}
for some
d^{*}>6
, and thus (with the results of Caicedo-Dickson
[
CD24b
]
) establish mean-field critical exponents. Mean-field theory predicts that mean-field criticality is true for all dimensions
d>6
, and thus
6
is the upper critical dimension.
On the other hand, the triangle condition, and hence the mean-field value of various critical exponents, has also been established for
long-range percolation
in lower dimensions provided that the edge occupation probability is decaying slowly enough
[
HHS08
,
CS15
,
CS19
]
.
A further question, which is open, is thus: Can strongly correlated (but short-range) percolation models have an upper critical dimension different from 6?
A special instance (with negative answer to this question) is the
cable system for the Gaussian Free field
on lattices.
The very particular dependency structure of that model admits a rigorous analysis via loop soups. Based on an explicit calculation of the two-point function of this model by Lupu
[
Lup16
]
, Werner
[
Wer21
]
pointed out that the upper critical dimension is indeed
6
for this model, and recently Cai and Ding
[
CD24a
]
even verified that the
one-arm
critical exponent takes its critical value whenever
d>6
.
The fact that this strongly correlated model shows mean-field behaviour as soon as
d>6
is nevertheless most remarkable, as Drewitz, Prévost, and Rodriguez
[
DPR23
]
used links with potential theory to identify various critical exponents in dimension
d=3
, which differ from the (supected) exponents for independent edge occupation.

1.2
Complex networks

Random geometric graphs are versatile stochastic models for real networks. As many real-world networks exhibit certain stylised facts such as scale-free degrees, short distances and geometric clustering, it is clear that mathematical models of complex networks must share such features. While numerous different mathematical models for complex networks exist, it has been pointed out by
[
GHMM22
]
that the
weight-dependent random connection model
provides a unified approach that generalises many models for (infinite) geometric random graphs.
This is a continuum random graph model whose vertices are given as a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,1)
. If
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s,t\in(0,1)
, where edges
\big\{(x,s),(y,t)\}
are inserted with probability given by a connection function
\varphi((x,s),(y,t))
. A standard assumption is that
\varphi
is decreasing in the spatial distance
|x-y|
as well as in both marks
s
and
t
.

Relevance of this model comes from the fact that different choices for the connection function
\varphi
generalise various well-known random graph models such as Boolean percolation, scale-free Gilbert graph, scale-free percolation, soft random geometric graphs, long-range percolation, and spatial preferential attachment graphs. Results proven in the generalised setup are thus valid in great generality.

For some choices of
\varphi
, these models are known to be
robust
, i.e., infinite components exist for all edge densities; see
[
GLM21
,
GLM22
]
.
Our interest is in regimes where these graphs are non-robust and thus a proper percolation phase transition exist: if
\varphi
is small (scaled by a certain parameter) then there is no infinite cluster, but if
\varphi
is large, then an infinite cluster does appear. Investigation of such transitions is classical in percolation theory, and of great importance in modern probability.

The present paper is the first one to prove an infrared bound for an inhomogeneous spatial percolation model. While such results are well-known for the homogeneous case (i.e., without marks), there are hardly any results for the percolation phase transition for inhomogeneous models. A notable exception is Boolean percolation, where sharp phase transition is known
[
DCRT20
]
and the mean-field bounds on three critical exponents is identified
[
DM23
]
.

The key challenge is therefore, to overcome the dependencies imposed by the vertex weights. We prove the infrared bound under specific conditions, and verify these conditions for various concrete examples.

1.3
The Marked Random Connection Model

In this paper we consider a generalisation of the weight-dependent random connection model, which we first describe informally and refer to Section
3.1
for a formal construction. It is for our purposes more convenient to generalise the setup by considering a general measurable mark space
\mathcal{E}
and thus define the model on the space
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
.
Let
\nu={\rm Leb}\times\mathcal{P}
be a
\sigma
-finite measure on
\mathbb{X}
where
{\rm Leb}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathcal{P}
is a probability measure on
\mathcal{E}
. Let
\lambda>0
, and then
\lambda\nu
is an intensity measure on
\mathbb{X}
. The connection function is given by the measurable and symmetric
\varphi\colon\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\left[0,1\right]
. We furthermore require that
\varphi
is translation and reflection invariant in the position components. That is, if we denote
x=\left(\overline{x},a\right)
and
y=\left(\overline{y},b\right)
for
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
, we require that
\varphi\left(x,y\right)=\varphi(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,b)=\varphi(-\overline{x}+\overline{y};a,b)
.
We denote by
\eta
the Poisson point process with intensity measure
\lambda\nu
(which represents the vertex set of the random graph) and by
\xi
the full random graph including additional randomness for the formation of edges.

Once we have the concept of two vertices being adjacent, we can consider the concept of two vertices being
connected
. We say
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
are connected, and write
x\longleftrightarrow y
, if they are equal or there exists a path in
\xi
connecting the two vertices. By this we mean that there exists a sequence of distinct vertices
x=u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},u_{k+1}=y\in\eta
(with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
) such that
u_{i}\sim u_{i+1}
for all
0\leq i\leq k
. This definition can also be extended to connectedness on the RCM augmented by the vertex
x\in\mathbb{X}
(resp.
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
), where
\left(\eta,\xi\right)
is replaced by
\left(\eta^{x},\xi^{x}\right)
(resp.
\left(\eta^{x,y},\xi^{x,y}\right)
).
Therefore given a pair of points
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
we can consider the event
\left\{x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}\right\}
, and in turn the probability of this event. We define the pair-connectedness (or two-point) function
\tau_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\left[0,1\right]
to be

\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right):=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}\right).

(1.1)

Using the connection and pair-connectedness functions
\varphi,\tau_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\left[0,1\right]
as kernel functions, we construct associated linear integral operators. Where
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
is the Hilbert space of square integrable functions on
\mathbb{X}
, we define the connection operator,
\varPhi\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
, and the pair-connectedness operator,
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
. For
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
, we have

\varPhi f\left(x\right)=\int\varphi(x,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right),\qquad\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}f\left(x\right)=\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right),

(1.2)

for
\nu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{X}
. Note that the composition of two of these operators can be seen as a “convolution over
\mathbb{X}
” of the kernel functions. Where we rarely use it, we reserve the
\star
notation for straight-forward convolution over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
only. For example, given
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}
,

\left(\varphi\left(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2}\right)\star\varphi\left(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4}\right)\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi\left(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\varphi\left(\overline{y};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{y}.

(1.3)

We will also consider Fourier transformed versions of these operators. For a Lebesgue-integrable function
g\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the Fourier transform of
g
to be

\widehat{g}(k)=\int\text{e}^{ik\cdot\overline{x}}g\left(\overline{x}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}

(1.4)

for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
k\cdot\overline{x}=\sum^{d}_{j=1}k_{j}\overline{x}_{j}
denotes the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For given marks
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
, we define
\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)
and
\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(k;a,b)
as the Fourier transforms of
\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)
and
\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)
respectively. We use these
\mathcal{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
functions to define linear operators on
L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
, the space of square integrable functions on
\mathcal{E}
. For each
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define the operators
\widehat{\varPhi}(k),\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
by their action on
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
:

\left[\widehat{\varPhi}(k)f\right]\left(a\right)=\int\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)f(b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right),\qquad\left[\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)f\right]\left(a\right)=\int\tau_{\lambda}(k;a,b)f(b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right),

(1.5)

for
\mathcal{P}
-almost every
a
.

Our main aims of this paper are to derive an infrared bound for the two-point function (via its integral operators), and to prove that the so-called
triangle condition
is satisfied by a large family of models in sufficiently high dimension. For
\lambda>0
we define

\triangle_{\lambda}:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{x,y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,v)\tau_{\lambda}(v,y)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right).

(1.6)

It is relatively simple to show that
\triangle_{\lambda}
is finite for sub-critical
\lambda
and infinite for super-critical
\lambda
, but a model is said to satisfy the triangle condition if
\triangle_{\lambda}
is finite at criticality.

An aside: the essential supremum taken in (
1.6
) is with respect to the measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{X}
. We will be taking the essential supremum many times in this paper over various spaces, and each space will naturally have a different measure implicitly associated with it. It should be clear from the context what that measure is.

1.4
Overview

In Section
2
we provide the main results of the paper. We begin by making precise the idea of a critical intensity in Section
2.1
. These propositions are largely independent of the rest of the paper and their proofs can be found in Appendix
A
. The main concern of the paper is in proving the results of Section
2.2
. Given the set of assumption
(.1)
,
(.2)
, and
(.3)
, we prove that the triangle condition is satisfied and we provide an infrared bound on the two-point operator in terms of the adjacency operator. Some examples of models that satisfy these assumptions are provided in Section
2.3
, with the proofs that they satisfy them given in Appendix
B
.

Before we begin with the proof proper, we give some preliminaries in Section
3
. First Section
3.1
gives a formal construction of the RCM. Then we have Sections
3.2
and
3.3
, which give standard but important results in probability and linear algebra respectively that we will frequently use in our arguments. The probabilistic lemmas will be familiar to students of point process theory and percolation theory, with the caveat that we require them to apply with the augmented space
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
taking the place of the more usual
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This adds no complication. The linear algebra results may well be foreign to a probabilistic audience, but will nevertheless be fundamental to our proof. Here we provide a sufficient technical description of the linear operators we construct and the Hilbert spaces upon which they act. Perhaps the most important result we call upon from this section is the Spectral Theorem (Theorem
3.5
), which allows us to relate bounded self-adjoint linear operators to a multiplication operator by some unitary map. Multiplication operators are far simpler to work with, and we use the theorem many times towards the end of our proof. For operators on finite dimensional vector spaces, the spectral theorem is equivalent to saying that a Hermitian matrix can be diagonalized over
\mathbb{R}
. The proofs of the remaining lemmas in this subsection can be found in Appendix
C
.

In Section
4
we describe the finite-term lace expansion. This describes the two-point function in terms of the adjacency function, the lace expansion coefficient functions, and a remainder function. The presence of marks makes little difference here and this step is largely the same as for
[
HHLM22
]
. The novelty is that we formulate the resulting convolution equation as an operator equation.

Next we show that the finite-term lace expansion converges in the sub-critical regime to an Ornstein-Zernike equation (OZE). The first step (in Section
5.1
) is to bound the lace expansion coefficient operators with long implicitly defined integral expressions which can be decomposed into simpler integral expressions like the triangle diagram (which is also implicitly defined). While the bounds derived here may not be surprising to those familiar with lace expansion arguments, the arguments in
[
HHLM22
]
make use of translation symmetry which does not apply if marks are present. The details of the proofs of these bounds can be found in Appendix
E
. In Section
5.2
these integral expressions are in turn bounded in terms of a bootstrap function. Once again, the marks introduce complications that mean that this is not a simple modification of the arguments in
[
HHLM22
]
. The proofs of the results in this section can be found in Appendix
F
. These bounds on the the lace expansion coefficient operators culminate in Proposition
5.22
, which proves that the OZE holds for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
.

Section
6
takes this further and shows that the convergence in the sub-critical regime is in fact uniform in
\lambda
. This is shown by deriving a uniform upper bound on the bootstrap function by means of a forbidden-region argument in Proposition
6.2
. A vital part of this argument is the differentiability of
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
and related functions. The proofs of these differentiability properties can be found in Appendix
D
.

This uniform convergence allows for many results to be taken from the sub-critical regime into the critical regime in Section
7
. Proposition
7.1
uses this uniformity to prove extend the bound on the triangle diagram to
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
, while Proposition
7.2
shows that the bounds on the lace expansion coefficient operator and the OZE also extend to the critical threshold.

2
Results

As we will be working with operators, we will require some concept of size for these operators. This will take a number of forms, as some will have advantages in various scenarios.

Let
\left(\mathcal{X},\mu\right)\in\left\{\left(\mathbb{X},\nu\right),\left(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{P}\right)\right\}
, and let
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\in\left\{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right),L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\right\}
denote the corresponding space of square integrable functions. In practice it will be clear which is being used. We will require five various ideas of size. First suppose
H\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
is a bounded linear operator. Then the
operator norm
is given by

\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup_{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X}):f\neq 0}\frac{\left\lVert Hf\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}},

(2.1)

where
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
is the standard
L^{2}
-norm on
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
. In particular, we have
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup\left\{\left\lvert z\right\rvert:z\in\sigma(H)\right\}
, where
\sigma\left(H\right)\subset\mathbb{C}
denotes the spectrum of
H
. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the inner product on
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
defined by (
3.12
). If
H
is also self-adjoint, then
\langle f,Hf\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
and we define the
spectral supremum

\mathbb{S}\left(H\right):=\sup_{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X}):f\neq 0}\frac{\langle f,Hf\rangle}{\langle f,f\rangle}.

(2.2)

In particular, the self-adjointness of
H
implies that
\sigma(H)\subset\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)=\sup\left\{z:z\in\sigma(H)\right\}
. Note that
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
is not a norm: it is not necessarily non-negative.

Now suppose
H\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is a bounded linear integral operator with measurable kernel function
h\colon\mathcal{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
. Then we have the explicit norms:

\displaystyle\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert h\left(\cdot,y\right)\right\rVert_{1}=\esssup_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\int\left\lvert h\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert\mu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right),

(2.3)

\displaystyle\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert h\left(\cdot,y\right)\right\rVert_{2}=\esssup_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\left(\int\left\lvert h\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert^{2}\mu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{y\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lVert h\left(\cdot,y\right)\right\rVert_{\infty}=\esssup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert h\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert.

(2.5)

2.1
Critical Intensities

Before we move on to our results describing behaviour at and around criticality, we will first explore how we are defining criticality. In our main results in Section
2.2
we use the
operator critical intensity

\lambda_{O}
, defined by

\lambda_{O}:=\inf\left\{\lambda>0:\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\infty\right\}.

(2.6)

Whilst the divergence of a two-point operator has been used previously to define a critical threshold in random graphs before (for example in
[
BJR07
,
Hut19
,
Hut20a
]
), at first glance it may seem to be quite far removed from the standard probabilistic interpretations of a percolation transition. A variety of transition thresholds for the continuum Boolean disc model are discussed in
[
Roy90
,
Gou08
,
DCRT20
]
. However, we are able to show that under certain conditions the operator critical intensity coincides with more familiar interpretations.

For
\lambda\geq 0
, we define the two functions
\theta_{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{E}\mapsto\left[0,1\right]
and
\chi_{\lambda}\colon\mathcal{E}\mapsto\left[0,+\infty\right]
as

\displaystyle\theta_{\lambda}(a)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},a)\right\rvert=\infty\right),

(2.7)

\displaystyle\chi_{\lambda}(a)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},a)\right\rvert\right].

(2.8)

For each
p\in\left[1,+\infty\right]
, we define corresponding functions
\theta^{(p)}\colon\lambda\mapsto\lVert\theta_{\lambda}\rVert_{p}
and
\chi^{(p)}\colon\lambda\mapsto\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\rVert_{p}
. These suggest the critical intensities

\displaystyle\lambda_{c}^{(p)}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\lambda\geq 0:\lVert\theta_{\lambda}\rVert_{p}>0\right\},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\lambda_{T}^{(p)}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\lambda\geq 0:\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\rVert_{p}=\infty\right\}.

(2.10)

Note that the critical intensity
\lambda_{T}^{(\infty)}
corresponds to the critical intensity
\lambda_{1\to 1}
used in
[
Dic24
]
and analogous to
p_{1\to 1}
used in
[
Hut19
,
Hut20a
]
. On the other hand,
\lambda_{O}
corresponds to to the critical intensity
\lambda_{2\to 2}
and is analogous to
p_{2\to 2}
used in these references.

Some relations between these intensities can be swiftly deduced without requiring the RCM to satisfy any special conditions.

•

Their definitions clearly show that
\theta_{\lambda}(a)>0
implies
\chi_{\lambda}(a)=\infty
. Therefore
\lambda^{(p)}_{c}\geq\lambda^{(p)}_{T}
for all
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
.

•

If
\left\lVert\theta_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{p}>0
for some
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
, then it is non-zero on some
\mathcal{P}
-positive set, and therefore
\left\lVert\theta_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{q}>0
for all
q\in\left[1,\infty\right]
. Therefore
\lambda_{c}^{(p)}=\lambda_{c}^{(1)}
for all
p\in\left[1,+\infty\right]
, and we denote this value simply by
\lambda_{c}
.

•

By Jensen’s inequality,
1\leq p_{1}\leq p_{2}\leq\infty
implies the ordering
\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{p_{1}}\leq\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{p_{2}}\leq\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty}
. Therefore we have
\lambda_{T}^{(1)}\geq\lambda^{(p_{1})}_{T}\geq\lambda^{(p_{2})}_{T}\geq\lambda^{(\infty)}_{T}
.

With some of the techniques developed in this paper it is possible to relate
\lambda_{O}
to the intensities
\lambda^{(1)}_{T}
,
\lambda^{(\infty)}_{T}
, and
\lambda_{c}
described here.

Proposition 2.1
.

The critical intensities satisfy
\lambda^{(\infty)}_{T}\leq\lambda_{O}\leq\lambda^{(1)}_{T}\leq\lambda_{c}
. If

\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}<\infty,

(2.11)

then we also have
\lambda^{(p)}_{T}=\lambda_{O}
for all
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
.

This proof of Proposition
2.1
uses Mecke’s equation, Lemma
3.2
, Lemma
3.6
, and some simple probabilistic ideas. The details can be found in Appendix
A
.

The coincidence of critical intensities for percolation in marked random connection models has also been considered by
[
MMS88
,
Gou08
,
DCRT20
]
in the specific case of a family of Boolean defect models, including Boolean disc models with random bounded radii. In our terminology they introduced a new mark,
\dagger
, which forms connections as if it had radius
0
, and proved that
\theta_{\lambda}\left(\dagger\right)=0
if and only if
\chi_{\lambda}\left(\dagger\right)<\infty
. Therefore if we define

\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right):=\inf\left\{\lambda\geq 0:\theta_{\lambda}\left(\dagger\right)>0\right\},\qquad\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right):=\inf\left\{\lambda\geq 0:\chi_{\lambda}\left(\dagger\right)=\infty\right\},

(2.12)

this gives
\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right)=\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)
. The following Proposition uses this to prove that for the Boolean disc model with bounded radii, all the critical intensities we have considered are equal.

Proposition 2.2
.

In the case of the Boolean disc model with bounded radii,

\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right)=\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)=\lambda_{c}=\lambda_{O}=\lambda_{T}^{(p)},

(2.13)

for all
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
.

To prove Proposition
2.2
we show
\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)\leq\lambda^{(p)}_{T}
by considering points in
\eta
adjacent to the root point with mark
\dagger
. The details can be found in Appendix
A
.

2.2
Two-Point Operator

The majority of this paper is concerned with describing the two-point function and the associated two-point operator. Our results will require Assumption
H
described below to hold. This can be considered to be a generalisation of the “finite variance” models described by
[
HHLM22
]
- extending to cases with multiple marks.

Assumption H
.

We require
\varphi
to satisfy the following properties:

(
H
.1)

For all dimensions
d
we have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)<\infty
, and there exists a
d
-independent constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right),

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(2.15)

(
H
.2)

There exist
d
-independent constants
C_{1}\in\left(0,1\right)
and
C_{2}>0
such that

\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(0\right)\right)}\leq C_{1}\vee\left(1-C_{2}\lvert k\rvert^{2}\right).

(2.16)

(
H
.3)

There exists a function
g\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with the following three properties. Firstly, that
g(d)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
. Secondly, that

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)^{-3}\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2})\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4})\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{5},a_{6})\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)\leq g(d).

(2.17)

Thirdly, that the family of sets
\left\{B\left(x\right)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}}
given by

B\left(x\right):=\left\{y\in\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)^{-2}\int\varphi\left(x,u\right)\varphi\left(u,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)>g\left(d\right)\right\}

(2.18)

satisfy
\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\nu\left(B\left(x\right)\right)\leq g\left(d\right)
.

Remark 2.3
.

We have some remarks regarding these assumptions.

•

By Jensen’s inequality, the condition (
2.14
) in
(.1)
also implies that

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq C\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right).

(2.19)

We will often use these bounds (the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
bound in particular) more directly than (
2.14
) itself.

•

From (
2.17
) and (
2.19
) we can use a supremum bound to deduce that analogous bounds hold for the convolution of
m
adjacency functions (for
m\geq 3
) with corresponding bound
C^{m-3}g(d)
.

•

The sets
\left\{B\left(x\right)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}}
appearing in
(.3)
are all measurable - this follows from the measurability of
\varphi
. Furthermore, the symmetry of
\varphi
implies that
y\in B\left(x\right)\iff x\in B\left(y\right)
.

•

In stating our theorems, we will use a parameter
\beta=\beta(\varphi,d)
that depends upon
g(d)
. The definition depends upon the asymptotic properties of
g(d)
in that

\beta(d):=\begin{cases}g(d)^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{3}{2d}}d^{-\frac{3}{2}}&:\lim_{d\to\infty}g(d)\rho^{-d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}=0\qquad\forall\rho>0,\\
g(d)^{\frac{1}{4}}&:\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.20)

That is,
\beta(d)=g(d)^{\frac{1}{4}}
unless
g(d)
approaches zero particularly quickly. The parameter
\beta
will reappear many times in this paper - often in the case where
\beta
is ‘small.’ This corresponds to ‘large’ dimension
d
, and in particular, where the Landau notation
\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)
is used the asymptotics are as
d\to\infty
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose
\varphi
and
\nu
satisfy the assumptions
(.1)
,
(.2)
, and
(.3)
. Then there exists a
d^{*}>6
and
C>0
such that for
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
there exists an operator
\varPi_{\lambda}
with
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq C\beta
and a family of operators
\left\{\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq C\beta
, such that for all
d\geq d^{*}
the following statements are satisfied.

•

For
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
the bounded operator
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
satisfies the Ornstein–Zernike equation:

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}=\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right).

(2.21)

•

For
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, the bounded operator
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
satisfies the Ornstein–Zernike equation in Fourier form:

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)=\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)+\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right).

(2.22)

Theorem 2.5
.

If
\varphi
and
\nu
satisfy the assumptions
(.1)
,
(.2)
, and
(.3)
, then there exist
d^{*}>6
and
C>0
such that for all
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
and
d\geq d^{*}

\displaystyle\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+C\beta}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(0\right)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}\vee 1\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}.

(2.23)

and
\triangle_{\lambda}\leq C\beta^{2}
.

For the following two results, we call upon a strong form of irreducibility:

Assumption I
.

“Some mark can be connected to every other mark in exactly
k
steps for some
k
.” That is,

\esssup_{a_{0}\in\mathcal{E}}\,\sup_{k\geq 1}\,\essinf_{a_{k}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\prod^{k}_{i=1}\widehat{\varphi}\left(0;a_{i},a_{i-1}\right)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes(k-1)}\!\left(\mathrm{d}\vec{a}_{\left[1,k-1\right]}\right)>0.

(2.24)

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose assumptions
H
and
I
hold. Then for
d\geq d^{*}
the critical percolation function
\theta_{\lambda_{O}}(a)=0
for
\mathcal{P}
-almost every
a\in\mathcal{E}
.

This infrared bound and particularly the resulting triangle condition, have immediate consequences on various critical exponents.
Such implications have been proven in the context of independent lattice percolations, see e.g.
[
AB87
,
AN84
,
BA91
]
; recently Caicedo and the first author
[
CD24b
]
extended it to continuum percolation models with vertex weights:

Corollary 2.7
.

If
\varphi
and
\nu
satisfy the assumptions
H
and
I
, then there exist
d^{*}>6
such that for all
d\geq d^{*}
there exist constants
C,C^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle C\left(\lambda_{c}-\lambda\right)^{-1}\leq

\displaystyle\;\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C^{\prime}\left(\lambda_{c}-\lambda\right)^{-1}\quad\text{for $\lambda<\lambda_{c}$,}

(2.25)

\displaystyle C\left(\lambda-\lambda_{c}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\;\left\lVert\theta_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{p}\leq C^{\prime}\left(\lambda-\lambda_{c}\right)\qquad\quad\text{for $\lambda>\lambda_{c}$,}

(2.26)

\displaystyle Cn^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq

\displaystyle\;\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{c}}\left(\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(0,{a}\right)\right\rvert\geq n\right)\leq C^{\prime}n^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\text{for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$}

(2.27)

for all
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
and
\mathcal{P}
-almost all
a\in\mathcal{E}
.
In other words, the critical exponents
\gamma
,
\beta
and
\delta
(as defined in
[
CD24b
]
) exist and take on their mean-field values 1, 1, and 2, respectively.

The assertions are readily implied by Theorems 1.8, 1.11, 1.12 of
[
CD24b
]
. Indeed, these theorems have three assumptions: Assumption (D1) (in that paper) is verified through our Hypothesis
(.1)
, Assumption (D2) is precisely our Assumption
I
, and their Assumption (T) is precisely guaranteed by the triangle condition in Theorem
2.5
.

This infrared bound can also be related directly to the two-point function by Lemma
3.2
via

\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k;a,b\right)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(2.28)

Remark 2.8
.

Note that in Theorem
2.4
we say there exists some
d^{*}>6
such that the results hold for all
d\geq d^{*}
. If we were willing to say that there exists some
d^{*}>10
instead (which would not change the strict logical content of the result), then the proof would be far simpler and Assumptions
(.1)
and
(.3)
could be weakened. The
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
in (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) could be replaced with
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2,\infty}
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
respectively, and (
2.17
) could be replaced with
\left\lVert\varPhi^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq g(d)\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)^{3}
. We do not pursue this approach because it is expected that a very similar argument to that presented here (with context appropriate changes to the assumptions) would prove the corresponding result for the spread-out model in
d>6
. For the simpler argument and weaker conditions, we would only get the spread-out result in
d>10
, and this
would
be a logically weaker statement.

2.3
Examples of Models

It is natural to ask if there are any models for which
\varphi
and
\nu
do indeed satisfy
(.1)
,
(.2)
, and
(.3)
. All the models we describe here also satisfy the condition (
2.11
) for every dimension
d
.
We prove in Appendix
B
that all the models below do satisfy our assumptions.

Single Mark Finite Variance Model.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}
is a singleton, so essentially
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu={\rm Leb}
. Then the finite variance models described in
[
HHLM22
]
all satisfy our conditions. These include the Poisson blob model (or the spherical Boolean model) in which
\varphi(\overline{x})=\mathds{1}_{\{\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert\leq r_{d}\}}
where
r_{d}
is the radius of the unit volume
d
-ball, and the Gaussian connection model with
\smash{\varphi}(\overline{x})=\left(2\pi\right)^{-d/2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert^{2}\big)
.

Space-Mark Factorisation Model

Suppose now that the connection function admits the factorisation
\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)=\overline{\varphi}(\overline{x})K(a,b)
. We require that
\overline{\varphi}
satisfies the conditions of a single mark finite variance model, and that the (
d
-independent) mark-kernel
K\colon\mathcal{E}^{2}\to\left[0,1\right]
is symmetric and measurable. Note that this means that the integral operator
\mathcal{K}\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
with kernel function
K
is a bounded self-adjoint operator with operator norm
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 1
. We further suppose that
\mathbb{S}\left(\mathcal{K}\right)=\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. Then the connection operator is a bounded operator on the mark-space multiplied by a scalar function of spatial position. This formulation places no constraint on the number of marks, permitting even models with uncountably many marks.

Marked Multivariate Gaussian Model.

Let
\Sigma\colon\mathcal{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a measurable map where for every
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
,
\Sigma\left(a,b\right)
is itself a symmetric positive definite covariance matrix. We further require that there exists
\Sigma_{\max}<\infty
and
\Sigma_{\min}=\Sigma_{\min}(d)>0
such that the spectrum
\sigma(\Sigma(a,b))\subset\left[\Sigma_{\min},\Sigma_{\max}\right]
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
and dimension
d
. Then for
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(d)>0
such that
\mathcal{A}^{2}\leq\left(2\pi\right)^{d}\det\Sigma(a,b)
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
and
\limsup_{d\to\infty}\mathcal{A}^{2}\left(4\pi\Sigma_{\min}\right)^{-d/2}=0
, let the connection function be given by

\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)=\mathcal{A}\left(2\pi\right)^{-d/2}\left(\det\Sigma(a,b)\right)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\overline{x}^{\intercal}\Sigma(a,b)^{-1}\overline{x}\right).

(2.29)

This class of model includes examples of models with (uncountably) infinitely many marks, and for which the mark and spatial behaviours are truly coupled. For a concrete example, consider
\mathcal{E}=\left[\tfrac{1}{4\pi},1\right]
with
\Sigma(a,b)=(a+b)\mathds{1}
and
\mathcal{A}=1
.

Bounded-Volume Boolean Disc Model.

Let
\left(\left[0,1\right],\Sigma_{\left[0,1\right]},\mathcal{P}\right)
be a separable probability space and
\left\{R_{d}\right\}_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of measurable functions
R_{d}\colon\left[0,1\right]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that there exist
0<c_{1}<1/\sqrt{8\pi\text{e}}
and
V^{({\rm max})}>0
such that

R^{({\rm min})}_{d}:=c_{1}d^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq R_{d}\left(a\right)\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\left(V^{({\rm max})}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}=:R^{({\rm max})}_{d},

(2.30)

for all
a\in[0,1]
. Note that as
d\to\infty
, Stirling’s formula gives that the upper bound
R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\sim\sqrt{d/8\pi\text{e}}
. It is also required that there exist
\epsilon>0
and
c_{2}>0
such that

\mathcal{P}\left(R_{d}>\left(1-\frac{c_{2}}{d}\right)R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\right)>\epsilon

(2.31)

for all
d
. Then let the connection function be defined by

\varphi_{d}(\overline{x};a,b)=\mathds{1}\left\{\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert\leq R_{d}(a)+R_{d}(b)\right\}.

(2.32)

It is also perhaps worth mentioning a model that
does not
satisfy condition
H
. Such an example would be a Boolean disk model with random radii for which
\mathcal{P}
gives positive measure to arbitrarily large radii. No matter the decay rate of
\mathcal{P}
for large radii, the presence of the suprema in (
2.14
) and (
2.15
) ensure that
(.1)
cannot be satisfied. Similarly the condition (
2.11
) is also not satisfied for such a model.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Formal Construction of the RCM

We choose to construct our RCM in largely the same way as
[
HHLM22
]
, with appropriate replacements of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the marked space
\mathbb{X}
. As they did, we follow
[
LZ17
]
in defining the RCM,
\xi
, as an independent edge-marking of a Poisson point process
\eta
.

Recall
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
. We denote by
\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
the set of all at most countably infinite subsets of
\mathbb{X}
and accompany it with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{X})
generated by the
sets
\{A\in\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}\right):|A\cap B|=k,B\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}},k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
,
where
\left\lvert A\cap B\right\rvert
denotes the cardinality of
A\cap B
. Then a point process on
\mathbb{X}
is a measurable mapping
\zeta\colon\Omega\to\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
for some underlying probability space
\left(\Omega,\Sigma_{\Omega},\mathbb{P}\right)
. The intensity measure of such a point process is then the measure on
\mathbb{X}
given by
B\mapsto\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\zeta\cap B\right\rvert\right]
for
B\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}}
. Let
\zeta
be a point process on
\mathbb{X}
with a
\sigma
-finite intensity measure. Then there exist measurable
\pi_{i}\colon\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\to\mathbb{X}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that almost surely

\zeta=\left\{\pi_{i}\left(\zeta\right):i\leq\left\lvert\zeta\cap\mathbb{X}\right\rvert\right\}.

(3.1)

For a
\sigma
-finite non-atomic measure on
\mathbb{X}
, say
\nu
, a Poisson
point process (PPP) on
\mathbb{X}
with intensity measure
\nu
is a point
process
\zeta
such that the number of points
\left\lvert\zeta\cap B\right\rvert
is

\text{Poi}(\nu(B))
-distributed for each
B\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}}
, and the
random variables
\left\lvert\zeta\cap B_{1}\right\rvert,\ldots,\left\lvert\zeta\cap B_{m}\right\rvert
are independent
whenever
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}}
are pairwise disjoint. In this paper we will be considering a PPP
\eta
in
\mathbb{X}
with intensity measure
\lambda\nu
, where
\nu={\rm Leb}\otimes\mathcal{P}
and
\lambda>0
. We write
\eta=\left\{X_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
, where
X_{i}=\pi_{i}\left(\eta\right)
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

We now construct the RCM as a (non-Poissionian) point process on the space
\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times\left[0,1\right]
. We denote by
\mathbb{X}^{[2]}
the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{X}
containing precisely two elements, and each
e\in\mathbb{X}^{[2]}
is then a potential edge of the RCM. Also let
\left\{U_{i,j}\right\}_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a double sequence of independent identically distributed random variables uniformly distributed on
\left[0,1\right]
, such that the sequence is independent of the PPP
\eta
. We define

\xi=\left\{\left(\left\{X_{i},X_{j}\right\},U_{i,j}\right):1\leq i\leq j\right\},

(3.2)

where we recall
X_{i}=\pi_{i}(\eta)
. Thus
\xi
is a random point process on
\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times\left[0,1\right]
, which we interpret as a random marked graph and say that
\xi
is an
independent edge-marking
of
\eta
.

The independent edge-marking
\xi
of
\eta
can then be related by a deterministic functional,
\Gamma_{\varphi}
, to a random graph on
\mathbb{X}
by defining its vertex and edge sets:

V(\Gamma_{\varphi}(\xi))=\eta=\{X_{i}:i\in\mathbb{N}\},\qquad E(\Gamma_{\varphi}(\xi))=\{\{X_{i},X_{j}\}:U_{i,j}\leq\varphi(X_{i},X_{j}),1\leq i\leq j\}.

(3.3)

Since the sequence
U_{i,j}
is independent of the Poisson process
\eta
, the ordering in (
3.1
) does not change the distribution of the resulting random graph.

This interpretation of the RCM also allows for the introduction of additional points. This will be required many times in this paper, not least in our definition of the two-point function itself. For additional points
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{X}
, the point process
\eta
is augmented to make

\eta^{x_{1}}:=\eta\cup\{x_{1}\},\quad\eta^{x_{1},x_{2}}:=\eta\cup\{x_{1},x_{2}\}.

(3.4)

To extend these point processes on
\mathbb{X}
to a RCM, we extend the sequence
\left\{U_{m,n}\right\}_{m,n\geq 1}
to a sequence
\left\{U_{m,n}\right\}_{m,n\geq-1}
of independent random variables uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
, independent of the Poisson process
\eta
. We then define
\xi^{x_{1},x_{2}}
on
\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times[0,1]
as

\xi^{x_{1},x_{2}}:=\{(\{X_{i},X_{j}\},U_{i,j}):-1\leq i\leq j\},

(3.5)

where
X_{0}=x_{1}
and
X_{-1}=x_{2}
. It is straightforward to define
\xi^{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}}
for arbitrary
m\geq 3
by the same idea, and to define
\xi^{x_{1}}
by removing all (marked) edges incident to
x_{2}
from
\xi^{x_{1},x_{2}}
.
We assume implicitly that the augmented points are properly coupled such that, e.g.,
\xi^{x_{1}}
obtained in this way from
\xi^{x_{1},x_{2}}
(by removing all edges adjacent to
x_{2}
) is the same as if obtained from
\xi^{x_{1},x_{3}}
(by removing all edges adjacent to
x_{3}
); see
[
HHLM22
, Section 3.2]
for further details.

3.2
Probabilistic Lemmas

We introduce here a number of important lemmas that are standard in point process theory or percolation theory.

3.2.1
The Mecke Equation

An important part of our analysis will be a version of the Mecke equation - for a discussion see
[
LP18
, Chapter 4]
. This result allows us to make sums over the random sum of points in
\eta
more tractable. Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
and a measurable function
f\colon\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times[0,1]\right)\times\mathbb{X}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, the Mecke equation for
\xi
states that

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\sum_{\vec{x}\in\eta^{(m)}}f(\xi,\vec{x})\right]=\lambda^{m}\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f\left(\xi^{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}},\vec{x}\right)\right]\nu^{\otimes m}\left(\mathrm{d}\vec{x}\right),

(3.6)

where
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
,
\eta^{(m)}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}):x_{i}\in\eta,x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ for }i\neq j\}
, and
\nu^{\otimes m}
is the
m
-product measure of
\nu
on
\mathbb{X}^{m}
. We will only need (
3.6
) for
m\leq 3
, and largely only use it for
m=1
.

3.2.2
The Margulis-Russo Formula

An often used and very important tool in (discrete) percolation theory, the Margulis-Russo formula will be widely used in our analysis as well. The version we use follows from a slight adjustment to the more general result given in
[
LZ17
, Theorem 3.2]
. Writing
\mathbf{N}:=\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times[0,1]\right)
, let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{X}
be
\nu
-finite,
\zeta\in\mathbf{N}
, and define

\zeta_{\Lambda}:=\{(\{x,y\},u)\in\zeta:\{x,y\}\subseteq\Lambda\}.

(3.7)

We call
\zeta_{\Lambda}
the restriction of
\zeta
to
\Lambda
. We say that
f\colon\mathbf{N}\to\mathbb{R}

lives
on
\Lambda
if
f(\zeta)=f(\zeta_{\Lambda})
for every
\zeta\in\mathbf{N}
. Assume that there exists a
\nu
-finite set
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{X}
such that
f
lives on
\Lambda
. Moreover, assume that there exists
\lambda_{0}>0
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{0}}\left[\left\lvert f(\xi)\right\rvert\right]<\infty
. Then the Margulis-Russo formula states that, for all
\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,

\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}[f(\xi)]=\int_{\Lambda}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f(\xi^{x})-f(\xi)\right]\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right).

(3.8)

The result in
[
LZ17
]
requires that
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and that
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact, but
[
Las14
, Theorem 3.1]
allows us to make the necessary adjustments to permit
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
.

3.2.3
The BK Inequality

The idea of the BK inequality is that we acquire a simple upper bound for the probability that two increasing events happen, and that they do so on disjoint subsets of the space. For example, we may want to bound the probability that there exist two disjoint paths (up to the start and end points) in
\xi
between two given points. The BK inequality we present here is slightly more general than the usual BK inequality in that the point process
\xi
is augmented with independent random variables. It differs only slightly from the version appearing in
[
HHLM22
]
.

For notational clarity, we write
\mathbf{N}:=\mathbf{N}(\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times[0,1])
and denote the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbf{N}
by
\mathcal{N}
. We call a set
E\subset\mathbf{N}

increasing
if
\mu\in E
implies
\nu\in E
for each
\nu\in\mathbf{N}
with
\mu\subseteq\nu
. Let
(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})
,
(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2})
be two measurable spaces. We say that a set
E_{i}\subset\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{Y}_{i}
is
increasing
if
\mathbb{E}^{z}_{i}:=\{\mu\in\mathbf{N}:(\mu,z)\in E_{i}\}
is increasing for each
z\in\mathbb{Y}_{i}
.

For Borel
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
and
\mu\in\mathbf{N}
, we define
\mu_{\Lambda}
, the restriction of
\mu
to all edges completely contained in
\Lambda\times\mathcal{E}
, analogously to (
3.7
). Also let
\mathcal{R}
denote the ring of all finite unions of half-open
d
-dimensional rectangles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with rational coordinates. Then for increasing
E_{i}\in\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, we define

E_{1}\circ E_{2}:=\left\{(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{Y}_{1}\times\mathbb{Y}_{2}:\exists K_{1},K_{2}\in\mathcal{R}\text{ s.t.\penalty\ }\right.\\
\left.K_{1}\cap K_{2}=\varnothing,(\mu_{K_{1}},z_{1})\in E_{1},(\mu_{K_{2}},z_{2})\in E_{2}\right\}.

(3.9)

A set
E_{i}\in\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{i}

lives on

\Lambda
if
\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}(\mu,z)=\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}(\mu_{\Lambda},z)
for each
(\mu,z)\in\mathbf{N}\times\mathbb{Y}_{i}
. We consider random elements
W_{1},W_{2}
of
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
and
\mathbb{Y}_{2}
, respectively, and assume that
\xi
,
W_{1}
, and
W_{2}
are independent.

Theorem 3.1

(BK inequality)
.

Let
E_{1}\in\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{1}
and
E_{2}\in\mathcal{N}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{2}
be increasing events that live on
\Lambda\times\mathcal{E}
for some bounded set
\Lambda\in\mathcal{B}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
. Then

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}((\xi,W_{1},W_{2})\in E_{1}\circ E_{2})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}((\xi,W_{1})\in E_{1})\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}((\xi,W_{2})\in E_{2}).

(3.10)

A proof for this theorem can be found in
[
HHLM22
]
. The presence of the marked space
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
rather than just
\mathbb{R}^{d}
makes no difference to the validity of their proof.
It is worth noting that the operation
\circ
is commutative and associative, which allows repeated application of the inequality.

3.2.4
The FKG Inequality

In contrast to the BK inequality, the FKG inequality gives us a simple lower bound on the probability of two increasing events occurring.

Given two increasing and integrable functions
f,g
on
\mathbf{N}\left(\mathbb{X}^{[2]}\times[0,1]\right)
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f(\xi)g(\xi)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}[f(\xi)g(\xi)\mid\eta]\right]\geq\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f(\xi)\mid\eta\right]\;\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[g(\xi)\mid\eta\right]\right]\geq\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f(\xi)\right]\;\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[g(\xi)\right].

(3.11)

The first inequality was obtained by applying FKG to the random graph conditioned to have
\eta
as its vertex set, the second inequality by applying FKG for point processes (see
[
LP18
]
for details).

3.3
Linear Operator Lemmas

We shall formulate our problem as one of linear operators acting on Hilbert spaces. We will want to define analogous objects on the full marked space
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
and on the mark space
\mathcal{E}
itself. Let
\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)\in\left\{\left(\mathbb{X},\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}},\nu\right),\left(\mathcal{E},\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{P}\right)\right\}
, and
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\in\left\{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right),L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\right\}
correspondingly.

Given the measure space
\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)
, we consider the Banach space of square-integrable functions
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})=L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)
. We augment this with the inner product defined by

\langle f,g\rangle:=\int\overline{f\left(x\right)}g\left(x\right)\mu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right),

(3.12)

for all
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, where
\overline{f(x)}
is the complex conjugate of
f(x)
. With this inner product,
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
is a Hilbert space.

The measure space
\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)
is separable if there exists a countable family
\left\{E_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}}
such that given
E\in\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mu(E)<\infty
there exists an
E
-dependent subsequence
\{n_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that

\mu\left(E\Delta E_{n_{k}}\right)\to 0,\qquad\text{ as }k\to\infty,

(3.13)

where
\Delta
here is the symmetric difference. A well-known example of a separable measure space is
\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathfrak{B},{\rm Leb}\right)
- where
\mathfrak{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra - and therefore if the probability measure space
\left(\mathcal{E},\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{P}\right)
is separable then so is the product measure space
\left(\mathbb{X},\Sigma_{\mathbb{X}},\nu\right)
. A topological space is called separable if there exists a countable dense subset. If the measure space
\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)
is separable, then the function spaces
L^{p}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)=L^{p}\left(\mathcal{X},\Sigma_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\right)
with
p\in\left[1,\infty\right)
are all separable (see, for example,
[
SS11
]
). Since we have assumed that
\left(\mathcal{E},\Sigma_{\mathcal{E}},\mathcal{P}\right)
is separable, we know that both
L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
are separable Hilbert spaces.

The inner product for
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
allows us to define the
L^{2}
-norm for
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
:

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}=\langle f,f\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(3.14)

This allows us to define a norm on the space of bounded linear operators on
L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. Suppose we have a linear operator
H\colon\mathcal{D}\left(H\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
for some domain
\mathcal{D}\left(H\right)\subset L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
. Then we can define the
operator norm
by

\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}:=\begin{cases}\sup_{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X}):f\neq 0}\frac{\left\lVert Hf\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}}&\text{if }\mathcal{D}\left(H\right)=L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right),\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(3.15)

and
H
is bounded if
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}<\infty
. The space of bounded linear operators on a Banach space is itself a Banach space when augmented with this operator norm. Since
H
is linear, the supremum can equivalently be taken over
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
such that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}=1
.

If
H
is defined as the integral operator with kernel function
h\colon\mathcal{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, then we can take

\mathcal{D}\left(H\right)=\left\{f\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\colon\esssup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left\lvert h\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\mu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)<\infty\right\},

(3.16)

since this ensures that
(Hf)(x)
is defined for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
by dominated convergence.

Note that
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\rm op}
is sub-multiplicative, in the sense that for linear operators
H\colon\mathcal{D}(H)\to\mathcal{D}(G)
and
G\colon\mathcal{D}(G)\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
we have

\left\lVert GH\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(3.17)

Given a linear operator
H\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
on Hilbert spaces, the
adjoint
of
H
is the operator
H^{\dagger}\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
such that

\langle f,Hg\rangle=\langle H^{\dagger}f,g\rangle

(3.18)

for all
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. An operator is
self-adjoint
if
H=H^{\dagger}
. It is worth noting that a bounded integral operator
H
with kernel function
h
is self-adjoint if and only if
h(x,y)=\overline{h(y,x)}
for
\mu
-a.e.
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. If
h
is real valued, this is equivalent to
h
being symmetric on such a set of
x,y
. A simple calculation shows that if
H
is bounded and self-adjoint, then
\langle f,Hf\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
. This allows us to define the following object. Given a bounded and self-adjoint linear
H\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{X})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
, define

\mathbb{S}\left(H\right):=\sup_{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X}):f\neq 0}\frac{\langle f,Hf\rangle}{\langle f,f\rangle}.

(3.19)

Note that, in contrast to
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\rm op}
, the spectral supremum
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
is not a norm - it is possible for it to be negative.

The objects
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}
and
\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)
can also be understood via the spectrum of
H
. The spectrum, denoted
\sigma(H)
, is the set of
z\in\mathbb{C}
such that
z\mathds{1}-H
does not have a bounded linear inverse. For linear operators on finite dimensional vector spaces this coincides with the set of eigenvalues of
H
. If
H
is self-adjoint, then
\sigma(H)\subset\mathbb{R}
. We can then express
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}
and
\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)
in terms of the spectrum:

\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup\left\{\left\lvert z\right\rvert:z\in\sigma(H)\right\},\qquad\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)=\sup\left\{z:z\in\sigma(H)\right\}.

(3.20)

Note that for self-adjoint operators the operator norm is equal to the spectral radius of the operator.

While
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\rm op}
and
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
are very important properties of the operators we will be considering, they are not necessarily simple to compute for an integral operator given its kernel function. Recall that in (
2.3
)-(
2.5
) we defined the three integral operator norms
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2,\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
. These have the advantage that they are explicitly defined in terms of the kernel function. We now provide some results regarding the four norms (plus
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
), including relations between some of them.

Lemma 3.2

(Bounds on the Operator Norm)
.

Let
H\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
be a bounded self-adjoint integral operator. Then we can bound the operator norm:

\left\lvert\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(3.21)

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{E}
and
H
has kernel function
h
, then

\int h\left(a,b\right)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)\leq\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)

(3.22)

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
h,g\colon\mathcal{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
are kernel functions for the self-adjoint linear operators
H,G\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
, such that
h\left(x,y\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and
h\left(x,y\right)\geq\left\lvert g\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert
for
\mu
-a.e.
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
. Then

\mathbb{S}\left(H\right)\geq\mathbb{S}\left(G\right),\quad\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\rm op},\quad\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\geq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{1,\infty},\quad\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{2,\infty}\geq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{2,\infty},\quad\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\geq\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(3.23)

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
G,H
be bounded self-adjoint linear operators on a separable Hilbert space. Then

\left\lvert\mathbb{S}\left(G+H\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(G\right)\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(3.24)

The main advantage of the Hilbert space formulation for the operators is that it allows us to use the following theorem to better understand the operators. Although the “Multiplication Operator” version presented here may not provide as much information as the “Projection-Valued Measure” or “Direct Integral” versions (see
[
Hal13
]
), it has the advantage of being simpler to state and is sufficient for our purposes.

Theorem 3.5

(Spectral Theorem - Multiplication Operator Version)
.

Let
\left\{H_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
be a commutative family of bounded, self-adjoint operators on a separable Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
. Then there exists a
\sigma
-finite measure
space
\left(\Omega,\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu\right)
, a family of bounded, measurable, real-valued functions
\left\{h_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
on
\Omega
, and a unitary map
U\colon\mathbb{H}\to L^{2}\left(\Omega,\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu\right)
such that for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega
,

\left(UH_{i}U^{-1}f\right)\left(\omega\right)=h_{i}\left(\omega\right)f\left(\omega\right)

(3.25)

for all
f\in L^{2}\left(\Omega,\Sigma_{\Omega}.\mu\right)
and
i\in I
.

Proof.

For a proof in more generality, see
[
Fol16
, Theorem 1.47]
.
∎

In the context of a finite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
, the Spectral Theorem is effectively just the statement that Hermitian matrices can be diagonalized over
\mathbb{R}
. Here
U
is represented by a unitary matrix composed from the orthonormal eigenbasis of
\left\{H_{i}\right\}
, and the entries of the resulting diagonal matrix corresponding to
H_{i}
are the values of the function
h_{i}(\omega)
. For convolution operators on functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Fourier transform plays the role of
U
and turns the complicated convolution operation into simple pointwise multiplication.

We also present here a lemma that relates the operator norm and spectral supremum for operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
to operators on
L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
. Given a family
\left\{A_{k}\right\}_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
of subsets of
\mathbb{C}
, we define the
essential union
of these sets to be

{\rm ess.}\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}A_{k}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\forall\epsilon>0,{\rm Leb}\left(k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\exists x\in A_{k}\text{ s.t. }\left\lvert x-z\right\rvert<\epsilon\right)>0\right\}.

(3.26)

This can be understood as the union of
A_{k}
over
k
neglecting null sets of
k
.

Lemma 3.6
.

We have

\sigma\left(\varPhi\right)={\rm ess.}\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right),\qquad\sigma\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)={\rm ess.}\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right).

(3.27)

It then follows that

\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op},\qquad\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op},

(3.28)

\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi\right)=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right),\qquad\mathbb{S}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right).

(3.29)

4
The Expansion

There are two parts to this section. First we recount an expansion of the two-point function. This argument is essentially identical to the corresponding step in
[
HHLM22
]
, so we refer to this reference for most of the details. The novelty in this section is rather in the formulation of this expansion in terms of operators, contained in the second part.

4.1
Function-level expansion

The expansion of the two-point function
\tau_{\lambda}
proceeds in essentially the same way as in
[
HHLM22
]
. The similarity holds because the argument uses quite general properties of Poisson point processes and connection models - the change from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
adds no further complication. We give here a very brief overview of the derivation - the details can be found in that reference.

We first introduce some notation. It will be important to consider thinning events and pivotal points.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, and let
A\subset\mathbb{X}
be locally finite and of cardinality
\lvert A\rvert
.

(1)

Set

\bar{\varphi}(A,x):=\prod_{y\in A}(1-\varphi(x,y))

(4.1)

and define
\eta_{\langle A\rangle}
as a
\bar{\varphi}(A,\cdot)
-thinning of
\eta
(or simply
A
-thinning of
\eta
) as follows. We keep a point
w\in\eta
as a point of
\eta_{\langle A\rangle}
with probability
\bar{\varphi}(A,w)
independently of all other points of
\eta
. We similarly define
\eta_{\langle A\rangle}^{x}
as a
\bar{\varphi}(A,\cdot)
-thinning of
\eta^{x}
using the marks in
\xi^{x}
.

(2)

We write
x\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}y\textrm{ in }\xi
if both
\{x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi\}
and
\{x\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}y\textrm{ in }\xi[\eta_{\langle A\rangle}\cup\{x\}]\}
take place. In words,
\{x\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}y\textrm{ in }\xi\}
is the event that
x,y\in\eta
and
x
is connected to
y
in
\xi
, but that this connection is broken by an
A
-thinning of
\eta\setminus\{x\}
. In particular, the connection does not survive if
y
is thinned out.

(3)

We define

\tau_{\lambda}^{A}(x,y)=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}[\eta^{y}_{\langle A\rangle}\cup\{x\}]\right).

(4.2)

In words,
\tau_{\lambda}^{A}(x,y)
is the probability of the event that there exists an open path between
x
and
y
in an RCM driven by an
A
-thinning of
\eta^{y}
, where the point
x
is fixed to be present (but
y
is not).

(4)

Given
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
and edge-marking
\xi
, we say
u\in\mathbb{X}
is
pivotal
and
u\in\textsf{Piv}(y,x,\xi)
if
\left\{y\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}\right\}
and yet
\left\{y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}x\textrm{ in }\xi\left[\eta\setminus\left\{u\right\}\right]\right\}
. That is, every path on
\xi^{x,y}
connecting
x
and
y
uses the vertex
u
. Note that the end points
x
and
y
are never pivotal.

(5)

We will also use the set

E(x,y;A,\xi):=\{x\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}y\textrm{ in }\xi\}\cap\{\nexists w\in\textsf{Piv}(x,y;\xi):x\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}w\textrm{ in }\xi\},

(4.3)

for a locally finite set
A\subset\mathbb{X}
, and
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. If we consider the pivotal points of the
x
to
y
connection in
\xi
in sequence, then this is the event that an
A
-thinning breaks the connection after the last pivotal point, but not before.

Now we can state a continuum version of a standard lemma often used in discrete models - see, for example,
[
HS90
, Lemma 2.1]
. In bond percolation, it has the name “Cutting-bond lemma”. The proof of this continuum version can be found in
[
HHLM22
]
. The notation
x\Longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi
(for
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
and configuration
\xi
) denotes the event that
x,y\in\eta
and
x\sim y
, or that
x,y\in\eta
and that there are two paths in
\xi
that connect
x
and
y
and that these paths are disjoint in their interior vertices.

Lemma 4.2

(Cutting-point lemma)
.

Let
\lambda\geq 0
and let
v,u,x\in\mathbb{X}
with
u\neq x
and let
A\subset\mathbb{X}
be locally finite. Then

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\big[\mathds{1}_{E(v,u;A,\xi^{v,u,x})}\mathds{1}_{\{u\in\textsf{{Piv}}(v,x;\xi^{v,u,x})\}}\big]=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathds{1}_{E(v,u;A,\xi^{v,u})}\cdot\tau_{\lambda}^{\mathscr{C}(v,\xi^{v})}(x,u)\right].

(4.4)

Moreover,

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\Longleftrightarrow u\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u,x},u\in\textsf{{Piv}}(y,x;\xi^{y,u,x})\right)=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{y\Longleftrightarrow u\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u}\}}\cdot\tau_{\lambda}^{\mathscr{C}(y,\xi^{y})}(x,u)\right].

(4.5)

We now define the lace expansion coefficient function.

Definition 4.3

(Lace-expansion function coefficients)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, we define

\displaystyle\pi_{\lambda}^{(0)}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\Longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x}\right)-\varphi(x,y),

(4.6)

\displaystyle\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{n}\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\left\{y\Longleftrightarrow u_{0}\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u_{0}}_{0}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}E\left(u_{i-1},u_{i};\mathscr{C}_{i-1},\xi^{u_{i-1},u_{i}}_{i}\right)\right)\nu^{\otimes n}\left(\mathrm{d}\vec{u}_{[0,n-1]}\right),

(4.7)

where
u_{n}=x
and
\mathscr{C}_{i}=\mathscr{C}(u_{i-1},\xi^{u_{i-1}}_{i})
is the cluster of
u_{i-1}
in
\xi^{u_{i-1}}_{i}
. Further define the remainder functions

\displaystyle r_{\lambda,0}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\left\{y\Longleftrightarrow u_{0}\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u_{0}}_{0}\right\}\cap\left\{u_{0}\xleftrightarrow{\,\,\mathscr{C}_{0}\,\,}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u_{0},x}_{1}\right\}\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u_{0}\right),

(4.8)

\displaystyle r_{\lambda,n}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=(-\lambda)^{n+1}\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\left\{y\Longleftrightarrow u_{0}\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u_{0}}_{0}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}E\left(u_{i-1},u_{i};\mathscr{C}_{i-1},\xi^{u_{i-1},u_{i}}_{i}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\left.\cap\left\{u_{n}\xleftrightarrow{\,\,\mathscr{C}_{n}\,\,}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u_{n},x}_{n+1}\right\}\right)\nu^{\otimes\left(n+1\right)}\left(\mathrm{d}\vec{u}_{[0,n]}\right).

(4.9)

Additionally, define
\pi_{\lambda,n}
as the alternating partial sum

\pi_{\lambda,n}(x,y):=\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^{m}\pi_{\lambda}^{(m)}(x,y).

(4.10)

We will also be requiring Fourier transforms of these functions. Since the whole model is spatially translation invariant, so are the functions defined above. Therefore for each pair of marks
(a,b)
we perform the Fourier transform on the spatial displacement to get the functions
\widehat{\pi}^{(n)}_{\lambda}(k;a,b)
,
\widehat{\pi}_{\lambda,n}(k;a,b)
, and
\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}(k;a,b)
for
n\geq 0
.

Proposition 4.4

(Lace expansion: function-level)
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
and
n\geq 0
. Then for
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, and for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
,

\displaystyle\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi\left(x,y\right)+\pi_{\lambda,n}\left(x,y\right)+\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\left(\varphi+\pi_{\lambda,n}\right)\left(u,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)+r_{\lambda,n}\left(x,y\right),

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k;a,b\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)+\widehat{\pi}_{\lambda,n}\left(k;a,b\right)+\lambda\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k;a,c\right)\left(\widehat{\varphi}+\widehat{\pi}_{\lambda,n}\right)\left(k;c,b\right)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)+\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}\left(k;a,b\right).

(4.12)

We outline the proof of (
4.11
) here. The equation (
4.12
) then follows by applying the Fourier transform. The details of the proof of (
4.11
) - with appropriate contextual changes - can be found in
[
HHLM22
]
. Firstly, the definitions give

\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)=\varphi(x,y)+\pi_{\lambda}^{(0)}(x,y)+\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x},y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x}\right).

(4.13)

With an appropriate event partition, Mecke’s equation and the Cutting-point lemma allow us to re-write the probability term as

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x},y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\Longleftrightarrow}}}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x}\right)=\lambda\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{y\Longleftrightarrow u\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u}\}}\tau_{\lambda}^{\mathscr{C}_{0}}(x,u)\right]\mathrm{d}u

(4.14)

Now we use the identity

\tau_{\lambda}^{A}(x,u)=\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)-\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(u\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u,x}\right),

(4.15)

to extract the deterministic function
\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)
from the expectation at the cost of an extra term. The
\tau_{\lambda}
term then gives us the second
\pi^{(0)}_{\lambda}
term and the extra term becomes the remainder
r_{\lambda,0}
. Repeated use of (
4.15
) with Mecke’s equation and the Cutting-point lemma gives an alternating series that produces the various
\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}
and
r_{\lambda,n}
terms.

4.2
Operator-level expansion

Definition 4.5

(Lace-expansion operator coefficients)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\colon\mathcal{D}\left(\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as the linear operator with kernel function
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}
,
R_{\lambda,n}\colon\mathcal{D}\left(R_{\lambda,n}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as the linear operator with kernel function
r_{\lambda,n}
, and
\varPi_{\lambda,n}\colon\mathcal{D}\left(\varPi_{\lambda,n}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as the linear operator with kernel function
\pi_{\lambda,n}
. It follows that

\varPi_{\lambda,n}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}(-1)^{m}\varPi_{\lambda}^{(m)},

(4.16)

and
\mathcal{D}\left(\varPi_{\lambda,n}\right)\supset\bigcap_{m=0}^{n}\mathcal{D}\left(\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right)
.

We also use the kernel functions
\widehat{\pi}^{(n)}_{\lambda}(k;a,b)
,
\widehat{\pi}_{\lambda,n}(k;a,b)
, and
\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}(k;a,b)
to define the integral operators
\widehat{\varPi}^{(n)}_{\lambda}(k),\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda,n}(k),\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(k)
respectively for each
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition 4.6

(Lace expansion: operator-level)
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
. Then for
n\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda,n}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda,n}\right)+R_{\lambda,n},

(4.17)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\varPhi}+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda,n}\right)(k)+\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\left(\widehat{\varPhi}+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda,n}\right)(k)+\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(k).

(4.18)

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
be a test function. Then Proposition
4.4
gives for
x\in\mathbb{X}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}f\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)f\left(y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\varphi\left(x,y\right)+\pi_{\lambda,n}\left(x,y\right)+\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\left(\varphi+\pi_{\lambda,n}\right)\left(u,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)+r_{\lambda,n}\left(x,y\right)\right)f\left(y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda,n}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda,n}\right)+R_{\lambda,n}\right)f\left(x\right).

(4.19)

Note that in this last equality we used

\displaystyle\int\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\left(\varphi+\pi_{\lambda,n}\right)\left(u,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)f\left(y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)

\displaystyle=\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\int\left(\varphi+\pi_{\lambda,n}\right)\left(u,y\right)f\left(y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda,n}\right)f\left(x\right).

(4.20)

This exchange of integrals is valid by Fubini’s theorem because
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
ensures the integrals are finite.

The same argument applies for the Fourier transformed operators to complete the proof.
∎

5
Sub-Critical Convergence

In this section, we outline the lemmas and propositions that show that the operator expansion in Proposition
4.6
converges for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
. We present the lemmas for this argument here, but the proof for them are contained in Appendix
E
. The bounds are largely analogous to those proven in
[
HHLM22
]
, but the argument here makes much reduced use of Assumption
(.3)
. The proofs are also significantly different in parts as we don’t have the universal applicability of tools like translation symmetry and the Fourier transform (due to the introduction of marks).

5.1
Diagrammatic bounds

Our argument will require bounds on
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
,
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
,
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
,
\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
and
\left\lVert\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. However, it turns out that we can bound the last two remainder terms using others.

Lemma 5.1
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\lambda>0
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(5.2)

We first present the bounds for
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
,
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
, and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. The bounds and proofs for these with
n\geq 1
will be qualitatively different.

Let us introduce the notation
\tau_{\lambda,k}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)=\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)
and similarly
\varphi_{k}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)=\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)
for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}
and
\varPhi_{k}
then denote the integral operators constructed using these kernels. We can also define the Fourier transforms of these functions:
\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda,k}\left(l;a,b\right)
and
\widehat{\varphi}_{k}\left(l;a,b\right)
for
k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and use these as kernels to define the integral operators
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}(l)
and
\widehat{\varPhi}_{k}(l)
. In particular note that this results in

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k+l\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l\right),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\varPhi}_{k}\left(l\right)
