\displaystyle=\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k+l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right),

(5.4)

for
k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proposition 5.2

(Bounds for
n=0
)
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
. Then

\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(5.5)

For
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{2}\left(\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}+\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\right).

(5.7)

5.1.1
Diagrammatic Bounds for Lace Expansion Coefficients

Now we aim to bound
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
for
n\geq 1
. Lemmas
3.2
and
3.3
along with the positivity of
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)
prove that it is sufficient to bound
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
to get bounds on both of these. Much of the hard work for this has been done previously - for example in
[
HHLM22
]
. The novelty here is to see how the argument can be naturally written in terms of operators.

We inherit the following notation from
[
HHLM22
]
. Note that we make use of the Dirac delta function - specifically the one that holds with respect to the measure
\nu
. They are used here primarily for convenience and to make the argument more readable. In particular, they appear when applying the Mecke equation (
3.6
) to obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{y\in\eta^{u}}f(y,\xi^{u})\right]=\int\left(\lambda+\delta_{y,u}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[f\left(y,\xi^{u,y}\right)\right]\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right),

(5.8)

and so the factor
\lambda+\delta_{y,u}
encodes a case distinction of whether point
y
coincides with
u
or not.

Definition 5.3

(The
\psi
functions)
.

Let
r,s,u,w,x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. We first set
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(x,y):=\lambda^{-1}\delta_{x,y}+\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
. Also define

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(1)}(w,u,y)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(y,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,w)\tau_{\lambda}(w,y),

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(2)}(w,u,y)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\delta_{w,y}\tau_{\lambda}(y,u)\int\tau_{\lambda}(u,t)\tau_{\lambda}(t,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{(3)}(w,u,y)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\varphi(u,y)\left(\lambda^{-1}\delta_{w,y}\right),

\displaystyle\psi^{(1)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{4}\tau_{\lambda}(w,u)\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,s)\tau_{\lambda}(t,w)\tau_{\lambda}(u,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,t)\tau_{\lambda}(z,r)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\psi^{(2)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{4}\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(w,s)\int\tau_{\lambda}(t,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,t)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,w)\tau_{\lambda}(z,r)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\psi^{(3)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(u,w)\tau_{\lambda}(w,s)\tau_{\lambda}(u,r),

\displaystyle\psi^{(4)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(u,w)\left(\lambda^{-1}\delta_{w,s}\right)\tau_{\lambda}(u,r),

\displaystyle\psi_{n}^{(1)}(x,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{3}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,s)\tau_{\lambda}(z,r)\tau_{\lambda}(t,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,x)\tau_{\lambda}(x,t)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{n}^{(2)}(x,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda\tau_{\lambda}(x,s)\tau_{\lambda}(x,r),

and set
\psi_{0}:=\psi_{0}^{(1)}+\psi_{0}^{(2)}+\psi_{0}^{(3)}
,
\psi_{n}:=\psi_{n}^{(1)}+\psi_{n}^{(2)}
, and
\psi:=\psi^{(1)}+\psi^{(2)}+\psi^{(3)}+\psi^{(4)}
. Note that by using
\lambda^{-1}\delta_{\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)}
in
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
and in
\psi^{(3)}_{0}
, we ensure that each integral that will be truly performed (that is, not an integration over a Dirac delta function accounting for a coincidence of points) has a
\lambda
factor associated with it when the bound
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}
is calculated.

Diagrammatic representations of these functions can be found in Figure
1
. In these diagrams, the declared variables are represented by
vertices whereas the variables that are integrated over are represented by
vertices. If
\tau_{\lambda}
connects two variables then a standard edge
connects their vertices, if
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
connects two variables then an edge
\circ
connects their vertices, and if
\varphi
connects two variables then an edge
\sim
connects their vertices.

y
w
u

(a)

\psi^{(1)}_{0}

y=w
u

(b)

\psi^{(2)}_{0}

\sim
y=w
u

(c)

\psi^{(3)}_{0}

\circ
s
r
w
u

(d)

\psi^{(1)}

\circ
\circ
s
r
w
u

(e)

\psi^{(2)}

s
r
w
u

(f)

\psi^{(3)}

r
s=w
u

(g)

\psi^{(4)}

\circ
s
r
x

(h)

\psi^{(1)}_{n}

s
r
x

(i)

\psi^{(2)}_{n}

Figure 1
:
Diagrams of the
\psi_{0}
,
\psi
, and
\psi_{n}
functions.

Definition 5.4

(The
\Psi
operators)
.

Here we turn the functions of Definition
5.3
into linear operators, with the
\psi
functions acting as their kernels. For
j\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
, we define
\Psi^{(j)}_{0}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
as the linear operator acting on
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as

\Psi^{(j)}_{0}f\left(w,u\right)=\int\psi^{(j)}_{0}(w,u,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right).

(5.9)

For
j\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
, we define
\Psi^{(j)}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
as the linear operator acting on
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
as

\Psi^{(j)}f\left(w,u\right)=\int\psi^{(j)}(w,u,r,s)f(r,s)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}r,\mathrm{d}s\right).

(5.10)

For
j\in\left\{1,2\right\}
, we define
\Psi^{(j)}_{n}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as the linear operator acting on
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
as

\Psi^{(j)}_{n}f\left(x\right)=\int\psi^{(j)}_{n}(x,r,s)f(r,s)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}r,\mathrm{d}s\right).

(5.11)

We can then naturally define
\Psi_{0}\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{X}^{2})
,
\Psi\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{X}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{X}^{2})
, and
\Psi_{n}\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{X}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
as the sums of
\Psi^{(j)}_{0}
,
\Psi^{(j)}
, and
\Psi^{(j)}_{n}
respectively, or equivalently as those operators having kernel functions
\psi_{0}
,
\psi
, and
\psi_{n}
respectively.

Proposition 5.5

(Bound in terms of
\psi
and
\Psi
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, and
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
. Then

\lambda\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}(x,y)\leq\int\psi_{n}(x,w_{n-1},u_{n-1})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\psi(\vec{v}_{i})\right)\psi_{0}(w_{0},u_{0},y)\nu^{\otimes\left(2n\right)}\left(\mathrm{d}\left(\left(\vec{w},\vec{u}\right)_{[0,n-1]}\right)\right),

(5.12)

where
\vec{v}_{i}=(w_{i},u_{i},w_{i-1},u_{i-1})
. In operator terms, this means

\lambda\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\Psi_{n}\Psi^{n-1}\Psi_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(5.13)

Proof.

The argument for (
5.12
) is essentially identical to
[
HHLM22
, Proposition 4.7]
. It uses only general properties of Poisson point process and connection models - for example thinning edges and Mecke’s equation.
The inequality (
5.13
) then follows from the clear sequential structure of (
5.12
).
∎

The inequality (
5.13
) suggests that we will bound the lace coefficient operators with some product of
\left\lVert\Psi_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\Psi_{n}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
terms. This is
nearly
correct. Unfortunately the norms
\left\lVert\Psi_{n}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
and
\left\lVert\Psi_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
will not have the decay in
d
that we will require. We will have to consider the norms of
pairs
of operators.

Definition 5.6
.

Define

\displaystyle\triangle_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(r,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,v\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(v,s\right)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right)=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},

\displaystyle\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(r,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,v\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(v,s\right)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right)=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},

\displaystyle\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(r,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(u,v\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(v,s\right)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right)=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+1,

\displaystyle\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(\overline{u}-\overline{v};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{v};a_{5},a_{6}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{v}.

We can think of
\triangle_{\lambda}
as an
\mathbb{X}
-convolution of three
\tau_{\lambda}
functions with suprema taken over the end vertices. Then
\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
and
\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}
are produced by adding on the
\mathbb{X}
-convolution of two
\tau_{\lambda}
functions and one
\tau_{\lambda}
function (which will trivially take value
1
once the suprema are taken). The object
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
differs from
\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}
in that now the marks at adjacent
\tau_{\lambda}
functions need not be equal, and then we take the supremum over all the marks - not just the end ones. We then use these elementary diagrams to produce composite objects:

U_{\lambda}:=\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}+\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}^{2}+\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty},\qquad V_{\lambda}:=\left(\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}U_{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(5.14)

We note a few relations. Firstly, since all the terms in
U_{\lambda}
are non-negative we have
\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\leq U_{\lambda}
and thus
V_{\lambda}\leq U_{\lambda}
. Also note that
1\leq\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
and thus
U_{\lambda}\geq\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
. In particular this means that
U_{\lambda}\geq 1
and
U_{\lambda}\geq\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
.

The following lemma tells us that we can use
U_{\lambda}
to bound single operators, and that we can use
V_{\lambda}^{2}
to bound pairs of operators. To aid the reader’s understanding, bear in mind that later (in Section
5.2
) we will prove that
U_{\lambda}=\mathcal{O}\left(1\right)
, whilst
V_{\lambda}
is much smaller and is
\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For all
j_{0}\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
,
j,j^{\prime}\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
, and
j_{n}\in\left\{1,2\right\}
,

\displaystyle U_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\vee\left\lVert\Psi^{(j)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\vee\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\right\rVert_{1,\infty},

(5.15)

\displaystyle V_{\lambda}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lVert\Psi^{(j)}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\vee\left\lVert\Psi^{(j)}\Psi^{(j^{\prime})}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\vee\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\vee\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(5.16)

These bounds with Proposition
5.5
lead to the following proposition.

Proposition 5.8
.

For
n\geq 1
,

\lambda\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq 6\times 4^{n-1}U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n}.

(5.17)

5.1.2
Diagrammatic Bounds for Displaced Lace Expansion Coefficients

Now let us aim to bound
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
for
n\geq 1
. Our strategy draws inspiration from the corresponding step in
[
HHLM22
]
, whilst being adapted to account for inhomogeneous marks.

The central idea is to bound

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right),

(5.18)

and bound
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}
using the function expression from Proposition
5.5
. This produces an integral (or diagram) composed from simpler segments with the displacement factor,
\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)
, spanning the whole length of the diagram. The following Cosine-Splitting result allows us to get a sum of diagrams where the displacement factor only spans a single segment of each diagram.

Lemma 5.9

(Split of cosines,
[
FH17a
, Lemma 2.13]
)
.

Let
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
and
t=\sum_{i=1}^{m}t_{i}
. Then

1-\cos(t)\leq m\sum_{i=1}^{m}[1-\cos(t_{i})].

To see this strategy in practice, consider the diagram corresponding to
\Psi^{(2)}_{n}\Psi^{(1)}\Psi^{(1)}_{0}
with the displacement factor:

(5.19)

In these diagrams and hereafter we use
to denote that a displacement factor is connecting the two indicated vertices. As above, the path the
takes will also suggest the route along which we will intend to use cosine-splitting. For the sake of simplicity, we will be taking the displacement path across the ‘top’ of the diagram from the perspective of the usual orientation of the
\Psi_{0}
segment. However - as can be seen in the above example - the way the operators compose with each other means that the orientation of each segment inverts when compared to its neighbours. Therefore the displacement may be across the ‘top’ or the ‘bottom’ of the
\Psi
and
\Psi_{n}
segments (in their usual orientation) depending upon the parity of its place in the sequence of segments.

Our general strategy will be to isolate the displaced segment (and perhaps a neighbouring segment) by splitting off ‘earlier’ and ‘later’ segments. When we split off earlier segments (and sometimes later segments), we will use the
\Psi
structure we have been using so far. However, we will sometimes want to group vertices and edges slightly differently when we split off ‘later’ segments. We use an observation from
[
HHLM22
]
to conveniently group these. Let us define the kernel functions

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(1)}_{0}\left(w,u,x\right)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(y,r)\tau_{\lambda}(r,s)\tau_{\lambda}(s,x),

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(2)}_{0}\left(w,u,x\right)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\left(\lambda^{-1}\delta_{y,r}\right)\left(\lambda^{-1}\delta_{y,s}\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(1)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{4}\tau_{\lambda}(w,u)\int\tau_{\lambda}(t,s)\tau_{\lambda}(t,w)\tau_{\lambda}(u,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,t)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(z,r)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(2)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{4}\tau_{\lambda}(w,s)\int\tau_{\lambda}(t,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,t)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,w)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(z,r)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right),

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(3)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(u,w)\tau_{\lambda}(w,s)\tau_{\lambda}(u,r),

\displaystyle\overline{\psi}^{(4)}(w,u,r,s)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\tau_{\lambda}(u,w)\tau_{\lambda}(w,s)\left(\lambda^{-1}\delta_{u,r}\right),

and set
\overline{\psi}_{0}:=\overline{\psi}^{(1)}_{0}+\overline{\psi}^{(2)}_{0}
and
\overline{\psi}:=\overline{\psi}^{(1)}+\overline{\psi}^{(2)}+\overline{\psi}^{(3)}+\overline{\psi}^{(4)}
. Diagrammatic representations of these functions can be found in Figure
2
. Note the similarity to the
\psi
functions, with the
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge appearing elsewhere in the integral.

x
w
u

(a)

\overline{\psi}^{(1)}_{0}

x=w=u

(b)

\overline{\psi}^{(2)}_{0}

\circ
s
r
w
u

(c)

\overline{\psi}^{(1)}

\circ
\circ
s
r
w
u

(d)

\overline{\psi}^{(2)}

s
r
w
u

(e)

\overline{\psi}^{(3)}

s
w
r=u

(f)

\overline{\psi}^{(4)}

Figure 2
:
Diagrams of the
\overline{\psi}_{0}
, and
\overline{\psi}
functions.

For
j\in\left\{1,2\right\}
we define
\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}_{0}\colon\mathcal{D}\left(\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}_{0}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
to be the linear operators acting on
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
as

\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}_{0}f\left(w,u\right)=\int\overline{\psi}^{(j)}_{0}(w,u,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right),

(5.20)

and for
j\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
we define
\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}\colon\mathcal{D}\left(\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
to be the linear operators acting on
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}^{2}\right)
as

\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}f\left(w,u\right)=\int\overline{\psi}^{(j)}(w,u,r,s)f(r,s)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}r,\mathrm{d}s\right).

(5.21)

In our terminology, the important observation of
[
HHLM22
]
was that for some
m\geq 0
the ‘later’ segments of each diagram can be bounded using
\big(\overline{\Psi}^{m}\overline{\Psi}_{0}\big)^{\dagger}
, the adjoint of
\overline{\Psi}^{m}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
. In terms of the kernel functions, taking the adjoint in this case amounts to reflecting the ‘input’ and ‘output’ arguments, and for the diagrams this amounts to reflecting in the vertical plane.

Lemma 5.10
.

For
n\geq 1
,
m\in\left\{0,\ldots,n\right\}
,
j_{0}\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
,
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-1}\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
, and
j_{n}\in\left\{1,2\right\}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{m})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{m},

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{m})}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-m},

(5.23)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{m})}\ldots\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{1})}\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}U_{\lambda}&:m=0\\
U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{m-1}&:m\geq 1.\end{cases}

(5.24)

Definition 5.11
.

In addition to the terms defined in Definition
5.6
, we will also use

\displaystyle W_{k}

\displaystyle:=\lambda\esssup_{x,y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda,k}(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)=\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},

\displaystyle\overline{W_{k}}

\displaystyle:=\lambda\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda,k}(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{u};a_{3},a_{4})\mathrm{d}u,

\displaystyle\overline{H_{k}}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{5}\esssup_{\overline{x}_{1},\overline{x}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{16}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{s}-\overline{x}_{1};a_{1},a_{2})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{u};a_{3},a_{4})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{v}-\overline{s};a_{5},a_{6})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{v}+\overline{x}_{2}-\overline{t};a_{7},a_{8})

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{s}-\overline{w};a_{9},a_{10})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{w}-\overline{u};a_{11},a_{12})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{t}-\overline{w};a_{13},a_{14})\tau_{\lambda,k}(\overline{t}-\overline{u};a_{15},a_{16})\mathrm{d}\overline{t}\mathrm{d}\overline{w}\mathrm{d}\overline{z}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}.

Note that we only use
\overline{W_{k}}
in Propositions
5.13
and
5.12
because
\overline{W_{k}}\geq W_{k}
, but in the proof we will sometimes use
W_{k}
to make the derivation easier to follow. The diagrams for
\overline{W_{k}}
and
\overline{H_{k}}
are in Figure
3
.

Also recall the sets
\left\{B\left(x\right)\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{X}}
assumed to exist in Assumption
(.3)
. Then define

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle:=\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\nu\left(B\left(x\right)\right),

\displaystyle\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{2}\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}:r\notin{B\left(s\right)}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}\left(r,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,v\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(v,s\right)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right).

Note that these are only required to deal with one specific diagram in the following
n=1
case.

(a)

\overline{W_{k}}

(b)

\overline{H_{k}}

Figure 3
:
Diagrams representing
\overline{W_{k}}
and
\overline{H_{k}}
. In this figure, the marks on edges incident to filled square vertices need not be equal.

We already have a bound for the
n=0
version of the displaced operators from Proposition
5.2
. The following proposition provides the bound for the
n=1
version.

Proposition 5.12
.

For
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(1)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(1)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 13\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}U_{\lambda}+\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\left(V_{\lambda}+\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}\right)+\lambda^{3}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+4\lambda\mathbb{B}\overline{W_{k}}.

(5.25)

The following proposition then provides the bounds for the
n\geq 2
versions, for which the proof will be qualitatively different.

Proposition 5.13
.

For
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
n\geq 2
,

\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\begin{cases}\left(n+1\right)^{2}4^{n-1}\left(50\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}+6\overline{H_{k}}\right)U_{\lambda}^{4}V_{\lambda}^{n-4}&:n\geq 4\\
16\times 16\left(50\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+6\overline{H_{k}}\right)U_{\lambda}^{2}&:n=3\\
9\times 4\left(50\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}+6\overline{H_{k}}\right)U_{\lambda}^{2}&:n=2.\end{cases}

(5.26)

5.2
The Bootstrap Function

Now we have some bounds for the terms in our expansion, we will in turn bound these using a bootstrap function. To aid readability, in our following arguments we will assume that

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=1.

(5.27)

This does not reduce the generality of our result. First note that since
\widehat{\varphi}(0;a,b)>0
for a positive
\mathcal{P}
-measure set of
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
, we have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)>0
. Now suppose we scale
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by a factor of
q^{-1/d}
(that is, the new unit radius ball is the previous ball of radius
q^{1/d}
), and leave the mark space
\mathcal{E}
unchanged. Then we find that the system we are left with has the distribution of an RCM model with the new parameters

\lambda^{*}=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\eta\cap\left[0,q^{\frac{1}{d}}\right]^{d}\right\rvert\right]=\lambda q,\qquad\varphi^{*}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)=\varphi\left(q^{\frac{1}{d}}\overline{x};a,b\right).

(5.28)

Therefore for all
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
we have

\widehat{\varphi}^{*}(0;a,b)=\int\varphi^{*}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=q^{-1}\int\varphi^{*}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=q^{-1}\widehat{\varphi}(\overline{x};a,b),

(5.29)

and
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}^{*}(0)\right)=q^{-1}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
. We can therefore achieve our assumption by choosing
q=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
. Furthermore, under this scaling we find that the old event
\left\{\left(\overline{x},a\right)\longleftrightarrow\left(\overline{y},b\right)\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}\right\}
becomes the new event
\left\{\left(q^{-1/d}\overline{x},a\right)\longleftrightarrow\left(q^{-1/d}\overline{y},b\right)\textrm{ in }\xi^{\left(q^{-1/d}\overline{x},a\right),\left(q^{-1/d}\overline{y},b\right)}\right\}
, and we get

\tau^{*}_{\lambda^{*}}(\overline{x};a,b)=\tau_{\lambda}\left(q^{\frac{1}{d}}\overline{x};a,b\right)

(5.30)

where
\tau^{*}_{\lambda^{*}}
is the two-point function in the RCM governed by the connection function
\varphi^{*}
and intensity
\lambda^{*}
.

It is also worth noting that the scaling choice to have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=1
means that
(.1)
reduces to the condition that there exists a
d
-independent constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{2,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(5.31)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(5.32)

Note that the inequalities relating
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2,\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
follow from Jensen’s inequality and a supremum bound on a probability space.

Definition 5.14

(Bootstrap Function)
.

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
and
k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define

\displaystyle\mu_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=1-\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)},

(5.33)

\displaystyle\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)},

(5.34)

\displaystyle\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)

\displaystyle:=\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right)\left(\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l-k\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l\right)+\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l+k\right)+\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l-k\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l+k\right)\right).

(5.35)

From these we can then define the bootstrap function
f:=f_{1}\vee f_{2}\vee f_{3}
, where

f_{1}\left(\lambda\right):=\lambda,\qquad f_{2}\left(\lambda\right):=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)},\qquad f_{3}\left(\lambda\right):=\esssup_{k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}.

(5.36)

5.2.1
Bounding the Lace Expansion Coefficient Operator

We have managed to bound the lace expansion coefficient operator and its displacement in terms of a variety of objects. For the lace expansion coefficient operator itself (and its associated Fourier transformed operators), these are:

\lambda,\triangle_{\lambda},\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}},\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(5.37)

As a guide, we expect
\lambda
,
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
, and
\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
to be bounded uniformly in
d
and
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, whereas we expect
\triangle_{\lambda}
to decay uniformly in
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
as
d\to\infty
. For some, these properties are easy to prove. The boundedness of
\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
follows from

\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq C,

(5.38)

where we have used
(.1)
via (
5.31
). Regarding
\lambda
, since we are only interested in
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
the question is perhaps more clearly written as
“Is
\lambda_{O}
uniformly bounded in
d
?”
. While there may be more elementary ways of proving this directly, we will get it for free from the bootstrap argument. Since we have the bound
\lambda\leq f(\lambda)
, proving the uniform boundedness of
f(\lambda)
with respect to
d
and
\lambda
for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
will prove the same for
\lambda_{O}
.

This will leave us with only
\triangle_{\lambda}
and
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
. Recall that we can write

\triangle_{\lambda}=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},\quad\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+1.

(5.39)

Then
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
differs from
\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}
in that now the supremum is taken over all the marks, and adjacent
\tau_{\lambda}
can have different marks. To get the desired decay and boundedness behaviour, we will therefore only need to prove that
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
decays and to bound

\displaystyle\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}-\overline{v};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{v};a_{5},a_{6}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{v},

(5.40)

\displaystyle\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}.

(5.41)

We begin by proving the decay of
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
.

Let us define the function
\overline{\epsilon}\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where

\overline{\epsilon}(d):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d}+\frac{\log d}{\log g(d)}&:\lim_{d\to\infty}g(d)\rho^{-d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{2}=0\qquad\forall\rho>0,\\
0&:\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.42)

Recall
g(d)
is the function defined in
(.3)
. In particular,
\overline{\epsilon}(d)=0
unless
g(d)
approaches zero particularly quickly, and
\beta(d)=g(d)^{\frac{1}{4}-\frac{3}{2}\overline{\epsilon}(d)}
.

Proposition 5.15

(Bound for the
n
-gon diagrams)
.

Let
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
. Then for each
n
such that
d>2n
, there exists finite
c_{f}=c(n,f(\lambda))
(where
x\mapsto c(n,x)
is increasing and independent of
d
) such that for
d>6

\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{n}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\begin{cases}c_{f}&:n=1,2,\\
c_{f}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}-n\overline{\epsilon}(d)}&:n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(5.43)

The following is not a direct corollary of the previous proposition, because
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
includes suprema over the intermediate marks. Nevertheless, the proof in Appendix
F
follows a similar strategy.

Lemma 5.16
.

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, there exists
c_{f}=c\left(f\left(\lambda\right)\right)
(where
x\mapsto c\left(x\right)
is increasing and independent of
d
) such that for
d>6
,

\displaystyle\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}-\overline{v};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{v};a_{5},a_{6}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{v}\leq c_{f},

(5.44)

\displaystyle\esssup_{\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a_{1},a_{2}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u};a_{3},a_{4}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\leq c_{f},

(5.45)

and therefore

\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\leq 1+2c_{f}.

(5.46)

5.2.2
Bounding the Lace Expansion Coefficient Operator Displacement

We now consider the objects required to bound the displacement of the lace expansion coefficient operator. In addition to the objects used to bound the lace expansion coefficient operator itself, we require bounds on

\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty},\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty},\overline{W_{k}},\overline{H_{k}},\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda},\mathbb{B}.

(5.47)

The decay
\mathbb{B}\leq g(d)
follows from Assumption
(.3)
, and the following Observation
5.17
demonstrates the decay of
\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}
.

Observation 5.17
.

In proving Proposition
5.15
(in Appendix
F
), it is proven that for all
\lambda\geq 0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{X}

\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)\leq\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\varphi(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(5.48)

This inequality allows us to bound
\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}
in terms of other terms. By using the inequality twice and appropriately bounding
\varphi
with
\tau_{\lambda}
, we get

\int\tau_{\lambda}(r,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,s)\nu(\mathrm{d}u)\leq\int\varphi(r,u)\varphi(u,s)\nu(\mathrm{d}u)+2\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}(r,v)\tau_{\lambda}(v,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,s)\nu^{\otimes 2}(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v)

(5.49)

for any
r,s\in\mathbb{X}
. Therefore

\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}\leq 3\triangle_{\lambda}+\lambda\esssup_{r\not\in B(s)}\int\varphi(r,u)\varphi(u,s)\nu(\mathrm{d}u)\leq 3\triangle_{\lambda}+\lambda g(d),

(5.50)

where we have used
(.3)
. Therefore Proposition
5.15
implies that for
d>6
there exists
c_{f}
such that

\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}\leq c_{f}\beta^{2}.

(5.51)

We are then left with
\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
,
\overline{W_{k}}
, and
\overline{H_{k}}
, and we will deal them in this order. First note that

\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\varphi_{k}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)=\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right))\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}a)\\
=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}_{k}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(5.52)

The following lemma therefore also allows us to bound
\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. Its extra generality will be required later.

Lemma 5.18
.

For all
k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq C\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right],

(5.53)

where
C>0
is the
d
-independent constant given in
(.1)
.

We now address
\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
.

Lemma 5.19
.

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, there exists
c_{f}=c\left(f\left(\lambda\right)\right)
(where
x\mapsto c\left(x\right)
is increasing and independent of
d
) such that for
d>6
,

\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq c_{f}\beta^{2}\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(5.54)

The following lemma allows us to deal with occurrences of
\overline{W_{k}}
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. Then

\tau_{\lambda,k}(x,y)\leq\varphi_{k}(x,y)+2\lambda\int\left(\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\varphi_{k}(u,y)+\tau_{\lambda,k}(x,u)\varphi(u,y)\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(5.55)

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, there exists
c_{f}=c\left(f\left(\lambda\right)\right)
(where
x\mapsto c\left(x\right)
is increasing and independent of
d
) such that for
d>6
,

\overline{W_{k}}\leq c_{f}\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(5.56)

We are now only left with bounding
\overline{H_{k}}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, there exists
c_{f}=c\left(f\left(\lambda\right)\right)
(where
x\mapsto c\left(x\right)
is increasing and independent of
d
) such that for
d>6
,

\overline{H_{k}}\leq c_{f}\beta^{2}\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(5.57)

Now we can bring together these bounds to show that the finite lace expansion of Proposition
4.6
converges to an Ornstein-Zernike equation (OZE) for both the raw operators on
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
and the Fourier-transformed operators on
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
. Proposition
5.22
does this for
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
, with the caveat that there is a constant
c_{f}
that is increasing in the bootstrap function
f
(but independent of
d
) appearing in the bounds.

Proposition 5.22

(Convergence of the operator lace expansion and OZE)
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
and
d>6
be sufficiently large. Then there exists
c_{f}=c(f(\lambda))
(which is increasing in
f
and independent of
d
) such that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{f}\beta,

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{n\geq 0}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{f}\beta,

(5.58)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq c_{f}\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)\beta,

(5.59)

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}c_{f}\beta^{n}.

(5.60)

Furthermore, the limit
\varPi_{\lambda}:=\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum^{M}_{n=0}\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}
exists and is a bounded operator with Fourier transform
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)=\lim_{M\to\infty}\sum^{M}_{n=0}\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)
for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The operators
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
satisfy operator Ornstein-Zernike equations, taking the form

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}=\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}),

(5.61)

and

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)=\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)+\lambda\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right),

(5.62)

for all
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

So far in this section we have provided bounds for the building blocks of the bounds appearing in Propositions
5.2
,
5.8
,
5.13
, and
5.12
. We still have to convert these into bounds for the composite terms
U_{\lambda}
and
V_{\lambda}
. From their definitions and the bounds on the building blocks, it is simple to see that there exists
c_{f}^{\prime}
such that for
d>6

U_{\lambda}\leq c_{f}^{\prime},\qquad V_{\lambda}\leq c_{f}^{\prime}\beta.

(5.63)

This then implies that there exists
c_{f}^{\prime\prime}
such that for
d>6

\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\begin{cases}c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta^{2}&:n=0,\\
c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\left(c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{n}&:n\geq 1.\end{cases}

(5.64)

If
d
is sufficiently large we have
c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta<1/2
(for example), and therefore there exists
c_{f}
such that

\sum_{n\geq 0}\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\left(\beta^{2}+\frac{c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta}{1-c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta}\right)\leq c_{f}\beta.

(5.65)

When we want to consider the displaced terms, we also make use of Observation
5.17
, and Lemmas
5.18
,
5.19
,
5.20
, and
5.21
to give us the existence of
c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that for
d>6

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\begin{cases}c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta^{2}\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)&:n=0,3,\\
c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)&:n=1,2,\\
\left(n+1\right)^{2}c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{n-3}\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)&:n\geq 4.\end{cases}

(5.66)

If
d
is sufficiently large we have
c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta<1/2
(for example), and therefore there exists
c_{f}
such that

\sum_{n\geq 0}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\left(2\beta^{2}+2\beta+\frac{16\left(c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{3}-39\left(c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{2}+25c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta}{\left(1-c_{f}^{\prime\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{3}}\right)\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)\\
\leq c_{f}\beta\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right).

(5.67)

Lemma
5.1
then allows us to use (
5.64
) to bound
\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. This immediately gives the required result for
n\geq 1
. For
n=0
, we simply additionally require
d
to be large enough that
\beta<1
and therefore
\beta^{2}<\beta^{0}
.

Note that the dual space of a Banach space (endowed with the operator norm) is also a Banach space. In particular, it is complete. Since
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\left(c_{f}^{\prime\prime}\beta\right)^{n}
for
n\geq 1
, the sequence
\varPi_{\lambda,n}
is a Cauchy sequence in the dual space of
L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
for sufficiently large
d
. Therefore the limit
\varPi_{\lambda}
exists and is a bounded linear operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
. Since the Fourier transform is a unitary linear operator it is bounded and continuous, and therefore the same argument says that the limit
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
exists and is indeed the Fourier transform of
\varPi_{\lambda}
.

Since
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
is finite for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, we have
\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
if
d
is sufficiently large. As a a consequence of this and equation (
4.17
) from Proposition
4.6
, the
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
operator satisfies the operator Ornstein-Zernike equation.
∎

The following lemma has two main uses in what follows. Firstly, it takes the OZEs from Proposition
5.22
and uses it to write the two-point operators in terms of the sum of the adjacency operators and the lace expansion coefficient operators. Secondly, it uses this and an intermediate value theorem argument to bound the spectral supremum of the sum of the adjacency operators and the lace expansion coefficient operators.

Lemma 5.23
.

Let
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
and
d>6
be sufficiently large. Then the operators
\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\right)
and
\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\right)
all have bounded linear inverses and

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\right)^{-1},

(5.68)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)

\displaystyle=\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\right)^{-1},\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(5.69)

Furthermore,
\lambda\mapsto\varPi_{\lambda}
and
\lambda\mapsto\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
are all continuous in the operator norm topology and

\displaystyle\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle<1,

(5.70)

\displaystyle\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)

\displaystyle<1,\qquad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(5.71)

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)
has a bounded linear inverse. This is clear for
\lambda=0
, so we only need to consider
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. Suppose for contradiction that
\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)
does not have a bounded linear inverse. Then there exists a sequence
f_{n}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
such that
\left\lVert f_{n}\right\rVert_{2}=1
and
\left\lVert\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\right)f_{n}\right\rVert_{2}\to 0
. This also means that
\lambda\left\lVert\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f_{n}\right\rVert\to 1
. Then from Lemma
3.6
and sub-criticality we have
\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}<\infty
, and therefore the OZE equation (
5.61
) implies that
\left\lVert\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f_{n}\right\rVert\to 0
, a contradiction. A similar argument proves the corresponding statement for the Fourier transformed operators.

Once we know that the inverses exist, it is only a matter of rearranging the OZE equations to get the expressions for
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
in terms of the other operators.

Since
\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}
is a bounded operator, a similar argument proves that
\mathds{1}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
has a bounded inverse, and therefore

\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}=\left(\mathds{1}+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}.

(5.72)

From Corollary
D.2
we have the continuity of
\lambda\mapsto\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
, and therefore the continuity
\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}
(and therefore
\varPi_{\lambda}
). We also have the continuity of
\lambda\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
from Corollary
D.2
, and we similarly get the continuity of
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
.

The continuity of
\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}
implies the continuity of
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)
(via Lemma
3.4
). At
\lambda=0
it is clear that
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)=0
, and our above argument implies that
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\neq 1
. Therefore an intermediate value theorem argument proves that
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)<1
. This argument also works for
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)
.
∎

6
Uniform Convergence

In Proposition
5.22
we proved that the lace expansion converges in the sub-critical regime. To show the convergence at criticality, we will need a stronger result. We will need uniform convergence.

Observation 6.1

(Uniform convergence of the lace expansion)
.

Suppose that there exists finite
C>0
such that
f\leq C
on
[0,\lambda_{O})
. Then for
d>6
sufficiently large there exits
c>0
(independent of
\lambda
and
d
) such that the bounds (
5.58
), (
5.59
), (
5.60
) hold with
c_{f}
replaced by
c
.

To show that
f
is indeed uniformly bounded for
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. We do this by performing a forbidden-region argument. In Proposition
6.2
we prove that
f(0)
is bounded and
f
is continuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. However we also prove that
f
is never in the region
\left(\kappa,\kappa+1\right]
, where

\kappa:=10C\left(1+C\right)^{2},

(6.1)

C
being the constant appearing in
(.1)
. Note that (
2.14
) in
(.1)
requires that
C\geq 1
. The intermediate value theorem then implies that
\kappa
acts as an upper bound on the whole domain
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. One should not read too much into the value of
\kappa
here. It is sufficient for our purposes, but not remotely optimal.

Proposition 6.2

(The forbidden-region argument)
.

The following three statements are all true:

1)

f
satisfies
f(0)\leq\kappa
.

2)

f(\lambda)\notin(\kappa,\kappa+1]
for all
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{O})
provided that
d
is sufficiently large.

3)

f
is continuous on
[0,\lambda_{O})
.

Therefore
f(\lambda)\leq\kappa
holds uniformly in
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
for all
d
sufficiently large.

Before we embark on our proof of Proposition
6.2
, we prove a lemma which will give us some properties of the components that went into the bootstrap function.

Lemma 6.3
.

The map
\lambda\mapsto\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)
is continuous and bounded below by
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. Therefore the map
\lambda\mapsto\mu_{\lambda}
is a continuous map from
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)\to\left[0,1\right)
. Furthermore,
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)
is bounded below by
\left(1+\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right)^{-1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

First note that it is proven in Appendix
D
that for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
the map
\lambda\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)
is differentiable with respect to the operator norm (Corollary
D.2
), and is therefore continuous. Lemma
3.4
then implies that
\lambda\mapsto\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)
is continuous. For the bounded below property, we use Lemma
3.3
to get
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\geq\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=1
.

The continuity of
\mu_{\lambda}
follows directly from the continuity and positivity of
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)
. The bound
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\geq 1
bounds
\mu_{\lambda}\geq 0
, and
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}<\infty
shows that
\mu_{\lambda}<1
for all
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. The case
\mu_{0}=0
follows immediately from
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\varPhi
.

Because
\mu_{\lambda}\in\left[0,1\right)
, to minimise
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)
we want to find a lower bound for
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)
. The
k
-uniform bound on
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)
then follows from
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\geq-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
.
∎

We now return to Proposition
6.2
. We will deal with each of the three statements in Proposition
6.2
in turn.

Proof of Proposition
6.2
-
1)
.

Firstly, it is clear
f_{1}\left(0\right)=0
. Then note that
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\varPhi
, and hence

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq C,

(6.2)

where we use
(.1)
(via (
5.31
)) in the last inequality. Furthermore
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(0)\right)=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=1
, and therefore
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{0}}\left(k\right)=\widehat{G}_{0}\left(k\right)=1
and
f_{2}\left(0\right)\leq C
. Similarly we find that

f_{3}\left(0\right)=\esssup_{k,l\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}_{k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{3\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]}.

(6.3)

We use then Lemma
5.18
to get the bound

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}_{k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq C\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right].

(6.4)

Hence
f_{3}\left(0\right)\leq\tfrac{2}{3}C
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2
-
2)
.

We will assume that
f\leq\kappa+1
and show that this implies that
f\leq\kappa
. Most crucially, the assumption
f\leq\kappa+1
allows us to apply Observation
6.1
. To highlight the
\kappa
-dependence, we write
c_{\kappa+1}
as the constant arising from this observation.

It will be convenient to introduce some temporary notation for this section. Define
\widehat{a}\left(k\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
as
\widehat{a}\left(k\right)=\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)
for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We will also make use of the functions
\delta_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\delta_{\lambda}\left(k\right):=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)

(6.5)

It is clear from the triangle inequality and Lemma
3.4
that

\lvert\delta_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\rvert\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c_{\kappa+1}\beta,

(6.6)

but we will also need the following inequality. We will prove it at the end of this section.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then

\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right\rvert\leq 2\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

(6.7)

\widetilde{a}(k)(e)
\widetilde{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)(e)
-1/\lambda
1/\lambda

Figure 4
:
If
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
and
\widehat{a}(k)
commute, then they are simultaneously diagonalizable in the sense of Theorem
3.5
. The arguments of their diagonal functions (
\widetilde{\tau}_{\lambda}(k)
and
\widetilde{a}(k)
respectively) are related by the monotone increasing function
x\mapsto\tfrac{x}{1-\lambda x}
depicted here.

It will be important to note how
\widehat{a}(k)
relates to
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
. From Observation
6.1
, the OZE gives us the following expression involving the commutator
\left[\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k),\widehat{a}(k)\right]=\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\widehat{a}(k)-\widehat{a}(k)\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
:

\left[\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k),\widehat{a}(k)\right]\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\widehat{a}(k)\right)=0.

(6.8)

Lemma
5.23
implies that
\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\widehat{a}(k)\right)
has a bounded linear inverse and with (
6.8
) implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
and
\widehat{a}(k)
commute. Therefore there exists a single unitary map that ‘diagonalizes’ them both in the sense of Theorem
3.5
. By considering the diagonal functions, the OZE then implies that
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)/\left(1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)\right)
. In particular, this relation allows us to write an alternative expression for
\mu_{\lambda}
:

\mu_{\lambda}:=1-\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)}=\lambda+\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)-1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)}=\lambda+\frac{\delta_{\lambda}\left(0\right)}{1+\delta_{\lambda}\left(0\right)}.

(6.9)

•

We first address
f_{1}
. From the above discussion relating
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
and
\widehat{a}(k)
, we have
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)/\left(1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)\right)
. From Lemma
3.4
and our choice of scaling we have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)\geq 1-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq 1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta
, and therefore we have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)>0
for sufficiently large
d
and

\lambda\leq\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)}{\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\leq 1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta.

(6.10)

Here we have used an idea that we will use very often in this part of the proof. For
d
large enough that
c_{\kappa+1}\beta<1
we have
\left(1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)^{-1}=\sum^{\infty}_{n=0}\left(c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)^{n}
, and for
d
sufficiently large enough (
c_{\kappa+1}\beta\leq 1/2
) we have
\left(1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)^{-1}\leq 1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta
. We also use a similar idea of neglecting higher order terms by increasing an earlier term’s coefficient in other cases.

•

For
f_{2}
we first replace
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. Since
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
and
\widehat{a}(k)
commute, they are simultaneously diagonalizable in the sense of Theorem
3.5
, and therefore their spectra are related by the function
x\mapsto\tfrac{x}{1-\lambda x}
(monotone increasing on
\left(-\infty,1/\lambda\right)
). Therefore we have
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup_{x\in\sigma\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\left\lvert\frac{x}{1-\lambda x}\right\rvert
. Now (perhaps with the aid of Figure
4
) it is easy to see that

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\vee\mathbb{S}\left(-\widehat{a}(k)\right)=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\vee\mathbb{S}\left(-\widehat{a}(k)\right).

(6.11)

Now since
\mathbb{S}\left(-\widehat{a}(k)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{a}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 1+c_{\kappa+1}\beta
, and
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{-1}\leq 1+C
(recall Lemma
6.3
and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq C
from
(.1)
), we only need to bound

\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}.

(6.12)

We now partition
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
into two. Define the subset of the Fourier space

A_{\frac{1}{2}}:=\left\{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

(6.13)

Then we only need to bound the two functions
f_{4},f_{5}
, where

f_{4}\left(\lambda\right):=\esssup_{k\in A_{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)},\qquad\qquad f_{5}\left(\lambda\right):=\esssup_{k\in A_{\frac{1}{2}}^{c}}\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}.

(6.14)

We first deal with the simpler
f_{4}
. We consider
k\in A_{\frac{1}{2}}
and choose
\beta
small enough that

1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)\geq 1-\frac{\frac{1}{2}+c_{\kappa+1}\beta}{1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\geq\frac{1}{2}-2c_{\kappa+1}\beta>0.

(6.15)

Then

\frac{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\leq\frac{1+\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\frac{1}{2}-2c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\leq 2\left(1+C\right)\left(1+5c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right).

(6.16)

Since we have
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)\leq\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)\leq 1+c_{\kappa+1}\beta
, we then get

f_{4}(\lambda)\leq 2\left(1+C\right)\left(1+5c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left(1+c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\leq 2\left(1+C\right)\left(1+7c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right).

(6.17)

To address
f_{5}
we will have
k\in A^{c}_{\rho}
. We define:

\widehat{N}(k)=\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)},\qquad\widehat{F}(k)=\frac{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)},\qquad\widehat{Q}(k)=\frac{1+\delta_{\lambda}(k)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(0)\right)}.

(6.18)

Note that
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)=\widehat{N}(k)/\widehat{F}(k)
. Rearranging gets us to

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{N}(k)\frac{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{F}(k)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{Q}(k)+\frac{\widehat{N}(k)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{F}(k)}\left(1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)-\frac{\widehat{Q}(k)}{\widehat{N}(k)}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{F}(k)\right).

(6.19)

Since
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=1
(by our scaling choice), the extracted term
\widehat{Q}(k)
satisfies
\left\lvert\widehat{Q}(k)\right\rvert\leq 1+3c_{\kappa+1}\beta
. We further observe that

\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{Q}(k)}{\widehat{N}(k)}=\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\left(1+\delta_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(k)}=1-\frac{\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right)\delta_{\lambda}(k)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(k)}=:1-\widehat{b}(k).

(6.20)

Recalling identity (
6.9
) for
\mu_{\lambda}
, we can rewrite the quantity
\left[1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)-(1-\widehat{b}(k))\widehat{F}(k)\right]
, appearing in (
6.19
), as

\frac{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)-\left[\lambda+\delta_{\lambda}(0)+\lambda\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right]\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)-1+\lambda\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(k)\right)+\widehat{b}(k)\left(1-\lambda\widehat{a}(k)\right)}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}\\
=\frac{\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\left(\delta_{\lambda}(0)+\lambda\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right)+\lambda\left[\delta_{\lambda}(k)-\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right]}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}+\frac{\widehat{b}(k)(1-\lambda\widehat{a}(k))}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}.

(6.21)

Noting that
\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(0)-\delta_{\lambda}(k)\rvert\leq 2c_{\kappa+1}\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\beta
by Lemma
6.4
and Observation
6.1
, the first term can be bounded

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\left(\delta_{\lambda}(0)+\lambda\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right)+\lambda\left[\delta_{\lambda}(k)-\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right]}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{c_{\kappa+1}\beta+\left(1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta+2\left(1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta}{1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 5c_{\kappa+1}\beta\left(1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 6c_{\kappa+1}\beta\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right].

(6.22)

Using (
6.10
), the last term is

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\widehat{b}(k)(1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right))}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\frac{\left(1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right)\delta_{\lambda}(k)}{\left(1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right)\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(k)\right)}-\frac{\lambda\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\delta_{\lambda}(k)}{1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k)\right\rvert}{\left\lvert 1+\delta_{\lambda}(0)\right\rvert}\left\lvert\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(k)}-\lambda\right\rvert\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{\kappa+1}\beta}{1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}+\frac{1}{1-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\right)\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{\kappa+1}\beta\left(1+2c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left(3+8c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4c_{\kappa+1}\beta\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right].

(6.23)

Putting these bounds back into (
6.19
), we can find

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{Q}(k)+10c_{\kappa+1}\beta\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}\frac{\widehat{N}(k)}{\widehat{F}(k)}\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\

\displaystyle\leq 1+3c_{\kappa+1}\beta+20c_{\kappa+1}\beta\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\left(20\kappa+23\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta.

(6.24)

Note that we have used the bound
\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\leq\left[1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]
(since
\mu_{\lambda}\leq 1
and
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)>\frac{1}{2}>0
) to get from
\widehat{G}_{1}(k)^{-1}
to
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{-1}
. As
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\in\left(\frac{1}{2},1\right]
, this concludes the improvement of
f_{5}
and hence of
f_{2}
.

Before we treat
f_{3}
, we introduce
f_{6}
given by

f_{6}(\lambda):=\esssup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}\left(k\right)\right)}.

(6.25)

We show that
f(\lambda)\leq\kappa+1
implies
f_{6}(\lambda)\leq M
uniformly for some
\kappa
-independent constant
M
. For
k\in A_{\frac{1}{2}}
we showed in (
6.16
) that we can bound uniformly with
3\left(1+C\right)
for sufficiently large small
\beta
. For
k\in A_{\frac{1}{2}}^{c}
, we have

\frac{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}=\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\leq\left(1+\left(20\kappa+23\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left\lvert 1-\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(k)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\right\rvert\\
\leq\left(1+\left(20\kappa+23\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left(1+\frac{c_{\kappa+1}\beta}{\frac{1}{2}-c_{\kappa+1}\beta}\right)\leq 1+\left(20\kappa+27\right)c_{\kappa+1}\beta.

(6.26)

Note that for the first bound in (
6.26
), we used the estimate established in (
6.24
), which is stronger than a bound on
f_{5}
. We therefore have the uniform bound
f_{6}(\lambda)\leq 3+3C
for
\beta
sufficiently small.

•

Let us now improve the bound on
f_{3}
. It will be notationally convenient to denote
A\left(k\right):=\left[\mathds{1}-\lambda\widehat{a}\left(k\right)\right]^{-1}
. Since
\widehat{a}\left(k\right)
is self-adjoint, it can be “diagonalized” in the sense of Theorem
3.5
. Therefore
A\left(k\right)
can be “diagonalized” over the same space and with the same unitary operator - it will have the diagonal function
\widetilde{A}(k)(e)=1/\left(1-\widetilde{a}\left(k\right)(e)\right)
. It is then clear that
A\left(k\right)
and
\widehat{a}\left(k\right)
commute (for each
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
). Using Observation
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\widehat{a}\left(l\right)A\left(l\right)-\widehat{a}\left(l+k\right)A\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert A\left(l\right)\widehat{a}\left(l\right)\left[\mathds{1}-\lambda\widehat{a}\left(l+k\right)\right]A\left(l+k\right)-A\left(l\right)\left[\mathds{1}-\lambda\widehat{a}\left(l\right)\right]\widehat{a}\left(l+k\right)A\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert A(l)\left(\widehat{a}(l)-\widehat{a}(l+k)\right)A(l+k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\widehat{a}(l)-\widehat{a}(l+k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert A(l)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert A(l+k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(6.27)

For the
\left\lVert\widehat{a}(l)-\widehat{a}(l+k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
factor, we use the triangle inequality to separate this into a term of
\widehat{\varPhi}
and
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}
. Lemma
5.18
immediately gives

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq C\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right],

(6.28)

for the
C>0
arising from
(.1)
. For the
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}
term, a similar argument to (
F.34
) gives

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(1+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq c_{\kappa+1}\beta\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right],

(6.29)

where the last inequality follows from Observation
6.1
. We then deal with the remaining factors of (
6.27
) by noting that for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\lVert A(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\frac{1}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}=\frac{1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{a}(k)\right)}\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)\leq 3\left(1+C\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k),

(6.30)

employing our improved bound on
f_{6}
. The result of this is that we have the bound

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 9\left(1+C\right)^{2}\left(C+c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right]\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(l+k\right).

(6.31)

Then since
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)\geq 0
we have

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 9\left(1+C\right)^{2}\left(C+c_{\kappa+1}\beta\right)\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right).

(6.32)

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2
-
3)
.

The continuity of
f_{1}
is obvious. For the other two functions we prove equicontinuity of a family of functions and use
[
Sla06
, Lemma 5.13]
to show the continuity of the desired functions.

The procedure is outlined here. Suppose we wish to show that
H\left(\lambda\right):=\sup_{\alpha\in B}\left\lVert h_{\alpha}\left(\lambda\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
is continuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. For our purposes the parameter
\alpha
will be either
k
or
\left(k,l\right)
and thus
B=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
B=\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

•

The continuity on the half-open interval is implied by having continuity on the closed interval
\left[0,\lambda_{O}-\rho\right]
for any
\rho>0
.

•

For closed intervals,
[
Sla06
, Lemma 5.13]
gives continuity if the family
\left\{\left\lVert h_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\}_{\alpha\in B}
is equicontinuous and
H\left(\lambda\right)<+\infty
for all
\lambda
in the closed interval.

•

The family
\left\{\left\lVert h_{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\}_{\alpha\in B}
is equicontinuous if for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
|s-t|<\delta
implies
\left\lvert\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(s)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(t)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon
uniformly in
\alpha\in B
. In fact, the reverse triangle inequality implies that

\left\lvert\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(s)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(t)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(s)-h_{\alpha}(t)\right\rVert_{\rm op},

(6.33)

and therefore we only need to prove equicontinuity for the un-normed family
\left\{h_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in B}
.

•

We prove equicontinuity by bounding the ‘near-derivative’

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert h_{\alpha}(\lambda+\varepsilon)-h_{\alpha}(\lambda)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

(6.34)

uniformly in
\alpha\in B
for
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}-\rho\right]
and arbitrary
\rho>0
.

For
f_{2}
the operator-valued functions
h_{\alpha}
are
\lambda\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)/\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)
. By using a variation on the chain rule, we get

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda+\varepsilon}\left(k\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}}\left(k\right)}-\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\frac{1}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)}\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\\
+\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)^{2}}\left\lvert\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\widehat{G}_{\mu}\left(k\right)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{\lambda}}\right\rvert\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}-\mu_{\lambda}\right\rvert.

(6.35)

Recall from Corollary
D.2
that
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)
is differentiable with the bound
\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}
. We also have
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)\geq 1/\left(1+\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right)
from Lemma
6.3
and
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\widehat{G}_{\mu}\left(k\right)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{\lambda}}=\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)^{2}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
immediately from the definition. It remains to deal with the
\mu_{\lambda}
term. Recall that
\mu_{\lambda}:=1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)^{-1}
. Therefore, using the reverse triangle inequality,

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}-\mu_{\lambda}\right\rvert=\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)^{2}}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda+\varepsilon}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\right\rvert\\
\leq\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)^{2}}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda+\varepsilon}(0)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)^{2}}\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2},

(6.36)

where we have used
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\geq 1
from Lemma
6.3
in the last inequality. Therefore

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda+\varepsilon}\left(k\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}}\left(k\right)}-\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)}{\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left(1+\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right)\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}+\left\lvert\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right\rvert\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}\\
\leq\left(1+C\right)\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}+C\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{3}.

(6.37)

We therefore have a finite
k
-independent bound.

This has proven the equicontinuity. For the uniform boundedness, we note that for
\lambda\in\left[0,\lambda_{O}-\rho\right]
we have
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{O}-\rho}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}<\infty
. In conjunction with
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)\geq 1/\left(1+\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right)
we have the required uniform boundedness and therefore the continuity of
f_{2}
.

We repeat this approach for
f_{3}
. The corresponding step to (
6.35
) now reads

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lVert\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda+\varepsilon,k}\left(l\right)}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}}\left(k,l\right)}-\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\frac{1}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\\
+\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)^{2}}\left\lvert\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\widehat{J}_{\mu}\left(k,l\right)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{\lambda}}\right\rvert\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}-\mu_{\lambda}\right\rvert.

(6.38)

Recall from Corollary
D.2
that
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)
is differentiable with the operator norm bound
\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 4\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. The operator itself has the similar bound
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. We would like to have the bound (
6.31
), but this was proven under the assumption
f\leq\kappa+1
, which we no longer assume. Fortunately, we only require our bound for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}-\rho
. Therefore we can use Lemma
D.3
and Lemma
5.18
to get

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\text{e}^{4\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\\
\leq C\text{e}^{4\left(\lambda_{O}-\rho\right)\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{O}-\rho}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left[1-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)\right].

(6.39)

From this and
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)\geq\left(1+C\right)^{-1}
, there exists a constant
\widetilde{M}>0
such that both

\frac{1}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\widetilde{M}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op},\qquad\frac{1}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\widetilde{M},

(6.40)

for sufficiently large
d
.

Now recall
\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\widehat{G}_{\mu}\left(k\right)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{\lambda}}=\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)^{2}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
and
