\left(1+C\right)^{-1}\leq\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k\right)\leq\left(1-\mu_{\lambda}\right)^{-1}=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. Therefore an application of the chain rule gives the bound

\left\lvert\frac{1}{\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}\widehat{J}_{\mu}\left(k,l\right)\right|_{\mu=\mu_{\lambda}}\right\rvert\leq 6\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{4}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op},

(6.41)

uniformly in
k
and
l
. These properties, along with the bound on
\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\mu_{\lambda+\varepsilon}-\mu_{\lambda}\right\rvert
from above, are sufficient to prove equicontinuity and the uniform boundedness for the required
\lambda
. Therefore we have proved that
f_{3}\left(\lambda\right)
is continuous.

∎

It just remains to prove that we can indeed bound the difference of
\delta_{\lambda}(k)
with the difference of
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
as we claimed.

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

First note that there is nothing to prove if
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}=0
, because the triangle inequality then forces
\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})=\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})=0
.

Without loss of generality, assume
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
with
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}>0
. Then

\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})=\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})}{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k_{1})\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left(\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right)+\left[\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right]

(6.42)

The second factor is the more troublesome:

\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})\frac{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right\rvert=\frac{1}{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert\\
\leq\frac{\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})\right\rvert\vee\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right\rvert}{\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left\lvert\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert.

(6.43)

Since
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\geq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
, we have
\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})\right\rvert\vee\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
. Therefore

\left\lvert\delta_{\lambda}(k_{1})-\delta_{\lambda}(k_{2})\right\rvert\leq 2\left\lvert\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert\leq 2\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}\left(k_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op},

(6.44)

where the last inequality holds because of the reverse triangle inequality.
∎

7
Proof of Main Theorems

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
d>6
be sufficiently large. Then for
\lambda\leq\lambda_{O}
there exists
c>0
such that
\triangle_{\lambda}<c\beta^{2}
.

Proof.

Proposition
5.15
gives us that there exists
c_{f}
(increasing in
f
) such that
\triangle_{\lambda}\leq c_{f}\beta^{2}
for all
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
. Proposition
6.2
then implies that there exists
c
such that
c_{f}\leq c
uniformly for all
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
.

It remains to prove the assertion for
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
.
Recall from Section
D
the definition of
\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
and
\Lambda_{n}(x)
. Furthermore, recall that if
x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}
then such a connection is achieved in finitely many steps and the bound in
(.1)
then implies that

\left\{x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}\right\}=\bigcup^{\infty}_{n=1}\left\{x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}_{\Lambda_{n}(y)}\right\}.

(7.1)

Therefore by monotone convergence we have the pointwise limit
\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)\to\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
as
n\to\infty
for all
\lambda>0
and
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. Since
\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
only depends upon the finite region
\Lambda_{n}(y)
, the functions
\lambda\mapsto\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
are continuous for all
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. This, with the monotonicity
\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)\leq\tau_{\lambda}^{n+1}(x,y)
, implies that the function
\lambda\mapsto\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
is lower-semicontinuous. Since
\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
is non-decreasing in
\lambda
, this lower semi-continuity implies that the pointwise limit
\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)\uparrow\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)
as
\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}
holds, and the limit holds monotonically.

This monotonic pointwise convergence implies that the integral

\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,v)\tau_{\lambda}(v,y)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right)\to\int\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(x,u)\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(u,v)\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(v,y)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v\right)

(7.2)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
as
\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}
, and that this convergence is monotone increasing. If we take the supremum over
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, we get that
\lim_{\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
. The uniform bound for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
then implies that this bound also holds at
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
.
∎

The following proposition uses the bound
\triangle_{\lambda_{O}}<c\beta^{2}
to show that percolation does not occur at criticality. Another element of the proof is that there is almost surely at most one infinite cluster. There are general considerations that show that this is the case. Indeed
[
GKN92
]
establishes that on the discrete space
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
there is at most one infinite cluster if the edge occupation measure is stationary and obeys the ‘finite energy property’, and an analogous result for Poisson processes in the continuum applies in our case (see
[
BM93
,
MR96
]
).

See
2.6

Proof.

Note that the strong form of irreducibility in Assumption
I
implies the more general form of irreducibility in
[
CL24
]
. We can therefore use their result that the infinite cluster is almost surely unique when it exists.

Suppose for contradiction that
\theta_{\lambda_{O}}(a)>0
for some positive measure of marks. Then there almost surely exists a unique infinite cluster. Assume that
(\overline{0},a),(\overline{x},b)\in\mathbb{X}
are in this almost surely unique infinite cluster, and are therefore connected. Then the FKG inequality implies that

\theta_{\lambda_{O}}(a)\theta_{\lambda_{O}}(b)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{O}}\big(|\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},a)|=\infty,|\mathscr{C}(\overline{x},b)|=\infty\big)\leq\tau_{\lambda_{O}}((\overline{0},a),(\overline{x},b)).

(7.3)

Since
\triangle_{\lambda_{O}}\leq c\beta^{2}
, for
\mathcal{P}
-a.e.
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
there exists a sequence
\overline{x}^{(a,b)}_{n}
such that
\tau_{\lambda_{O}}((\overline{0},a),(\overline{x}^{(a,b)}_{n},b))\to 0
. Therefore for
\mathcal{P}
-a.e.
a\in\mathcal{E}
we have
\theta_{\lambda_{O}}(a)=0
.
∎

We can define the linear operator
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}
by its action on
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
:

\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}f\right)(x)=\int\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right).

(7.4)

Contrary to
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}
may be an unbounded linear operator.

Proposition 7.2

(The operator OZE at the critical point)
.

Let
d>6
be sufficiently large. The Ornstein-Zernike equation then extends to
\lambda_{O}
in the sense that the sum of (unbounded) linear operators vanishes:

\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}-\varPhi-\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\lambda_{O}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)=0.

(7.5)

Furthermore, for
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
the linear operator
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{O}}(k)
is a bounded linear operator and the following equality holds:

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(k\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(k\right)-\lambda_{O}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(k\right)\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(k\right)\right)=0.

(7.6)

Finally, there exists
c>0
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c\beta,\qquad\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c\beta.

(7.7)

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
. Then by the triangle inequality, for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}

\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}-\varPhi-\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\lambda_{O}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)f\rVert_{2}\leq\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}-\varPhi-\varPi_{\lambda}-\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\right)f\rVert_{2}\\
+\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}-\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}-\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}+\varPi_{\lambda}-\lambda_{O}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)+\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda}\right)\right)f\rVert_{2}.

(7.8)

The first norm on the right hand side vanishes, since the OZE holds for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
. To prove that the left hand side vanishes, we aim to show that the second norm on the right hand side vanishes as
\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}
. To do this, the triangle inequality implies that we only need to show that the following limits hold as
\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}
:

\displaystyle\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}-\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\to 0,

(7.9)

\displaystyle\lVert\left(\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\to 0,

(7.10)

\displaystyle\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\to 0.

(7.11)

We begin with (
7.9
). Recall from the proof of Proposition
7.1
that
\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,y):=\tau_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)-\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
converges monotonically and pointwise to zero. From the definition of the
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
norm,

\displaystyle\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}-\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left(\int\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

(7.12)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,z)\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(z)\right\rvert\nu^{\otimes(3)}\left(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z\right).

(7.13)

The monotone convergence of
\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)\downarrow 0
then implies that the limit (
7.9
) holds.

For (
7.10
), we use the functions
h^{(n)}_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by

h^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y):=\begin{cases}\lambda^{-1}\int\psi_{n}(x,w_{n-1},u_{n-1})\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\psi(\vec{v}_{i})\right)\psi_{0}(w_{0},u_{0},y)\nu^{\otimes(2n)}\left(\mathrm{d}\left(\left(\vec{w},\vec{u}\right)_{[0,n-1]}\right)\right)&:n\geq 1\\
\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,w\right)\varphi\left(w,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}w\right)\right)^{2}&:n=0.\end{cases}

(7.14)

From Proposition
5.5

and the proof of Proposition
5.2
, we know that
0\leq\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)\leq h^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)
for all
n\geq 0
,
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, and
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
- the non-negativity follows from the definition of
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}
. The functions
h^{(n)}_{\lambda}
can also be defined for
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
, and since they are monotone increasing in
\lambda
, we have the
\lambda
-independent bound
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)\leq h^{(n)}_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)
for all
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
and
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. For the single-mark version of the model it is proven in
[
HHLM22
, Corollary 6.1]
that
\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}
converges pointwise to zero. This same argument works for our multi-mark version, and therefore
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)\leq h^{(n)}_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)
for all
\lambda\leq\lambda_{O}
. The triangle inequality then implies that

\left\lvert\left(\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\sum^{\infty}_{n=0}\left(-1\right)^{n}\left(\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)-\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)\right)\right\rvert\leq 2\sum^{\infty}_{n=0}h^{(n)}_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y).

(7.15)

It will be convenient to define
h_{\lambda}\colon\mathbb{X}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
as
h_{\lambda}(x,y):=\sum^{\infty}_{n=0}h^{(n)}_{\lambda}(x,y)
. Then

\displaystyle\lVert\left(\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int\left(\int\left(\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)f(y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle=\int\left(\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)\left(\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}\right)(x,z)f(y)f(z)\nu^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z\right)

(7.16)

\displaystyle\leq 4\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,z)\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(z)\right\rvert\nu^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z\right)

\displaystyle=4\int\left(\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right).

(7.17)

By Schur’s test, this integral is finite for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
if both the values

\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)

(7.18)

are finite. To prove this for the former, we repeat the arguments of Proposition
5.2
and Section
5.1
to bound it in terms of
\lambda_{O}
, the triangle diagrams, and other
\lambda
-independent terms. The argument of Proposition
7.1
with the uniform bounds of Observation
6.1
then proves that
\esssup_{y}\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)
is indeed finite. A similar argument also holds for
\esssup_{x}\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)
.

We now have an integrable function
h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,z)\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(z)\right\rvert
that dominates the integrand of (
7.16
). Recall that
\pi_{\lambda_{O}}-\pi_{\lambda}
converges pointwise to zero. Thus the dominated convergence theorem implies that the limit (
7.10
) holds.

For (
7.11
), we can use
h_{\lambda_{O}}
to get the bound

\lVert\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}-\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPi_{\lambda}\right)f\rVert_{2}^{2}\\
\leq\int\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,u)h_{\lambda_{O}}(u,y)\left(\tau_{\lambda_{O}}-\tau_{\lambda}\right)(x,v)h_{\lambda_{O}}(v,z)\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(z)\right\rvert\nu^{\otimes 5}\left(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y,\mathrm{d}z\right).

(7.19)

The integrand converges monotonically to zero, and therefore the integral vanishes in the limit.

We now consider the Fourier version of the OZE. We use the notation that, given a bounded linear operator
A\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{X})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{X})
, the Fourier transform is given by
\mathcal{F}\left(A\right)(k)\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
. By hypothesis,
\varPhi
is a bounded operator, and the finiteness of
\esssup_{y}\int h_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)
implies that
\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}
is also bounded. Then (
7.5
) implies that

\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)\right)(k)=\mathcal{F}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)(k)

(7.20)

for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We now make the claim that
\mathcal{F}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)(k)
has a bounded linear inverse for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
. This is proven in Lemma
7.3
below. This claim then implies that for
k\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)(k)\mathcal{F}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)(k)^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)\right)(k)\mathcal{F}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)(k)^{-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\varPhi+\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right)\right)^{-1}\right)(k)=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\right)(k).

(7.21)

That is,
\mathcal{F}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\lambda_{O}}\right)(k)
is a bounded linear operator for
k\neq 0
.

The limit (
7.10
) also allows us to extend our sub-critical bound on
\varPi_{\lambda}
to apply at criticality. The limit (
7.10
) may be phrased as saying that the function
\lambda\mapsto\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}f\right\rVert_{2}
is continuous at
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
(from the left) for all
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
. Since
\lambda\mapsto\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
can be written as a supremum of such functions, it is a lower semi-continuous function. Our bound
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c\beta
for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
(from Proposition
5.22
) then implies

\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda_{O}}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\liminf_{\lambda\uparrow\lambda_{O}}\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq c\beta.

(7.22)

The limit
\left\lVert\left(\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}^{(n)}(0)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(0)\right)f\right\rVert_{2}\to 0
for each
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
can be derived in essentially the same way as (
7.10
) above. The above lower semi-continuity argument can then be used to show that the bound from Proposition
5.22
extends to also hold at
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
. From Lemma
3.3
and the positivity of
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda_{O}}(x,y)
this bound also holds for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By summing over
n
, we then get the desired bound on
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

For
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
and
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
, the bounded linear operator
\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda_{O}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}(k)\right)\right)\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
has a bounded linear inverse.

Proof.

We first introduce some notation. Let
A\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
be a self-adjoint linear operator. Then we define the
minimum modulus
to be

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\rm min}:=\inf_{0\neq f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})}\frac{\left\lVert Af\right\rVert_{2}}{\lVert f\rVert_{2}}.

(7.23)

Note that it follows from the Spectral Theorem (Theorem
3.5
) that
A
has a bounded linear inverse if and only if
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\rm min}>0
. Now suppose that
A=A_{\lambda}
, and that for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
the map
\lambda\mapsto\left\lVert A_{\lambda}f\right\rVert_{2}
is continuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
. Then the map
\lambda\mapsto\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm min}
is upper semicontinuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
. That is, for any sequence
\lambda_{n}
in
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
such that
\lambda_{n}\to\lambda
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left\lVert A_{\lambda_{n}}\right\rVert_{\rm min}\leq\left\lVert A_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm min}.

(7.24)

We now consider
A_{\lambda}=\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)
. A similar argument to the way we proved (
7.10
) shows that
\lambda\mapsto\left\lVert A_{\lambda}f\right\rVert
is continuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
. Our result is then proven if we can show that for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
we have an
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(k)>0
such that
\left\lVert\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\right\rVert_{\rm min}\geq\varepsilon
uniformly in
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. We will in fact show the stronger result that
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\leq 1-\varepsilon
uniformly in
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
.

Recalling the definition of
\delta_{\lambda}(k)
from (
6.5
), we have

\displaystyle 1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)-\lambda\delta_{\lambda}(k)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)}_{>0\text{ from Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lem:Optlam-a}}}+\lambda\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)+\lambda\left(\delta_{\lambda}(0)-\delta_{\lambda}(k)\right)

\displaystyle>\lambda\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)\right).

(7.25)

Here we have used Lemma
6.4
to bound the difference of
\delta
with the difference of
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}
. For sufficiently large
d
,
(.2)
then proves
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\leq 1-\varepsilon
uniformly in
\lambda\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
.
∎

Note that in conjunction, Proposition
5.22
, Proposition
6.2
and Proposition
7.2
prove the OZE equations of Theorem
2.4
. In particular, the former two prove that the OZE holds in the subcritical regime, and the latter extends this to criticality where possible. Furthermore, Proposition
5.22
gives the bounds on
\varPi_{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
subcritically whilst Proposition
7.2
extends this bound to criticality.

Regarding Theorem
2.5
, the bound on
\triangle_{\lambda}
and the lack of percolation at criticality were proven at the beginning of this section. The infrared bound component of the theorem is proven below.

Proof of Theorem
2.5
.

First note that there is nothing to prove for
k=0
. From Proposition
7.2
and Lemma
7.3
, we can write

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)=\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\left(\mathds{1}-\lambda\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)\right)^{-1},

(7.26)

for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
, and for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
if
\lambda=\lambda_{O}
.

If we use the Spectral Theorem (Theorem
3.5
) to ‘diagonalize’ the operator
\widehat{a}(k)=\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
, we get a multiplication operator on some Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathfrak{E}_{k},\mu_{k})
taking values
\widetilde{a}(k)(e)
for
e\in\mathfrak{E}_{k}
. Since,
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)
is formed from
\widehat{a}(k)=\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)
, it can also be diagonalized over
L^{2}(\mathfrak{E}_{k},\mu_{k})
, and takes values

\widetilde{\tau}_{\lambda}(k)(e)=\frac{\widetilde{a}(k)(e)}{1-\lambda\widetilde{a}(k)(e)}.

(7.27)

Since
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)<1
(from the proof of Lemma
7.3
), we know
\lambda\widetilde{a}(k)(e)>1
and
\lambda\widetilde{\tau}_{\lambda}(k)(e)\geq-1
for
\mu_{k}
-almost every
e\in\mathfrak{E}_{k}
. It therefore only remains to bound
\widetilde{\tau}_{\lambda}(k)(e)
from above. Equivalently, we only need to bound
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)
from above.

We also note that the proof of (
7.10
) can be adapted to show that
\lambda\mapsto\left<f,\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)f\right>
is continuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
for all
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
. Then because
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)=\sup_{f}\tfrac{\left<f,\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)f\right>}{\left<f,f\right>}
, this proves that
\lambda\mapsto\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)
is lower-semicontinuous on
\left[0,\lambda_{O}\right]
. The bound
\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)<1
for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
(from Lemma
5.23
) then implies that
\lambda_{O}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda_{O}}(0)\right)\leq 1
.

From (
7.27
) the calculation proceeds:

\displaystyle\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\underbrace{1-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)}_{\geq 0}+\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(0)\right)-\lambda\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)+\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}(k)\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)+\delta_{\lambda}(0)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)-\delta_{\lambda}(k)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)}{\left(\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)\right)}=\frac{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\beta\right)}{\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)-\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)},

(7.28)

where we have bounded
\delta_{\lambda}(0)-\delta_{\lambda}(k)
using Lemma
6.4
and Observation
6.1
.
∎

Appendix A
Critical Intensities

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

By the Mecke equation (
3.6
), we find

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle=1+\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)=1+\lambda\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(0;a,b)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right),

(A.1)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=1+\lambda\esssup_{b}\int\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=1+\lambda\esssup_{b}\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(0;a,b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=1+\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(A.2)

From Lemma
3.2
we have
\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(0;a,b)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. Therefore
\lambda^{(1)}_{T}\geq\lambda_{O}\geq\lambda_{T}^{(\infty)}
.

Also recall from the discussion in Section
2.1
that
\lambda_{c}\geq\lambda^{(1)}_{T}
. Therefore we only now need to prove that
\lambda^{(1)}_{T}=\lambda^{(\infty)}_{T}
. We do this by proving that if
\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{1}<\infty
then
\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
.

Fix
b\in\mathcal{E}
. Then by considering the vertices in the cluster
\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},b)
adjacent to
\left(\overline{0},b\right)
, we find

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},b)\right\rvert\right]\leq 1+\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\sum_{\left(\overline{x},a\right)\in\eta:\left(\overline{x},a\right)\sim\left(\overline{0},b\right)}\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{x},a)\right\rvert\right]=1+\lambda\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{x},a)\right\rvert\right]\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right).

(A.3)

In this equality we have used Mecke’s equation (
3.6
). From the spatial translation invariance of the model,
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{x},a)\right\rvert\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},a)\right\rvert\right]
for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Therefore an application of a supremum bound to the
a
-integral gives

\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},b)\right\rvert\right]\leq 1+\lambda\left(\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\right)\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}(\overline{0},c)\right\rvert\right]\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)\\
=1+\lambda\left(\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\right)\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{1}.

(A.4)

From the finiteness of the parenthesised factor,
\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{1}<\infty
implies
\left\lVert\chi_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty}<\infty
and the result is proven.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

The equality
\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right)=\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)
holds from
[
MMS88
]
. The equality
\lambda_{O}=\lambda_{T}^{(p)}
follows from Proposition
2.1
because the condition (
2.11
) clearly holds for the bounded radii model. Also recall from the discussion in Section
2.1
that
\lambda_{c}\geq\lambda^{(p)}_{T}
for all
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
.

Now note that for all
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
we have that if
\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right)\sim\left(\overline{x},b\right)
then
\left(\overline{0},a\right)\sim\left(\overline{x},b\right)
. Therefore
\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right)\geq\lambda_{c}
, and the equality
\lambda_{c}\left(\dagger\right)=\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)
then means we only need to prove
\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)\leq\lambda_{T}^{(p)}
for some
p\in\left[1,\infty\right]
. We will do so for
p=1
.

Suppose that
\lambda>\lambda_{T}^{(1)}
, so that
\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};a\right)\right]\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=\infty
. That is, the expected cluster size of a vertex with a random (
\mathcal{P}
-distributed) mark is infinite. Note that we have an ordering of the marks in that if two radii
a,b
satisfy
a\geq b
, then
\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,c\right)\geq\varphi\left(\overline{x};b,c\right)
for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
c\in\mathcal{E}
. This also implies that
a\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0},a\right)\right\rvert\right]
is a non-decreasing function. This in turn implies that if
\lambda>\lambda_{T}^{(1)}
and
a
satisfies
\int\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)>0
, then
\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};b\right)\right\rvert\right]\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)=\infty
.

Given some radius
a\in\mathcal{E}
such that
a>0
and
\int\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)>0
, we define a new mark,
\star_{a}
, that forms connections according to

\varphi\left(\overline{x};\star_{a},b\right)=\mathds{1}\left\{\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert\leq a\right\},

(A.5)

for all
b\in\mathcal{E}
. Note that if
b\geq a
then
\varphi\left(\overline{x};\star_{a},c\right)\leq\varphi\left(\overline{x};b,c\right)
for all
c\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};\star_{a}\right)\right\rvert\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};b\right)\right\rvert\right]
. We now consider the expected cluster size of vertices adjacent to
\left(\overline{0},\star_{a}\right)
with radius
\geq a
. We also restrict to cases for which there is an unique neighbour, and that neighbour has radius
\geq a
. Since the probability of a connection is independent of the radius of the proposed neighbour, the distribution of the unique neighbour is equal to
\mathcal{P}
- conditioned upon having radius
\geq a
. Therefore we have the lower bound

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};\star_{a}\right)\right\rvert\right]\geq 1+\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\exists!\left(\overline{x},b\right)\in\eta:\left(\overline{x},b\right)\sim\left(\overline{0},\star_{a}\right),b\geq a\right)\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};b\right)\right\rvert\right]\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right).

(A.6)

As noted above,
\int\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};b\right)\right\rvert\right]\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)=\infty
. Furthermore, as
a>0
and
\int\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)>0
, the random variable
\#\left\{\left(\overline{x},b\right)\in\eta:\left(\overline{x},b\right)\sim\left(\overline{0},\star_{a}\right),b\geq a\right\}
obeys a Poisson distribution with strictly positive mean. Therefore
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\exists!\left(\overline{x},b\right)\in\eta:\left(\overline{x},b\right)\sim\left(\overline{0},\star_{a}\right),b\geq a\right)>0
and
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};\star_{a}\right)\right\rvert\right]=\infty
. This in turn implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};a\right)\right\rvert\right]=\infty
for all
a>0
.

To relate this to
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right)\right\rvert\right]
, we perform a similar lower bound. Given a radius
a>0
and
\int\mathds{1}\left\{b\geq a\right\}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)>0
, we restrict to cases where there is an unique neighbour of
\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right)
with radius
\geq a
and bound

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right)\right\rvert\right]\geq 1+\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\exists!\left(\overline{x},b\right)\in\eta:\left(\overline{x},b\right)\sim\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right),b\geq a\right)\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0};a\right)\right\rvert\right].

(A.7)

As before, the probability factor is strictly positive and the expected cluster size term is infinite. Hence
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\left\lvert\mathscr{C}\left(\overline{0},\dagger\right)\right\rvert\right]=\infty
and
\lambda\geq\lambda\left(\dagger\right)
. Therefore
\lambda_{T}\left(\dagger\right)\leq\lambda^{(1)}_{T}
as required.

∎

Appendix B
Model Properties

Here we will prove that the models outlined in Section
2.3
do indeed satisfy Assumption
H
.

First we consider the single mark “finite variance models” considered by
[
HHLM22
]
. As proven in that reference, this includes the Poisson blob and Gaussian connection models.

Lemma B.1
.

Single mark finite variance models satisfy the conditions of Assumption
H
.

Proof.

First note that since
\mathcal{E}
is a singleton, the operators
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
are simply scalars. In particular, this means that the conditions (
2.14
), and (
2.15
) of
(.1)
hold trivially. The finiteness of
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
is also equivalent to the finiteness of
\int\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}x
required by
[
HHLM22
]
.

Since
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
is simply a scalar, assumption
(.2)
follows directly from the third finite variance condition of
[
HHLM22
]
.

The condition
(.3)
is similarly a generalisation of the second finite variance condition, and therefore follows. Specifically, the sets
B(x)
are the
\varepsilon
-balls around
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
0<\varepsilon<r_{d}
where
r_{d}
is the radius of the ball of volume
1
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Space-mark factorisation models satisfy the conditions of Assumption
H
.

Proof.

Note that
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)=\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)\mathcal{K}
, where
\mathcal{K}\colon L^{2}(\mathcal{E})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
is the self-adjoint linear integral operator with kernel function
K
. Since
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\esssup_{b}\int K(a,b)\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}a)\leq 1
,
\mathcal{K}
is a bounded operator and

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)=\begin{cases}\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)\mathbb{S}\left(\mathcal{K}\right)&:\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)\geq 0\\
-\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)\mathbb{S}\left(-\mathcal{K}\right)&:\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)<0.\end{cases}

(B.1)

Since
\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq 1
, the finiteness of
\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(0)
implies that
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
is finite, and the
d
-independence of
\mathcal{K}
implies that
(.1)
holds.

Since
\overline{\varphi}(\overline{x})\geq 0
, we have
\left\lvert\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)\right\rvert<\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(0)
for all
k\neq 0
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{S}\left(\mathcal{K}\right)=\left\lVert\mathcal{K}\right\rVert_{\rm op}
implies that
\mathbb{S}\left(\mathcal{K}\right)\geq\mathbb{S}\left(-\mathcal{K}\right)
. Then
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)
inherits the properties required by
(.2)
from
\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)
.

For
(.3)
, we note that the
d
-independence of
\mathcal{K}
means that required decay properties are again inherited from
\widehat{\overline{\varphi}}(k)
.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

The marked multivariate Gaussian model satisfies the conditions of Assumption
H
.

Proof.

Firstly note that for each
\left(a,b\right)\in\mathcal{E}^{2}
the Gaussian structure of
\varphi
means that it factorises over the
d
eigenvector directions of
\Sigma(a,b)
. This factorisation ensures that that the
d
-dimensional Fourier transform is the product of the
1
-dimensional Fourier transforms. We have

\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)=\mathcal{A}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}k^{\intercal}\Sigma(a,b)k\right).

(B.2)

In particular, when
k=0
the Fourier transform is
a,b
-independent. This means
\widehat{\varPhi}(0)=\mathcal{A}\mathbf{1}
where
\mathbf{1}
is the integral operator with constant kernel function
1
. For
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{E})
we have

\displaystyle\langle f,\mathbf{1}f\rangle

\displaystyle=\int f(a)\left(\int f(b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\left[f\right]^{2},

(B.3)

\displaystyle\langle f,f\rangle

\displaystyle=\int f(a)^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{P}}\left[f^{2}\right].

(B.4)

By Jensen’s inequality we therefore have
\mathbb{S}\left(\mathbf{1}\right)=1
(equality follows by considering the test function
f(a)\equiv 1
). It therefore follows that
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)=\mathcal{A}<\infty
. From (
B.2
) we have

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=\mathcal{A}\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}k^{\intercal}\Sigma(a,b)k\right)\leq\mathcal{A}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\min}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(B.5)

This bound then implies the condition (
2.14
) of
(.1)
holds with
C=1
.

Before we address (
2.15
) of
(.1)
, we turn to
(.2)
. From our above bounds we already have

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\mathcal{A}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\min}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right)=\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{\min}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(B.6)

This exponential term ensures that
(.2)
is satisfied.

We now return to (
2.15
) of
(.1)
. For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
1-\cos(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}x^{2}
, and therefore

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(e\cdot\overline{x}\right)^{2}\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x},

(B.7)

for some unit vector
e\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since the vector
e
picks out the second moment of
\varphi
in only one direction, a standard calculation gives

\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(e\cdot\overline{x}\right)^{2}\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\leq\Sigma_{\max}^{2}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(B.8)

Therefore
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
is bounded by a
d
-independent quadratic function. In conjunction with the quadratic bound of
(.2)
, this then proves (
2.15
) in the
k\to 0
regime. The remaining regime then holds because the triangle inequality implies
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq 2\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=2\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
.

To address
(.3)
, recall that the spatial convolution of two multivariate Gaussian functions with means
\overline{0}
and covariance matrices
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
is a multivariate Gaussian function with mean
\overline{0}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}
. Therefore for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\displaystyle\esssup_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a_{1},a_{2})\varphi(\overline{y};a_{3},a_{4})\mathrm{d}\overline{y}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathcal{A}^{2}\left(2\pi\right)^{-d/2}\esssup_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\det\left(\Sigma(a_{1},a_{2})+\Sigma(a_{3},a_{4})\right)\right)^{-1/2}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\overline{x}^{\intercal}\left(\Sigma(a_{1},a_{2})+\Sigma(a_{3},a_{4})\right)^{-1}\overline{x}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\mathcal{A}^{2}\left(4\pi\Sigma_{\min}\right)^{-d/2}\exp\left(-\frac{\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert^{2}}{4\Sigma_{\min}}\right)\leq\mathcal{A}^{2}\left(4\pi\Sigma_{\min}\right)^{-d/2}\to 0.

(B.9)

In particular, this bounds the integral appearing in the definition of the sets
B(x)
(see (
2.18
)) and therefore proves they are empty. For the bound of the convolution of three
\varphi
functions, we can get this immediately from the above calculation and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq C\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
. Specifically
(.3)
holds with
g(d)=\left(4\pi\Sigma_{\min}(d)\right)^{-d/2}
and the sets
B(x)=\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

The bounded-volume Boolean disc model satisfies the conditions of Assumption
H
.

Proof.

Crucial to the conditions of
(.1)
holding is the upper bound on
R_{d}(a)
in (
2.30
). We use the shorthand

R_{a,b}:=R_{d}(a)+R_{d}(b),\qquad V_{a,b}:=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}R^{d}_{a,b},

(B.10)

so
V_{a,b}
equals the Lebesgue volume of the
d
-dimensional Euclidean ball with radius
R_{a,b}
. Note that the upper bound
R^{({\rm max})}_{d}
on
R_{d}(a)
in (
2.30
) ensures
V_{a,b}\leq V^{({\rm max})}
uniformly in
d
. We first find

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\esssup_{b\in\left[0,1\right]}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)=\esssup_{b\in\left[0,1\right]}\int V_{a,b}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)\leq V^{({\rm max})}.

(B.11)

This proves the statement that
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
is finite in
(.1)
. From the condition (
2.31
) we also get

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)\geq\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)\geq\epsilon^{2}\frac{d\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}\int^{2\left(1-\frac{c_{2}}{d}\right)R^{({\rm max})}_{d}}_{0}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r\\
=\epsilon^{2}V^{({\rm max})}\left(1-\frac{c_{2}}{d}\right)^{d}\to\epsilon^{2}\text{e}^{-c_{2}}V^{({\rm max})},

(B.12)

where we have used the test function
f(a)\equiv 1
to lower bound
\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)
. We then prove (
2.14
) by bounding

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=\esssup_{a,b\in\left[0,1\right]}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\esssup_{a,b\in\left[0,1\right]}V_{a,b}\leq V^{({\rm max})}.

(B.13)

We will return for (
2.15
) after addressing
(.2)
.

For
(.2)
, it will be useful to have an expression for
\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)
for each
a,b\in\left[0,1\right]
. Suppose we are able to find
C_{1}\in\left(0,1\right)
and
C_{2}>0
such that for all
a,b\in\left[0,1\right]

\left\lvert\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)\right\rvert\leq\widehat{\varphi}(0;a,b)\left(C_{1}\vee\left(1-C_{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right)\right).

(B.14)

Then since the
k
-dependent factor is
a,b
-independent we can use Lemma
3.3
to show that

\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\leq\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right)\left(C_{1}\vee\left(1-C_{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right)\right),

(B.15)

and therefore prove that
(.2)
is satisfied.

In finding an expression for
\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)
we are assisted by the spherical symmetry of the connection function and follow
[
Gra08
, Appendix B]
. It transpires that

\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)=\left(\frac{2\pi R_{a,b}}{\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(R_{a,b}\left\lvert k\right\rvert\right),

(B.16)

where
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
is the Bessel function of the first kind of order
\frac{d}{2}
. This function has the expansion

J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(r\right)=\sum^{\infty}_{m=0}\frac{\left(-1\right)^{m}}{m!\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+m+1\right)}\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}+2m},

(B.17)

converging for all
r\geq 0
. We will consider three different regimes for
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
. For
r\ll\sqrt{d}
the expansion (
B.17
) is asymptotic, and since
R^{({\rm max})}_{d}
and
R^{({\rm min})}_{d}
are both asymptotically proportional to
\sqrt{d}
we have

\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)\sim\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}R_{a,b}^{d}-\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{4\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+2\right)}R_{a,b}^{d+2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}=V_{a,b}\left(1-\frac{R^{2}_{a,b}}{2\left(d+2\right)}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}\right)

(B.18)

for
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\ll 1
. This proves the quadratic part of the desired bound.

The Bessel function
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
is bounded and achieves its global maximum (in absolute value) at its first non-zero stationary point, denoted
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}
. From
[
AS64
, p.371]
, we have
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}=\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{1}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)
for a given
\gamma_{1}\approx 0.81
, and
\left\lvert J_{\frac{d}{2}}\left(j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\right)\right\rvert\leq Md^{-\frac{1}{3}}
for a
d
-independent
M>0
. Since
R_{a,b}\in\left[2c_{1},1/\sqrt{2\pi\text{e}}+o(1)\right]d^{\frac{1}{2}}
(for
c_{1}\in\left(0,1/\sqrt{8\pi\text{e}}\right]
from (
2.30
)) and
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R_{a,b}\in\left[\sqrt{\frac{\pi\text{e}}{2}}+o(1),\frac{1}{4c_{1}}+o(1)\right]d^{\frac{1}{2}}
we have

\left\lvert\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)\right\rvert\leq\left(\frac{2\pi R_{a,b}}{\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}Md^{-\frac{1}{3}}\leq\left(2e^{-1}+o(1)\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}Md^{-\frac{1}{3}}\to 0

(B.19)

for
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\geq j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R_{a,b}
.

We are now left with the intermediate range for
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
. The first positive zero of
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
occurs at
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}
, where
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}=\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+1+\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right)
for a given
\gamma_{2}\approx 1.86
(see
[
AS64
]
). In particular, we will always have
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}<j_{\frac{d}{2},1}
. From differential identities relating Bessel functions (see
[
Gra08
]
), we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)=-R_{a,b}\left(\frac{2\pi R_{a,b}}{\left\lvert k\right\rvert}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}J_{\frac{d}{2}+1}\left(R_{a,b}\left\lvert k\right\rvert\right).

(B.20)

The Bessel function
J_{\frac{d}{2}+1}(r)
is positive for
r>0
until its first positive zero at
j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}
, and
j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}>j_{\frac{d}{2},1}
. Therefore
\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)
is positive and decreasing on the whole region
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\in\left(0,j_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R_{a,b}\right]
. Since this overlaps with the high
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
range, and we have the uniform quadratic behaviour near
k=0
, the function
k\mapsto\left\lvert\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)\right\rvert
can never increase and approach
\widehat{\varphi}(0;a,b)
again after leaving a neighbourhood of
k=0
. We therefore have the bound (
B.14
) and thus have proven that
(.2)
is satisfied.

\left\lvert k\right\rvert
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)
V_{a,b}
j_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R_{a,b}
j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}/R_{a,b}
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}/R_{a,b}

Figure 5
:
Sketch of
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)
against
\left\lvert k\right\rvert
. It approaches its maximum quadratically as
\left\lvert k\right\rvert\to 0
. The first local maximum of
J_{\frac{d}{2}}
occurs at
j^{\prime}_{\frac{d}{2},1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{1}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}
. The first zero of
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)
occurs at
\left\lvert k\right\rvert R_{a,b}=j_{\frac{d}{2},1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}
where
\gamma_{2}>\gamma_{1}
. Furthermore,
\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)
is strictly decreasing until
\left\lvert k\right\rvert R_{a,b}=j_{\frac{d}{2}+1,1}\sim\frac{d}{2}+\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}+\frac{1}{2}
.

We return to (
2.15
) in
(.1)
. We first calculate the second moment:

\int\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert^{2}\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\frac{d}{d+2}V_{a,b}R^{2}_{a,b}.

(B.21)

From
\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert^{2}=\overline{x}_{1}^{2}+\ldots+\overline{x}^{2}_{d}
(for an orthonormal basis) and the rotational symmetry, we have

\int\left(e\cdot\overline{x}\right)^{2}\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\frac{1}{d+2}V_{a,b}R^{2}_{a,b}

(B.22)

for all unit vectors
e\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Therefore

\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(e\cdot\overline{x}\right)^{2}\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\leq\frac{4}{d+2}V^{({\rm max})}\left(R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\right)^{2}.

(B.23)

While
V^{({\rm max})}
is a constant,
R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\sim\sqrt{d/8\pi\text{e}}
and therefore we have a bound uniform in
d
. The argument now proceeds similarly to the corresponding part of the proof for the multivariate Gaussian model. From (
B.7
) and
(.2)
we have both sides of (
2.15
) satisfying quadratic bounds around
k=0
, and the triangle inequality with our other bounds prove that the bound also holds on the remainder.

For
(.3)
we first want to bound the convolution of three
\varphi
functions. Given marks
a_{1},\ldots,a_{6}\in\left[0,1\right]
and
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\mathbb{B}_{a,b}(\overline{x})
denote the ball of radius
R_{a,b}
centred on
\overline{x}
, and let
\mathds{1}_{a,b}\left(\overline{x}\right)=\mathds{1}\left\{\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert\leq R_{a,b}\right\}
. Then

\displaystyle\left(\varphi(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2})\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4})\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{5},a_{6})\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\mathds{1}_{a_{1},a_{2}}(\overline{y})\mathds{1}_{a_{3},a_{4}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\mathds{1}_{a_{5},a_{6}}(-\overline{z})\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\mathrm{d}\overline{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\mathds{1}_{a_{3},a_{4}}(\overline{z}-\overline{y})\mathrm{d}\overline{z}\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{y}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{y}\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{y}.

(B.24)

Then for
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{y}\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{y}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\int_{\delta\mathbb{B}_{a,b}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{y}+\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\setminus\delta\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rvert\esssup_{\overline{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\setminus\delta\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{y}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\left(V^{({\rm max})}\right)^{2}\delta^{d}+V^{({\rm max})}\esssup_{\overline{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\setminus\delta\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{y}\right)\right\rvert.

(B.25)

Let
\overline{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a unit vector. Then by spherical symmetry we have

\esssup_{\overline{y}\in\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\setminus\delta\mathbb{B}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\left(\overline{0}\right)}\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\overline{y}\right)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\delta R_{a_{1},a_{2}}\overline{e}_{1}\right)\right\rvert.

(B.26)

We bound the volume of this intersection with the volume of the
d
-ball with radius equal to the radius of the
d-1
-sphere formed by the intersection of their boundaries. This radius is equal to the length
h
in Figure
6(a)
, which is maximised when
R_{a_{5},a_{6}}
and
R_{a_{3},a_{4}}
are maximised and
\delta R_{a_{1},a_{2}}
is minimised. Recall we defined the constant
c_{1}\in\left(0,1/\sqrt{8\pi\text{e}}\right)
when we defined the model in Section
2.3
. Since we have
R_{a_{5},a_{6}},R_{a_{3},a_{4}}\leq 2R^{({\rm max})}_{d}
and
\delta R_{a_{1},a_{2}}\geq\delta c_{1}d^{\frac{1}{2}}
, we have
h\leq\sqrt{\left(2R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\right)^{2}-\delta^{2}c^{2}_{1}d}
- we have used the fact that the triangle is isosceles if both the
R_{a_{5},a_{6}}
and
R_{a_{3},a_{4}}
edges attain their maximum. The ball with this radius then has the volume

\frac{\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)}h^{d}\leq V^{({\rm max})}\left(1-\frac{\pi\delta^{2}c^{2}_{1}d}{\left(V^{({\rm max})}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(B.27)

Since
\frac{\pi\delta^{2}c^{2}_{1}d}{\left(V^{({\rm max})}\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{2}{d}}}=2\pi\text{e}\delta^{2}c_{1}^{2}\left(1+o(1)\right)
and
c_{1}>0
, this shows that

\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a_{5},a_{6}}\left(\overline{0}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\left(\delta R_{a_{1},a_{2}}\overline{e}_{1}\right)\right\rvert\leq V^{({\rm max})}\left(1-\pi\text{e}\delta^{2}c_{1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}

(B.28)

for
d
sufficiently large. Sine we now have a mark independent bound with the required decay, we have proved the bound for the convolution of three
\varphi
functions.

R_{a_{5},a_{6}}
R_{a_{3},a_{4}}
\delta R_{a_{1},a_{2}}
h

(a)

Diagram demonstrating the radius
h
in the three
\varphi
function argument.

R_{a,c}
R_{c,b}
\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert
h

(b)

Diagram demonstrating the radius
h
in the two
\varphi
function argument.

Figure 6
:
For both the arguments, we bound the intersection of two
d
-balls with a new
d
-ball that has the radius
h
in these diagrams.

For finding the sets
B(x)
in (
2.18
), we write

\int\varphi\left(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c\right)\varphi\left(\overline{y};c,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)=\int\left\lvert\mathbb{B}_{a,c}\left(\overline{x}\right)\cap\mathbb{B}_{c,b}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rvert\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right).

(B.29)

As before, we can bound the volume of this intersection independently of the mark
c
. We can bound it with the volume of the ball with radius
h
where
h\leq\sqrt{\left(2R^{({\rm max})}_{d}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert^{2}}
(as can be seen in Figure
6(b)
). As before, if
\left\lvert\overline{x}\right\rvert\geq\kappa d^{\frac{1}{2}}
for some
\kappa>0
, then the volume of the ball with radius
h
vanishes in the
d\to\infty
limit. Furthermore, if
\kappa\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\text{e}}}
then the volume of the ball with radius
\kappa d^{\frac{1}{2}}
vanishes in the
d\to\infty
limit. Therefore assumption
(.3)
is satisfied with the sets
B(x)\equiv B\left(\overline{x},a\right)=\left\{\left(\overline{y},b\right)\in\mathbb{X}:\left\lvert\overline{x}-\overline{y}\right\rvert\leq\kappa d^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}
for any
\kappa\in\left(0,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\text{e}}}\right)
.

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Linear Operator Lemmas

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

The first inequality in (
3.21
) holds from the expressions of
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\rm op}
in terms of the spectrum (see (
3.20
)). The boundedness and self-adjointness of
H
implies that the second inequality follows from an application of the Schur Test and the conjugate symmetry of its kernel function (see, for example
[
HS78
, Theorem 5.2]
). For the lower bound on
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
, we use the definition (
3.19
) and consider the test function
f(a)=1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

For the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2,\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
norms the inequality is clear from the definitions. For the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\rm op}
version, let
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
, and use
\rm Abs
to denote the operator
f\mapsto\left\lvert f\right\rvert
. While this operator is not linear, it is clear that
\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}
. With this setup,

\left\lVert Gf\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int\left\lvert\int g\left(x,y\right)f\left(y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right\rvert^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)\leq\int\left(\int\left\lvert g\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)\\
\leq\int\left(\int h\left(x,y\right)\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)=\left\lVert H{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}.

(C.1)

Therefore

\left\lVert G\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert Gf\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}}\leq\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert H{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}}=\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert H{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}\leq\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert Hf\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}}=\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(C.2)

Here we used that the image of
\rm Abs
is a subset of
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{X}\right)
.

A similar argument works for the
\mathbb{S}\left(\cdot\right)
inequality. Given
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X})
,

\left\lvert\left<f,Gf\right>\right\rvert\leq\int\left\lvert f(x)\right\rvert\left\lvert g(x,y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f(y)\right\rvert\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y\right)\leq\left<{\rm Abs}f,H{\rm Abs}f\right>.

(C.3)

Therefore

\mathbb{S}\left(G\right)=\sup_{f}\frac{\left<f,Gf\right>}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\leq\sup_{f}\frac{\left<{\rm Abs}f,H{\rm Abs}f\right>}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}=\sup_{f}\frac{\left<{\rm Abs}f,H{\rm Abs}f\right>}{\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}\leq\sup_{f}\frac{\left<f,Hf\right>}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}}=\mathbb{S}\left(H\right).

(C.4)

∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

It is a well-known result (for example see
[
Kat95
]
) that
\sigma(G+H)
is contained in the closed
\left\lVert H\right\rVert_{\rm op}
-neighbourhood of
\sigma(G)
. Perturbing
G
by
+H
, and perturbing
G+H
by
-H
then implies the result.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

We present the proof of (
3.27
) for the connection operator case. The two-point operator case follows similarly.

First suppose
\zeta\in{\rm ess.}\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)
. Therefore for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
P_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
{\rm Leb}\left(P_{\epsilon}\right)>0
and for all
k\in P_{\epsilon}
there exists
z\in\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
such that
\left\lvert\zeta-z\right\rvert<\epsilon
. Furthermore, given
z\in\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
there exists
g\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
with
\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{2}=1
such that
\left\lVert\left(z\mathds{1}-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)g\right\rVert_{2}<\epsilon
. For all
k\in P_{\epsilon}
we let
z^{(k)}
and
g^{(k)}
denote such choices. Now let
\widehat{f}
be any element of
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
supported on
P_{\epsilon}
with
\left\lVert\widehat{f}\right\rVert_{2}=1
. Then define

h\left(\overline{x},a\right):=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\text{e}^{-ik\cdot\overline{x}}\widehat{f}(k)g^{(k)}(a)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(C.5)

By the Fourier Inversion Theorem, the Fourier transform of
h
is given by
\widehat{h}\left(k,a\right)=\widehat{f}(k)g^{(k)}(a)
for
k\in P_{\epsilon}
and vanishes elsewhere. By Plancherel’s Theorem (that is, the Fourier transform is unitary),
h
and
\widehat{h}
have the same
L^{2}
-norm:

\left\lVert h\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\left\lVert\widehat{h}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\left(\int\left\lvert g^{(k)}(a)\right\rvert^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\\
=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lVert g^{(k)}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}=\left\lVert\widehat{f}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=1.

(C.6)

By applying Plancherel’s Theorem once again, we can write

\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\int\left\lvert\zeta g^{(k)}(a)-\int\widehat{\varphi}(k;a,b)g^{(k)}(b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right\rvert^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\\
=\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)g^{(k)}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}<\int_{P_{\epsilon}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(k)\right\rvert^{2}\left(\epsilon+\underbrace{\left\lVert\left(z(k)\mathds{1}-\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)g^{(k)}\right\rVert_{2}}_{<\epsilon}\right)^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}<4\epsilon^{2}.

(C.7)

Here we use the triangle inequality and
\left\lVert g^{(k)}\right\rVert_{2}=1
to replace
\zeta
with
z(k)
at the cost of an
\epsilon
. Since for each
\epsilon>0
we have constructed a unit
h\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
such that
\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert_{2}<2\epsilon
, we have proven that
\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)
has no bounded linear inverse and therefore
\zeta\in\sigma\left(\varPhi\right)
.

We now show the converse. Suppose
\zeta\in\sigma\left(\varPhi\right)
. Then for all
\epsilon>0
there exists
h\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
such that
\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{2}=1
and
\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert_{2}<\epsilon
. By using Plancherel’s Theorem to replace the original functions with their Fourier transforms, we get

\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int\left\lvert\zeta h\left(\overline{x},a\right)-\int\varphi\left(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,b\right)h\left(\overline{y},b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)\\
=\int\left\lvert\zeta\widehat{h}\left(k,a\right)-\int\widehat{\varphi}\left(k;a,b\right)\widehat{h}\left(k,b\right)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right\rvert^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right).

(C.8)

Now let us define the family of functions
g^{(k)}\colon\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{C}
by
g^{(k)}\left(a\right):=\widehat{h}\left(k,a\right)
for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then we can again re-write the equality above as

\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)g^{(k)}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(C.9)

For each
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
is self-adjoint and therefore by the Spectral Theorem (Theorem
3.5
) there exists a Hilbert space
L^{2}\left(\mathfrak{E}_{k},\mu_{k}\right)
, a unitary operator
U_{k}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathfrak{E}_{k}\right)
, and a measurable function
e\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)
such that
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
is unitarily equivalent to pointwise multiplication by
\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)
on
L^{2}\left(\mathfrak{E}_{k}\right)
. If we define
f^{\left(k\right)\left(e\right)}:=\left(U_{k}g^{\left(k\right)}\right)\left(e\right)\in\mathbb{C}
, then (
C.9
) becomes

\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert^{2}_{2}=\int\left(\int\left\lvert\zeta-\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert f^{(k)(e)}\right\rvert^{2}\mu_{k}\left(\mathrm{d}e\right)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(C.10)

Note that since the Fourier transform and the maps
\left\{U_{k}\right\}_{k}
are all unitary, the normalisation of
h
passes on to
g^{(k)}
and on to
f^{(k)(e)}
so that

\int\left(\int\left\lvert f^{(k)(e)}\right\rvert^{2}\mu_{k}\left(\mathrm{d}e\right)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}=1.

(C.11)

Now suppose for contradiction that
\left\lvert\zeta-\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)\right\rvert\geq\epsilon
for
\mu_{k}
-a.e.
e\in\mathfrak{E}_{k}
, for
{\rm Leb}
-a.e.
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then the normalisation of
f^{(k)(e)}
implies that

\int\left(\int\left\lvert\zeta-\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert f^{(k)(e)}\right\rvert^{2}\mu_{k}\left(\mathrm{d}e\right)\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\geq\epsilon^{2}.

(C.12)

However this contradicts the condition that
\left\lVert\left(\zeta\mathds{1}-\varPhi\right)h\right\rVert_{2}<\epsilon
. Instead, for each
\epsilon>0
there exists
P_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{\rm Leb}\left(P_{\epsilon}\right)>0
such that for all
k\in P_{\epsilon}
there exists
Q^{(k)}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathfrak{E}_{k}
with
\mu_{k}\left(Q^{(k)}_{\epsilon}\right)>0
such that for all
e\in Q^{(k)}_{\epsilon}
we have
\left\lvert\zeta-\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)\right\rvert<\epsilon
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the operator
U_{k}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)U_{k}^{-1}
is a multiplication operator on
L^{2}\left(\mathfrak{E}_{k}\right)
with function
e\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}(k)(e)
, and therefore it is easy to calculate its spectrum. Since the operator is unchanged by changing
\widetilde{\varphi}(k)(e)
on a
\mu_{k}
-null set, the spectrum is given by the essential image of
\widetilde{\varphi}(k)
:

{\rm ess.Im}\left(\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\right)=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\forall\epsilon>0,\mu_{k}\left(e\in\mathfrak{E}_{k}:\left\lvert\widetilde{\varphi}\left(k\right)\left(e\right)-z\right\rvert>\epsilon\right)>0\right\}.

(C.13)

Since the unitary equivalence preserves the spectrum, this gives us an expression for
\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
. In particular, we can write our previous conclusion as the following. For all
\epsilon>0
there exists
P_{\epsilon}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
{\rm Leb}\left(P_{\epsilon}\right)>0
such that for all
k\in P_{\epsilon}
there exists
z\in\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right)
such that
\left\lvert\zeta-z\right\rvert<\epsilon
. Equivalently, this means that
\zeta\in{\rm ess.}\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)
.

Since
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\sup\left\{\left\lvert z\right\rvert:z\in\sigma\left(A\right)\right\}
and
\mathbb{S}\left(A\right)=\sup\left\{z:z\in\sigma\left(A\right)\subset\mathbb{R}\right\}
for self-adjoint operator
A
, the remaining equalities follow from (
3.27
).
∎

Appendix D
Differentiating the Two-Point Operator

The ideas of this section can be found in
[
HHLM22
]
, but we will need extra care to account for the fact that we are dealing with operators and marks and not just functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Recall
\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right):=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y})
. Using the notation
x=\left(\overline{x},a\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
, define
\Lambda_{n}\left(x\right)=\left(\overline{x}+\left[-n,n\right)^{d}\right)\times\mathcal{E}
. Then we can define the truncated two-point function

\tau_{\lambda}^{n}\left(x,y\right):=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(x\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y}_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}\right).

(D.1)

We will want to give meaning to the event
\left\{x\longleftrightarrow\Lambda_{n}^{c}\left(y\right)\textrm{ in }\xi^{x}_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}\right\}
. To do so we add a “ghost vertex”
g
in the same way we added deterministic vertices, and add an edge between
v\in\xi_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}
and
g
with probability
1-\exp\left(-\int_{\Lambda_{n}^{c}\left(y\right)}\varphi(u,v)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\right)
. We now identify
\Lambda_{n}^{c}\left(y\right)
with
g
.

Lemma D.1

(Differentiability of
\tau_{\lambda}
)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
and
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. The function
\lambda\mapsto\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
is differentiable on
[0,\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon]
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore,
\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
converges to
\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
uniformly in
\lambda
and
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)
converges to a limit uniformly in
\lambda
. Consequently,
\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)
is differentiable w.r.t.
\lambda
on
[0,\lambda_{O})
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)=\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(y\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y,u},y\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\longleftrightarrow}}}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,y})\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(D.2)

Proof.

The proof of this lemma follows as in
[
HHLM22
, Lemma 2.2]
. The key idea is to uniformly bound the probabilities that a path that leaves
\Lambda_{n}(y)
is used by the various events. The importance of
\lambda_{O}
arises from the observation that for
\lambda<\lambda_{O}

\int\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)\leq\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right)\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}<\infty,

(D.3)

which is how we know that for
\nu
-almost every
y\in\mathbb{X}
we have
\int_{\Lambda_{n}^{c}\left(y\right)}\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

A family of operators
\left\{H_{x}\right\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
is differentiable at
x
if there exists an operator
F_{x}
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(H_{x+\varepsilon}-H_{x}\right)-F_{x}\right\rVert_{\rm op}=0.

(D.4)

Using the notation
x=\left(\overline{x},a\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{E}
, recall that we require that the model has
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-translation invariance and so we can write
\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)=\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,b\right)
. Also recall that we defined the Fourier transform
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
as the integral operator with kernel
\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k;a,b\right)=\int\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}
. We now define the
displaced
Fourier transform
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
as the integral operator with kernel
\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda,k}\left(l;a,b\right)=\int\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\cos\left(l\cdot\overline{x}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}
.

Corollary D.2

(Differentiability of
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
)
.

The operators
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)
are differentiable w.r.t.
\lambda
on
\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
and their derivatives are the bounded linear operators
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\colon L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
, and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
with kernel functions
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)
,
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}\left(k;a,b\right)
, and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda,k}\left(l;a,b\right)
respectively. Specifically, we have the following bounds on the derivatives:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2},

(D.5)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2},

(D.6)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq 4\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(D.7)

Proof.

We use Lemma
D.1
in conjunction with the Leibniz integral rule to differentiate the two-point operator.

Fix
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
and let
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\lambda_{O}\right)
. From the reasoning of (
D.3
) we know that the subcritical integral
\int\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{(\otimes 2)}\left(\mathrm{d}a,\mathrm{d}b\right)<\infty
, and therefore

\int\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(x,u\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\leq\int\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(x,u\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)<\infty,

(D.8)

for
\nu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{X}
. Here we have also used that
\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)
is
\left[0,1\right]
-valued. This implies that
\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(x,\cdot\right)\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
for
\nu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{X}
. In conjunction with
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
, Hölder’s inequality implies that the function
\Theta_{\varepsilon,x}\colon y\mapsto\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(x,y\right)\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert
is in
L^{1}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
for
\nu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{X}
. Furthermore,
\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)
is non-decreasing in
\lambda
and so for all
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\Theta_{\varepsilon,x}
dominates
y\mapsto\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)f\left(y\right)
. This domination by an
L^{1}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
function allows us to use the Leibniz integral rule to exchange the integral and derivative to get (for
\nu
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{X}
)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(\left[\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}f\right]\left(x\right)\right)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)f\left(u\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)=\int\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\right)f\left(u\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)=\left[\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right)f\right]\left(x\right),

(D.9)

where Lemma
D.1
gives the differentiability of the function
\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)
.

We now prove that the operator
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
is bounded. From Lemma
D.1
and the BK inequality, we get the bound

0\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(x,y)\leq\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\{y\longleftrightarrow u\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u}_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}\}\circ\{u\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u,x}_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}\}\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\\
\leq\int\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(u,y)\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(u\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u,x}_{\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)}\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\leq\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,y\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right),

(D.10)

where the last inequality holds because we are removing the
\Lambda_{n}\left(y\right)
restriction. Note that the right hand side of this equation is the kernel function of the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}
. Choose
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
, and use
\rm Abs
to denote the map
f\mapsto\left\lvert f\right\rvert
. Now the above inequality implies that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right]f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int\left\lvert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)\right\rvert\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,y\right)\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)
