\displaystyle=\int\left(\left[\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}{\rm Abs}{f}\right]\left(x\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)=\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}.

(D.11)

Since
\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}
, this then gives us

\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}}=\sup_{f}\frac{\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\right\rVert_{\rm op}=\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}<\infty.

(D.12)

Now we address the Fourier transform. Pick
\varepsilon\in(0,\lambda_{O}-\lambda)
. Recall
\left\lvert\text{e}^{ik\cdot\overline{x}}\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\right\rvert
is bounded by
\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)
for all
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
. From the reasoning of (
D.3
), we know that
\tau_{\lambda_{O}-\varepsilon}\left(\cdot;a,b\right)
is Lebesgue integrable for
\mathcal{P}
-a.e.
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
. We are then justified in using the Leibniz integral rule to say that for every
b\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{P}
-a.e.
a\in\mathcal{E}
,
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\int\text{e}^{ik\cdot\overline{x}}\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\int\text{e}^{ik\cdot\overline{x}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}
. Applying Lemma
D.1
as well as (
D.10
), we derive that for such
a,b
we have

\left\lvert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(k;a,b)\right\rvert\leq\int\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\tau_{\lambda}^{n}(\overline{x};a,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\leq\int\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{y};c,b)\,\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\,\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right).

(D.13)

Note that the right hand side of this equation is the kernel function of the operator
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)^{2}
. By repeating the argument for
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
- with
\mathbb{X}
replaced by
\mathcal{E}
- we similarly arrive at

\left\lVert\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}.

(D.14)

For the displaced Fourier transform, note that
0\leq 1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\leq 2
, and so the same argument as for the Fourier transform allows us to use the Leibniz integral rule to exchange derivative and integral. We can also improve on the immediate bound of
2\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{2}
for the operator norm of the derivative. Let
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}\left(l\right)\right]f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int\left\lvert\text{e}^{il\cdot\overline{x}}\right\rvert\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{y};c,b)\left\lvert f\left(b\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\,\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right)^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int 2\Big[\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\left(\overline{x}-\overline{y}\right)\right)\right)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{y};c,b)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{y}\right)\right)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{y};c,b)\Big]\left\lvert f\left(b\right)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\,\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\Big)^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle=4\left\lVert\left[\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)+\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)\right]{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2},

(D.15)

where we have used the cosine-splitting lemma (Lemma
5.9
) to divide the displacement factor
\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)
into one spanning
\overline{x}-\overline{y}
and one spanning
\overline{y}
. Using the above approach of (
D.12
) and the triangle inequality then produces the required bound.
∎

We use the following lemma in the proof of Proposition
6.2
to bound the displacement on the two-point operator using the displacement of the connection operator when working in the sub-critical regime away from the critical threshold.

Lemma D.3
.

For
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
,

\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\text{e}^{4\lambda\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(D.16)

Proof.

For the inequality removing
l
, let
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(l+k\right)\right)f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int\left\lvert\text{e}^{il\cdot\overline{x}}\right\rvert\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\left\lvert f\left(b\right)\right\rvert\,\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}b\right)\right)^{2}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right){\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}.

(D.17)

Since
\left\lVert{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}
, we can use the approach of (
D.12
) to get the first inequality.

For the second inequality, we first note that
\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{0}\left(k\right)=\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)
. We then use the differential inequality (
D.7
) to extend to
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
. Using the reverse triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left\lvert\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s+\varepsilon}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s+\varepsilon}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}-\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\left(\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(k\right)\right)\right|_{\lambda=s}\right\rVert_{\rm op}

\displaystyle\leq 4\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}.

(D.18)

Since
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
is monotone increasing for
s\in\left[0,\lambda\right]
, the right hand side of the inequality is bounded by
4\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}\left(0\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{s}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
, giving the exponential factor in the result.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs of Diagrammatic Bounds

See
5.1

Proof.

As
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(u_{n}\xleftrightarrow{\,\,A\,\,}x\textrm{ in }\xi^{u_{n},x}_{n+1})\leq\tau_{\lambda}(x,u_{n})
for an arbitrary locally finite set
A
, the definition (
4.9
) gives

\left\lvert r_{\lambda,n}(x)\right\rvert\leq\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(E.1)

Let
f\in L^{2}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)
. Then this gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{\lambda,n}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\left(\int\left\lvert r_{\lambda,n}(x,y)\right\rvert\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{2}\int\left(\int\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\right)\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\int\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\left(\int\pi_{\lambda}^{(n)}(u,y)\left\lvert f\left(y\right)\right\rvert\nu\left(\mathrm{d}y\right)\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}{\rm Abs}f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}.

(E.2)

Note that we were able to use Tonelli’s theorem to swap the
u
and
y
integrals because
\tau_{\lambda}
and
\pi^{(n)}_{\lambda}
are both non-negative and measurable. Finally, the approach of (
D.12
) implies the result.

For each
n\geq 0
, either
r_{\lambda,n}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
or
r_{\lambda,n}(x)\leq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{X}
. Therefore
\left\lvert\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}(k;a,b)\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}(0;a,b)\right\rvert
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
and
\left\lVert\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{R}_{\lambda,n}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
(by Lemma
3.3
) for all
k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. From (
E.1
) we have

\left\lvert\widehat{r}_{\lambda,n}(0;a,b)\right\rvert\leq\int\widehat{\tau}_{\lambda}(0;a,c)\widehat{\pi}_{\lambda}^{(n)}(0;c,b)\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right).

(E.3)

Therefore the same argument as above gives the second bound in the result.
∎

See
5.2

Proof.

Our first comment relates to these bounds and every bound we perform hereafter in Section
5.1
. From the positivity of
\pi^{(0)}_{\lambda}(x,y)
and Lemma
3.3
we have
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
, and by Lemma
3.2
we have
\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(0)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. However from the definitions of the norm we also have
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
, and so we will be interested in bounding this last norm.

Now note that for the event
\{y\Longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x}\}
to hold, either there is a direct edge between
y
and
x
, or there exist vertices
w,z
in
\eta
that are direct neighbours of
y
and have respective disjoint paths to
x
that both do not contain
y
. Hence, by the Mecke equation (
3.6
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(y\Longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{\mathbf{0},x})

\displaystyle\leq\varphi(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\Big[\sum_{(w,z)\in\eta^{(2)}}\mathds{1}_{(\{y\sim w\text{ in }\xi^{y}\}\cap\{w\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x}\})\circ(\{y\sim z\text{ in }\xi^{y}\}\cap\{z\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x}\})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\varphi(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\big((\{y\sim y\text{ in }\xi^{y,w}\}\cap\{w\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,w}\})

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\circ(\{y\sim z\text{ in }\xi^{y,z}\}\cap\{z\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{x,z}\})\big)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}z\right).

(E.4)

After applying the BK inequality to the above probability,

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(y\Longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x}\right)\leq\varphi(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left(\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\left\{y\sim w\text{ in }\xi^{y,w}\right\}\cap\left\{w\longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{w,x}\right\}\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}w\right)\right)^{2}\\
=\varphi(x,y)+\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,w\right)\varphi\left(w,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}w\right)\right)^{2}.

(E.5)

Thus, recalling that
\pi_{\lambda}^{(0)}(x,y)=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(y\Longleftrightarrow x\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,x})-\varphi(x,y)\geq 0
, and using the symmetry of
\varphi
and
\tau_{\lambda}
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(0)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle=\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\pi^{(0)}_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,w\right)\varphi\left(w,y\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}w\right)\right)^{2}\nu\left(\mathrm{d}x\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\esssup_{y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\varphi\left(y,u\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(u,x\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,w\right)\varphi\left(w,y\right)\nu^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{2}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(E.6)

We then use a supremum bound on this integral to split one of the
\varPhi
off from the others, and bound the other from above with
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
to get the first two bounds of the result.

For the last bound of Proposition
5.2
, we apply (
E.5
) and obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\rm op}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPi}_{\lambda}^{(0)}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle\qquad=\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\pi^{(0)}_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\left(\int\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u};c,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\varphi\left(-\overline{u}^{\prime};b,c^{\prime}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}^{\prime}-\overline{x};c^{\prime},a\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u};c,b\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 284.52756pt\times\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}c,\mathrm{d}c^{\prime},\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lambda^{2}\esssup_{b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\Big[\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u}\right)\right)\right)\varphi\left(-\overline{u}^{\prime};b^{\prime},c^{\prime}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}^{\prime}-\overline{x};c^{\prime},a\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u};c,b\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 85.35826pt+\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{u}\right)\right)\varphi\left(-\overline{u}^{\prime};b,c^{\prime}\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}^{\prime}-\overline{x};c^{\prime},a\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u};c,b\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\hskip 284.52756pt\times\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}c,\mathrm{d}c^{\prime},\mathrm{d}a\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\lambda^{2}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi+\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(E.7)

Here we have again used the symmetry of
\varphi
and
\tau_{\lambda}
, and also used Lemma
5.9
to split the cosine factor over
\overline{x}-\overline{u}
and
\overline{u}
.

We can consider each term individually by using the triangle inequality. By using a supremum bound, the symmetry of
\varphi
, and by bounding
\varphi
with
\tau_{\lambda}
, we get the bound

\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{3}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}.

(E.8)

∎

See
5.7

Proof.

We begin with the single operators. For
\Psi^{(1)}
we find

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(1)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{4}\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(w,u)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,s)\tau_{\lambda}(t,w)\tau_{\lambda}(u,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,t)\tau_{\lambda}(z,r)\nu^{\otimes 4}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}u\right),

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{4}\left(\esssup_{r,s\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(r,z)\tau_{\lambda}(z,t)\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(t,s)\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\left(\esssup_{t^{\prime},z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{X}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(t^{\prime},w)\tau_{\lambda}(w,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,z^{\prime})\nu^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}u\right)\right),

\displaystyle=\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle_{\lambda},

(E.9)

where we have used a supremum bound on the
t
and
z
integrals to split them into
L^{1}
and
L^{\infty}
bounds. It is also clear that
\left\lVert\Psi^{(3)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\triangle_{\lambda}
and
\left\lVert\Psi^{(4)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
. The
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
bound is more involved and we return to it in a moment.

We are able to represent these
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
norms and their bounds pictorially. The vertices of the diagrams represent the variables
w,u,z,t,a,b
, etc. appearing in the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
expression. The variables over which a
\nu
-integral is taken are represented by the
vertices, and the variables over which a supremum is taken are represented by the
vertices. The presence of a
\tau_{\lambda}
connecting two variables is then represented by a standard edge
between their vertices, and the presence of a
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
connecting two variables is correspondingly represented by an edge
\circ
between their vertices. When using a supremum bound to split the diagrams, an integrated vertex gets split into two new vertices, precisely one integrated vertex and one supremum vertex. Each edge connected to such a split vertex can independently choose which of the new vertices to associate to. The calculations producing the bounds on
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
for
j=1,3,4
can then be represented by:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(1)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\quad

\displaystyle=\quad\raisebox{-15.0pt}{

(E.11)

Our strategy for
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
is to use the spatial translation invariance to shift the origin to the bottom right vertex, and then use a supremum bound to split the diagram in two. Unfortunately, this spatial shift decouples the spatial and mark components of the vertices so - for example - at some vertices we end up taking suprema over marks whilst integrating over space. Let us introduce some more notation. The vertex
indicates that the spatial component is integrated over whilst the mark component is fixed and its supremum is taken after all the integrals. We also introduce the edge-vertex combination
which indicates that there is no term connecting the associated vertices, but there is a supremum taken over the spatial displacement. Note that in principle we could use a vertex that had a spatial supremum and a mark integral, but we will always bound that probability integral by the supremum. The bound for
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
can therefore be expressed in the following:

\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\quad\raisebox{-15.0pt}{
\hbox to61.31pt{\vbox to47.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.2pt\lower-19.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 59.06 23.62 M 61.82 23.62 C 61.82 25.15 60.58 26.39 59.06 26.39 C 57.53 26.39 56.29 25.15 56.29 23.62 C 56.29 22.09 57.53 20.85 59.06 20.85 C 60.58 20.85 61.82 22.09 61.82 23.62 Z M 59.06 23.62}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(E.12)

To clarify the calculation for
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
, we write out the integral here:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\esssup_{\overline{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{w}-\overline{s};a_{3},a_{1})\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{t}-\overline{w};a_{4},a_{3})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{u}-\overline{t};a_{6},a_{4})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z}-\overline{u};a_{5},a_{6})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{t}-\overline{z};a_{4},a_{5})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z};a_{5},a_{2})\mathrm{d}\overline{t}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{w}\mathrm{d}\overline{z}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 4}\left(\mathrm{d}a_{3},\mathrm{d}a_{4},\mathrm{d}a_{5},\mathrm{d}a_{6}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\esssup_{\overline{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},a_{2},a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{w}^{\prime}-\overline{s}+\overline{u};a_{3},a_{1})\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{t}^{\prime}-\overline{w}^{\prime};a_{4},a_{3})\tau_{\lambda}(-\overline{t}^{\prime};a_{6},a_{4})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z}^{\prime};a_{5},a_{6})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{t}^{\prime}-\overline{z}^{\prime};a_{4},a_{5})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z}^{\prime}+\overline{u};a_{5},a_{2})\mathrm{d}\overline{t}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{w}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{z}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}a_{3},\mathrm{d}a_{4},\mathrm{d}a_{5}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\left(\esssup_{\overline{s},\overline{t}^{\prime},\overline{z}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},a_{1},a_{2},a_{4},a_{5}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{w}^{\prime}-\overline{s}+\overline{u};a_{3},a_{1})\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}(\overline{t}^{\prime}-\overline{w}^{\prime};a_{4},a_{3})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z}^{\prime}+\overline{u};a_{5},a_{2})\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{w}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a_{3}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left(\esssup_{a_{6}\in\mathcal{E}}\int\tau_{\lambda}(-\overline{t}^{\prime};a_{6},a_{4})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{z}^{\prime};a_{5},a_{6})\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{t}^{\prime}-\overline{z}^{\prime};a_{4},a_{5})\mathrm{d}\overline{t}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{z}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}a_{4},\mathrm{d}a_{5}\right)\right).

(E.13)

Then the two components of the first factor that are joined by the
edge can be spatially translated together to form three consecutive edges. Note however, that the marks on either side of this join are not necessarily equal. We can therefore bound this first term by
\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}
. In summary, these manipulations then imply that
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\triangle_{\lambda}
.

\Psi^{(1)}_{0}
and
\Psi^{(2)}_{0}

}\quad\leq\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}.

(E.16)

In the first case we expanded a
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge into a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge and a contraction. Then in the contracted case we bound
\tau_{\lambda}\leq 1
on the diagonal edge.

We have now proven the bounds for the single operator norms. For most of the operator pairs, we can then use the sub-multiplicity of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
to immediately get sufficient bounds. The only pairs for which this strategy fails are:
\left\lVert\Psi^{(4)}\Psi^{(4)}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}_{n}\Psi^{(3)}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
,
\left\lVert\Psi^{(4)}\Psi^{(4)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
, and
\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}_{n}\Psi^{(4)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. We now deal with these cases by hand:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Psi^{(4)}\Psi^{(3)}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}=\left\lVert\Psi^{(2)}_{n}\Psi^{(3)}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}

\displaystyle=\quad\raisebox{-15.0pt}{
\hbox to41.19pt{\vbox to38.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.53474pt\lower-19.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(E.18)

For the first bound we used
\varphi\leq\tau_{\lambda}
, and then split the single supremum into two. For the second bound we used
\tau_{\lambda}\leq 1
on the diagonal edge.

See
5.8

Proof.

Recall from Lemma
5.5
that
\lambda\left\lVert\varPi_{\lambda}^{(n)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\Psi_{n}\Psi^{n-1}\Psi_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. We therefore aim to bound
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
for
j_{0}\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}
,
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-1}\in\left\{1,2,3,4\right\}
, and
j_{n}\in\left\{1,2\right\}
. We have two cases depending upon the parity of
n
.

If
n
is odd then we have an even number of operators. We pair them off, use the sub-multiplicity of
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
, and Lemma
5.7
to get

\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\left(V_{\lambda}^{2}\right)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}=V_{\lambda}^{n+1}.

(E.19)

If
n
is even, then we pair off the first
n
operators and treat the last
\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}
operator by itself. We then get

\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq U_{\lambda}\left(V_{\lambda}^{2}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}=U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n}.

(E.20)

Since
U_{\lambda}\geq V_{\lambda}
, we have the bound
U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Since there are
3
choices for
j_{0}
,
4
choices for each of
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n-1}
, and
2
choices for
j_{n}
, we get the pre-factor of
6\times 4^{n-1}
in our result by applying the triangle inequality.
∎

See
5.10

Proof.

The argument for
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{m})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
and
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{n})}_{n}\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{m})}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
is the same as that in Lemma
5.7
and Proposition
5.8
. We consider bounds for single operators and pairs of operators, and then use sub-multiplicity to get a bound for each diagram.

For
\left\lVert\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{m})}\ldots\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{1})}\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
the argument is a little more complicated. For
m=0,1
we can easily check each diagram satisfies the bound. For
m\geq 2
we have the issue that there exists one pair of operators which we can’t bound by
V_{\lambda}^{2}
:

}\quad\leq\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\leq U_{\lambda}^{2}.

(E.21)

If
m\geq 2
is even (an odd number of operators) then we use sub-multiplicity to pull off the
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
term which we bound with
U_{\lambda}
. We then pull off pairs which we can bound with
V_{\lambda}^{2}
to get the bound
U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{m}
. If
m\geq 2
is odd (an even number of operators) then we use sub-multiplicity to pull off the
\overline{\Psi}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
pair which we bound with
U_{\lambda}^{2}
. We then pull off pairs which we can bound with
V_{\lambda}^{2}
to get the bound
U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{m-1}
.
∎

See
5.13

Proof of Proposition
5.13
.

In bounding the diagrams with one displacement segment, we have three broad cases. Let
i\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,n\right\}
denote which segment the displacement lies upon. Our three cases are then:

1)

the displacement lies on the
\psi_{n}
segment (i.e.
i=n
),

2)

the displacement lies on the
\psi_{0}
segment (i.e.
i=0
),

3)

the displacement lies on a
\psi
segment (i.e.
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
).

We will aim to get a single bound for each diagram having a displacement crossing a single segment. This will be uniform in the sequence
\left(j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\right)
. We then account for the number of possible choices of
\left(j_{0},j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\right)
, and finally include the factor of
(n+1)^{2}
arising from using cosine-splitting (the explicit factor and the number of diagrams).

Case
1)
is the simplest. We use a supremum bound to pull off the displaced
\Psi_{n}
term. We then bound this by hand and use Lemma
5.10
to bound the remainder. In representing this scheme we use a shorthand
\Psi^{n-1}\Psi_{0}
to denote a particular sequence
\Psi^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\Psi^{(j_{1})}\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}
rather than actually the operators
\Psi
and
\Psi_{0}
defined above. We also use labelled grey shapes to represent these groupings of diagrams, highlighting only end vertices or vertices that connect to neighbouring segments. This scheme look like:

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 2U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-1}\left(\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}+W_{k}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq 4\overline{W_{k}}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-1}.

(E.22)

Note that this bound also holds if the displacement runs along the bottom of the
\Psi_{n}
segment. Since there are
3\times 4^{n-1}
possible sequences of indices for the
\Psi^{n-1}\Psi_{0}
diagram, this case contributes that as a pre-factor.

For Case
2)
, we need a few more tricks. Since we are taking the displacement across the top of the diagram, the only term that will make a contribution is
\Psi^{(1)}_{0}
. To tackle this case we first use the observation of
[
HHLM22
]
that the diagram we want to bound can in turn be bounded by a diagram using
\overline{\Psi}^{n-1}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
. As above, we use
\overline{\Psi}^{n-1}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
as a shorthand for a particular sequence
\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{n-1})}\ldots\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{1})}\overline{\Psi}^{(j_{0})}_{0}
. We are also imprecise in this notation over whether it is this operator or its adjoint. Nevertheless the diagrams we use are clear on which vertices are integrated over and which have suprema, and this will avoid ambiguity. We then spatially translate the supremum to the far end and pull off the
\overline{\Psi}^{n-2}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
diagram using a supremum bound. This looks like

}.

(E.23)

For
\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}
with
j=1,2,3
, a simple supremum bound shows that this starting diagram can be bounded by
\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\left\lVert\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}
. For
j=4
a little more care is needed:

}\quad\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}.

(E.24)

We therefore find that

}\quad\leq\begin{cases}\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{n-1}U_{\lambda}^{2}&:n\geq 3\\
\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}U_{\lambda}&:n=2.\end{cases}

(E.25)

Since there are
4
choices for the appended
\overline{\Psi}^{(j)}
term, and
4^{n-2}\times 2
for the remaining
\overline{\Psi}^{n-2}\overline{\Psi}_{0}
diagram, this case contributes
2\times 4^{n-1}
as a pre-factor.

For Case
3)
, we shall find we have
3
sub-cases, depending not only on the
j
-index of the displaced segment, but on which term we are looking at once it has been expanded. Sub-cases
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
and
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
deal with
j=1,2,3
. In Appendix
E.1
we expand out the various displaced segments using cosine-splitting and by expanding
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges into
\tau_{\lambda}
edges and points, and label each of the resulting diagrams as sub-case
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
and
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
. These sub-cases are distinguished by the strategy we use to bound them. Sub-case
\mathrm{\left(III\right)}
then addresses the case with
j=4
.

For sub-case
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
, we just use the
\Psi
structure of the diagrams. We first split off segments from the left of the displaced term like we did in Case
1)
. If
i=n-1
we can then bound the remaining two pair of segments with
\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}
(Appendix
E.1
explains how this bound is found). This can be described diagrammatically as

}\\
\leq\overline{W_{k}}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-1}.

(E.27)

Note that the since
U_{\lambda}\geq V_{\lambda}
, the bound in (
E.27
) is greater than or equal to the bound in (
E.26
). It will be convenient to find an
i
-independent bound. To this end, since the
i\leq n-2
case only exists for
n\geq 3
, we can bound an instance of a displaced diagram in Case
3)
, sub-case
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
with

\begin{cases}\overline{W_{k}}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-1}&:n\geq 3\\
\overline{W_{k}}U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{2}&:n=2,\end{cases}

(E.28)

uniformly in
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

For sub-case
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
, we use a different strategy. First we split off the earlier (left) segments as we did for the first sub-case and in Case
1)
. However we arrange the later terms differently - more like we did in Case
2)
. We associate our displaced
\Psi
term with
\tau_{\lambda}
and
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges from the subsequent term. If the subsequent term would be
\Psi^{(2)}_{n}
, these are actually two
\tau_{\lambda}
edges, but in our schematic diagrams we will draw a
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge and bear this special case in mind when it is relevant. While the displacement portrayed on the ‘top’ of the segment could equally well be on the ‘bottom’, the
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge will always be attached to the bottom in the usual orientation. Everything to the right of these
\tau_{\lambda}
and
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges can then be described as a sequence of
\overline{\Psi}
and
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
terms. If
i=n-1
, then we pair our augmented displaced segment with the subsequent
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
segment. We then perform a spatial translation to make the right-most vertex a supremum vertex like we did in (
E.23
). The remaining diagram of a
\Psi^{(j)}
segment and
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
segment connected by a
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
and
\tau_{\lambda}
edge can then be bounded by
\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)
(see Appendix
E.1
). Recall that if
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
is in fact
\overline{\Psi}^{(2)}_{0}
then we have two
\tau_{\lambda}
edges connecting the terms, not a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge and a
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge. We need to note this to get the
\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)
bound. The scheme is then represented diagrammatically as

\leq\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-2}.

(E.29)

If
i\leq n-2
, we also perform a spatial translation to move a supremum to the right-most vertex, but then also split off all
\overline{\Psi}
and
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
segments after the
\overline{\Psi}
segment immediately following the displaced term. Once again the remaining pair can be bounded by
\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)
(see Appendix
E.1
) and the split-off diagrams bounded using Lemma
5.10
. The scheme can be represented diagrammatically as

\circ
\times
\Psi^{i-1}\Psi_{0}
\Psi^{(j)}
\overline{\Psi}
\overline{\Psi}^{n-i-2}\overline{\Psi}_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\leq\quad\raisebox{-15.0pt}{
\hbox to172.84pt{\vbox to53.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.37207pt\lower-23.87704pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}

}\quad\times\begin{cases}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-4}&:i\leq n-3\\
U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-3}&:i=n-2\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\leq\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)\begin{cases}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-4}&:i\leq n-3\\
U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-3}&:i=n-2\end{cases}

(E.30)

Again for simplicity, we would like to get and
i
-independent bound for such diagrams. Since the
i=n-2
case only exists for
n\geq 3
, and the
i\leq n-3
case only exists for
n\geq 4
, we can bound an instance of a displaced diagram in Case
3)
, sub-case
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
with

\begin{cases}\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)U_{\lambda}^{3}V_{\lambda}^{n-4}&:n\geq 4\\
\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)U_{\lambda}^{2}&:n=3\\
\left(\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}\right)U_{\lambda}&:n=2,\end{cases}

(E.31)

uniformly in
i\in\left\{1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

We now address sub-case
\mathrm{\left(III\right)}
, that is when the displacement crosses a
\Psi^{(4)}
segment. This is the simplest sub-case because when we take a displacement across
\Psi^{(4)}
in isolation, we get exactly

}\quad=W_{k}.

(E.32)

We will be able to use the
\Psi
-arrangement of segments here. For
n\geq 4
we split off the earlier and later segments without pairing the displaced term with any of them. With Lemma
5.10
this produces the bound

\left(U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{i-1}\right)W_{k}\left(U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{n-i-1}\right)=\overline{W_{k}}U_{\lambda}^{2}V_{\lambda}^{n-2}\qquad:n\geq 4.

(E.33)

For
n=3
we split off two segments from one side and one from the other. This gives the finer bound

W_{k}U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}^{2}\quad:n=3.

(E.34)

For
n=2
, we use
j_{0}
to indicate which
\Psi_{0}
term we have and
j_{2}
to denote which
\Psi_{n}
term we have. We note that if
j_{0}=1,2
then
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{0})}_{0}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}\leq V_{\lambda}
, and if
j_{2}=1
then
\left\lVert\Psi^{(j_{2})}_{n}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}\leq V_{\lambda}
. Therefore if
\left(j_{0},j_{2}\right)\neq\left(3,2\right)
we have the bound
W_{k}U_{\lambda}V_{\lambda}
. If
\left(j_{0},j_{2}\right)=\left(3,2\right)
we proceed by hand. We bound
\varphi\leq\tau_{\lambda}
and spatially shift one vertex to show

\raisebox{-15.0pt}{

}\quad\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}.

(E.35)

Carefully comparing the above bounds gives the required bounds for
n\leq 2
. Including the multiplicity of each diagram (including that arising from cosine-splitting), we find that we can bound the displaced and subsequent non-displaced pair by
\left(24\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+2\overline{H_{k}}\right)
. Note that this includes summing over the
j
-index of the displaced term but not the index of the subsequent term. Since there are
3\times 4^{n-2}\times 2
options for the
j
-indices of the other segments, this case contributes this to the counting pre-factors in our result.
∎

See
5.12

Proof of Proposition
5.12
.

For
n=1
, we can consider each of the six cases by hand. Most of the diagrams can be dealt with routinely using cosine-splitting, supremum bounds, and spatial shifts. We enumerate the diagrams by their
\left(j_{0},j_{1}\right)
values corresponding to the constituent segments.

\displaystyle(1,1):

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq 3\left(\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle_{\lambda}+\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}+\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}W_{k}\right)

\displaystyle(1,2):

\times

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}+\triangle_{\lambda}W_{k}\right)

\displaystyle(2,1):

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}+\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}W_{k}\right)

\displaystyle(2,2):

\times

\displaystyle\leq\triangle_{\lambda}W_{k}

\displaystyle(3,1):

\times
\sim
\circ

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}+\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}+\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\triangle_{\lambda}W_{k}+\triangle_{\lambda}W_{k}\right)

These terms sum to no more than

13\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}U_{\lambda}.

(E.36)

For the
\left(3,2\right)
diagram, we use a different ‘trick.’ In Lemma
F.1
we will prove the bound
\tau_{\lambda}\left(x,y\right)\leq\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\varphi(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)
. When applied to this diagram, this looks like

(E.37)

Here we have also used the cosine-splitting result on the second part. For the first of these
4
-vertex diagrams, we split the supremum in two and get the bound
\lambda^{3}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\lambda\triangle_{\lambda}\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}
. For the second of the
4
-vertex diagrams, we also split the supremum in two and get the bound
\lambda^{3}\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
. For the
3
-vertex diagram we condition on whether two of the vertices are in each other’s
B
-set (defined in Assumption
(.3)
). In the following diagrams
\in B
indicates that an indicator of the form
\mathds{1}_{\left\{w\in B\left(u\right)\right\}}
relates two variables, and
\not\in B
indicates that an indicator of the form
\mathds{1}_{\left\{w\not\in B\left(u\right)\right\}}
relates them:

\times
\sim
\sim

\displaystyle=\quad\raisebox{-15.0pt}{
\hbox to35.24pt{\vbox to57.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.2pt\lower-19.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi_{k}\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\triangle^{(B)}_{\lambda}+4\lambda\mathbb{B}W_{k}.

(E.38)

∎

E.1
Displacement Diagram Case Distinctions

This section provides the details omitted from the proof of Proposition
5.13
. Here we show which diagrams arise from displacements crossing a
\Psi
segment, and whether they can be dealt with by the first sub-case,
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
, or by the second sub-case,
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
. Recall that
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
are those that use a
\Psi
decomposition of the larger diagram only (and the displacement is not crossing a
\Psi^{(4)}
segment), while
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
are those that will require a combination of a
\Psi
decomposition and a
\overline{\Psi}
decomposition.

We first expand out the displaced
\Psi
terms and label which are in sub-case
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
and which are in sub-case
\mathrm{\left(II\right)}
. Note that the displacement may be across the ‘top’ or the ‘bottom’ of the segments in the usual orientation. If we consider a displacement across the ‘top’ or the ‘bottom’ of a
\Psi^{(1)}
term, we use cosine-splitting and sometimes expand
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges into a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge and a contraction to get

For most of these, it is sufficient to separate the pair using a supremum bound and use the individual bound above for the displaced
\Psi^{(j)}
term and the bound on the
\Psi
or
\Psi_{n}
term from Lemma
5.7
. The only pairs that need a more careful approach are those that have the bound
W_{k}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
above for the displaced
\Psi^{(j)}
, followed by a
\Psi^{(4)}
or
\Psi^{(2)}_{n}
term. In these cases we consider the pair together and get the diagram bounds

}\quad\leq W_{k}\triangle_{\lambda}\leq W_{k}V_{\lambda}^{2}.

In both of these cases we bounded a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge by
1
(essentially omitting the edge) before applying a supremum bound. In the first we omit the diagonal edge and split the upper supremum vertex in two. In the second we omit the left-most vertical edge and apply a supremum bound at the bottom right vertex.

\mathrm{\left(II\right)}

5.13

}\quad\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}.

(E.45)

Also recall that if the
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
term is actually a
\overline{\Psi}^{(2)}_{0}
term, then the
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge connecting them is actually just a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge. We first investigate bounds for the displaced
\Psi^{(j)}
term with the connecting
\tau_{\lambda}
and
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges. By using supremum bounds, spatial translations, splitting supremum vertices in two, and sometimes expanding
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges into a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge and a contraction, we get

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{H_{k}}+W_{k}\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}.

For most of these, it is clear from a crude supremum bound that we have the bound
\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}+\overline{H_{k}}U_{\lambda}
for the pair of these with the subsequent
\overline{\Psi}
or
\overline{\Psi}_{0}
term (like we did for sub-case
\mathrm{\left(I\right)}
). The only pairs that need a more careful approach are those that have the bound
\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
or
\overline{W_{k}}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}
above, followed by a
\overline{\Psi}^{(4)}
or
\overline{\Psi}^{(2)}_{0}
term. We first consider the cases where they are followed by a
\overline{\Psi}^{(4)}
term. Here we use supremum bounds, spatial translations, and expanding
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edges into a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge and a contraction to get

\times
\circ
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}+\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}=\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times
\circ
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}+\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}=\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\overline{\triangle^{\circ\circ}_{\lambda}}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}.

We now consider the cases where they are followed by a
\overline{\Psi}^{(2)}_{0}
term. Recall that for this case, the connecting
\tau_{\lambda}^{\circ}
edge is in fact only a
\tau_{\lambda}
edge. We then get

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times
\circ

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\triangle^{\circ}_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2},

\times

\displaystyle\leq\overline{W_{k}}\triangle_{\lambda}\leq\overline{W_{k}}V_{\lambda}^{2}.

The remaining sub-case
\mathrm{\left(III\right)}
was fully dealt with in the main text.

Appendix F
Bounding diagrams with the Bootstrap Function

Lemma F.1
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
. Then

\displaystyle\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\varphi(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right),

(F.1)

\displaystyle\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\tau_{\lambda}(x,u)\varphi(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(F.2)

Then for
n\geq 2
,

\left\lVert\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{n}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\sum_{m=0}^{n}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{m}\lambda^{m}\left\lVert\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(F.3)

Proof.

By combining Mecke’s formula and the BK inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\tau_{\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\varphi(x,y)+\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[\sum_{u\in\eta}\mathds{1}_{\{x\sim u\text{ in }\xi^{x}\}\cap\{u\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{y}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}\left(\{x\sim u\text{ in }\xi^{x,u}\}\cap\{u\longleftrightarrow y\textrm{ in }\xi^{y,u}\}\right)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right)

\displaystyle=\varphi(x,y)+\lambda\int\varphi(x,u)\tau_{\lambda}(u,y)\nu\left(\mathrm{d}u\right).

(F.4)

In the last line, we have used that the two intersected events are independent. This is due to the fact that
\nu
is non-atomic and
x\notin\eta^{y,u}
a.s.

The second inequality is then proven nearly identically, but with the adjacency to the intermediate point
u
holding for
y
instead of
x
.

To get (
F.3
), we first use (
F.2
) to expand out the right-most
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
. This produces one term with
\varPhi
replacing the
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
, and one term with
\lambda\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi
replacing it. We then repeatedly use (
F.1
) to expand the left-most
\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
to get a
\varPhi
factor and a
\lambda\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}
factor until we have a total of
n
factors of
\varPhi
in the expression. Counting the number of ways to get to each possible end term then gives the binomial
\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{m}
factor as required.
∎

Lemma F.2
.

Let
H\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
be an integral linear operator with kernel function
h\colon\mathcal{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then for all
h\left(a,b\right)\in{\rm ess.Im}\left(h\right)
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
\mathcal{P}
-positive sets
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
such that
g_{i}(c):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}\left(E_{i}\right)}\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}\left(c\right)
for
i=1,2
satisfy

\left\lvert h\left(a,b\right)-\langle g_{1},Hg_{2}\rangle\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

(F.5)

Proof.

Since
h
is measurable, the set
A=\left\{c,c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}:\left\lvert h\left(c,c^{\prime}\right)-h\left(a,b\right)\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon\right\}
is measurable. Since
h\left(a,b\right)\in{\rm ess.Im}\left(h\right)
, it has positive measure:
\mathcal{P}\left(A\right)>0
. Furthermore, by considering the
\pi
-system of product sets
\left\{E_{1}\times E_{2}:E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}\text{ measurable}\right\}
that generates the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{E}^{2}
, we can find
B=E_{1}\times E_{2}
such that
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
are both measurable,
B\subset A
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(B\right)=\mathcal{P}\left(E_{1}\right)\mathcal{P}\left(E_{2}\right)>0
. Then use
E_{1},E_{2}
to define
g_{1},g_{2}
as in the statement on the lemma. Since
E_{1},E_{2}
are both measurable and have positive measure, both
g_{1},g_{2}\in L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
. We also have

\langle g_{1},Hg_{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(B\right)}\int_{B}h\left(c,c^{\prime}\right)\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}c,\mathrm{d}c^{\prime}\right)\in\left[h(a,b)-\varepsilon,h(a,b)+\varepsilon\right],

(F.6)

proving the result.
∎

Lemma F.3
.

Let
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
,
n\geq 2
, and
d>2m
. Then there exists finite
c_{f}=c_{f}(m,n,f(\lambda))
(increasing in
f(\lambda)
and independent of
d
) such that

\left\lVert\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\begin{cases}c_{f}&:n=2\\
c_{f}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}-m\overline{\epsilon}(d)}&:n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(F.7)

The condition
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
here is required to allow us to swap integrals over the mark space with integrals over the Fourier space. We are able to use Fubini’s Theorem because sub-criticality implies that the relevant integrals are finite.

Proof.

For clarity, let us introduce the notation

\varphi^{(n)}(x,y)=\int\prod^{n}_{j=1}\varphi(u_{j-1},u_{j})\nu^{\otimes(n-1)}\left(\mathrm{d}u_{[1,n-1]}\right),\quad\tau_{\lambda}^{(n)}(x,y)=\int\prod^{n}_{j=1}\tau_{\lambda}(u_{j-1},u_{j})\nu^{\otimes(n-1)}\left(\mathrm{d}u_{[1,n-1]}\right),

(F.8)

where
u_{0}=x
and
u_{n}=y
. Furthermore, for each
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
let us define the integral linear operator
\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
as the integral operator with kernel function

(a,b)\mapsto\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x};a,b\right):=\int\varphi^{(n-1)}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\tau_{\lambda}^{(m)}\left(\overline{u}-\overline{u}^{\prime};c,c^{\prime}\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u}^{\prime};c^{\prime},b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}^{\prime}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 2}\left(\mathrm{d}c,\mathrm{d}c^{\prime}\right).

(F.9)

Given
\delta>0
,
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
such that
\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\in{\rm ess.Im}\left(\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x};\cdot,\cdot\right)\right)
, we can use Lemma
F.2
to get measurable and
\mathcal{P}
-positive sets
E^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{1},E^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
such that

\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\leq\left\langle g^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{1},\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{2}\right\rangle+\delta,

(F.10)

where
g^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{i}(c)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}\left(E^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{i}\right)}\mathds{1}_{E^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{i}}\left(c\right)
for
i=1,2
.

Now let
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
be arbitrary measurable
\mathcal{P}
-positive sets and
g_{1},g_{2}
be their associated functions. We aim to produce bounds that are independent of the choice of
E_{1},E_{2}
. We first use the Fourier inversion theorem to reformulate the position behaviour in terms of a
k
-integral of the Fourier transforms, and use
(.1)
and
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
(so the relevant integrals are finite) to allow us to swap the
k
-integral and the mark integrals. This produces

\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lvert\int\text{e}^{-i\overline{x}\cdot k}\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right\rvert\\
\leq\int\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(F.11)

Recall that
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
is self-adjoint, so we can move
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}
to the other side of the inner product, and then we can use Cauchy-Schwarz and the definition of the operator norm to get

\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle=\left\langle\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}g_{1},\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\\
\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}g_{1}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}^{m}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}g_{1}\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rVert_{2}.

(F.12)

We can then use the definition of the bootstrap function
f(\lambda)
to replace the
\left\lVert\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\right\rVert_{\rm op}
factors with factors of
f(\lambda)
and
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)
. Also writing the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
as the square root of an inner product and using the self-adjoint property of
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
gives

\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{n-1}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)^{m}\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\leq f(\lambda)^{m}\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{m}\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(F.13)

We therefore want to derive bounds for the integral

\int\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{m}\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}.

(F.14)

We first derive pointwise and integral bounds for the
\widehat{\varPhi}
factors. Given
N\geq 1
, and
g(c)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}\left(E\right)}\mathds{1}_{E}(c)
for some measurable and
\mathcal{P}
-positive set
E\subset\mathcal{E}
,

\left\langle g,\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2N}g\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}(E)^{2}}\int_{E}\int_{E}\widehat{\varphi}^{(2N)}(k;c,c^{\prime})\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}c)\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}c^{\prime})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2N}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(F.15)

By using Cauchy-Schwarz and supremum bounds we can split this into norms of
\widehat{\varPhi}(k)
only, then use Lemma
3.3
to replace
k
with
0
, and finally use
(.1)
to bound these terms:

\left\langle g,\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2N}g\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2N}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{2,\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}^{N-1}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{2,\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}^{N-1}\leq C^{N}.

(F.16)

For the integral bound, we apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the
k
-integral and swap the
k
-integrals with the mark integrals to un-do the Fourier transforms. We get

\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\leq\left(\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
=\left\langle g_{1},\varPhi^{(2n-2)}\left(\overline{0}\right)g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\varPhi^{(2)}\left(\overline{0}\right)g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}},

(F.17)

where
\varPhi^{(N)}\left(\overline{0}\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
is the integral operator with kernel function
\left(a,b\right)\mapsto\varphi^{(N)}\left(\overline{0};a,b\right)
. Given
N\geq 1
, and
g(c)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}\left(E\right)}\mathds{1}_{E}(c)
for some measurable and
\mathcal{P}
-positive set
E\subset\mathcal{E}
,

\left\langle g,\varPhi^{(2N)}\left(\overline{0}\right)g\right\rangle=\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}(E)^{2}}\int_{E}\int_{E}\varphi^{(2N)}\left(\overline{0};c,c^{\prime}\right)\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}c)\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}c^{\prime})\right)\leq\left\lVert\varPhi^{(2N)}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(F.18)

Here for
N\geq 2
we bound with
\left\lVert\varPhi^{(2N)}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\left\lVert\varPhi^{2N}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}
from
(.3)
. For
N=1
we bound using
\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\in\left[0,1\right]
and
(.1)
:

\left\lVert\varPhi^{(2)}\left(\overline{0}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,c\right)\varphi\left(-\overline{x};c,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}c\right)\leq\esssup_{b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\mathcal{P}\left(\mathrm{d}a\right)\\
=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{1,\infty}\leq C.

(F.19)

Therefore

\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\leq\begin{cases}C&:n=2,\\
C^{\frac{1}{2}}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}&:n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(F.20)

We now return to bounding (
F.14
). To perform the
k
-integral, we split the domain. We first consider
B_{\varepsilon}(0)=\{k\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lvert k\right\rvert<\varepsilon\}
, where we choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
\varepsilon^{2}\leq\tfrac{1-C_{1}}{C_{2}}
- the constants
C_{1},C_{2}
coming from the assumption
(.2)
. From
(.2)
, we have
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)=\left(1-\mu_{\lambda}\mathbb{S}\left(\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\right)\right)^{-1}\leq 1/C_{2}\left\lvert k\right\rvert^{2}
on
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
and
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)\leq 1/\left(C_{2}\varepsilon^{2}\right)
on
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
. Our pointwise bound on the
\widehat{\varPhi}
factors then gives

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(0)}\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{m}\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\\
\leq\frac{C^{n}}{C^{m}_{2}}\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\int^{\varepsilon}_{0}\frac{1}{r^{2m}}r^{d-1}\mathrm{d}r=\frac{C^{n}}{C^{m}_{2}}\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}}{d-2m}\frac{\varepsilon^{d-2m}}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}},

(F.21)

where
\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}=d\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}/\Gamma\left(\tfrac{d}{2}+1\right)
is the surface area of an unit
d
-sphere. Note that for all fixed
\varepsilon>0
this term vanishes in the
d\to\infty
limit.

To deal with the integral over
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
, we find the upper bound

\int_{B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}}\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)^{m}\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\\
\leq\frac{1}{C^{m}_{2}\varepsilon^{2m}}\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2n-2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\leq\frac{C^{\frac{1}{2}}}{C^{m}_{2}\varepsilon^{2m}}\begin{cases}C^{\frac{1}{2}}&:n=2\\
g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}&:n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(F.22)

Note that the bounds we found were independent of the sets
E_{1},E_{2}
, and therefore these bounds also apply to
\left\langle g^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{1},\left(\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g^{(\overline{x},a,b,\delta)}_{2}\right\rangle
uniformly over every
\delta>0
,
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\mathcal{P}
-almost every
a,b\in\mathcal{E}
. Since we can take
\delta\to 0
, our bound also applies to
\left\lVert\varPhi^{n-1}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}^{m}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
.

For
n=2
, the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
integral dominates the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
integral, and so the result is proven.

For
n\geq 3
, both parts of the integral approach
0
as
d\to\infty
. If both are of the same order or if the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
integral dominates, then we can fix
\varepsilon>0
and get the result. On the other hand, if
g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\frac{1}{d}\mathfrak{S}_{d-1}\varepsilon^{d}
for all fixed
\varepsilon>0
and the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
integral always dominates, we can improve the overall bound by letting
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(d)
and having
\varepsilon(d)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
. Having a smaller value of
\varepsilon
produces a smaller bound for the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
integral, but a larger bound for the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
integral. To get an optimal
\varepsilon
we can set both terms to be of the same order - that is by having
\varepsilon(d)=2\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}A^{\frac{1}{d}}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2d}}\left(1-\frac{2m}{d}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}
for some fixed
A>0
. For this choice, we have both integrals of the order

\varepsilon^{-2m}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}=2^{-2m}\pi^{-m}A^{-\frac{2m}{d}}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{m}{d}}\left(1-\frac{2m}{d}\right)^{-\frac{2m}{d}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+1\right)^{-\frac{2m}{d}}\\
=\left(\frac{\text{e}}{2\pi}\right)^{m}g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{m}{d}}\frac{1}{d^{m}}\left(1+o\left(1\right)\right),

(F.23)

as
d\to\infty
.

∎

See
5.15

Proof.

The
n=1
case is trivial because
\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{x},a,b\right)\in\left[0,1\right]
.

For
n=2
, we first bound
\left\lVert\varPhi^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
. We get

\left\lVert\varPhi^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=\esssup_{x,y\in\mathbb{X}}\int\left\lvert\varphi(x,u)\varphi(u,y)\right\rvert\nu(\mathrm{d}u)\leq\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\int\left\lvert\varphi(x,u)\right\rvert^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}u)\\
\leq\esssup_{x\in\mathbb{X}}\int\left\lvert\varphi(x,u)\right\rvert\nu(\mathrm{d}u)=\left\lVert\varPhi\right\rVert_{1,\infty},

(F.24)

by using Cauchy-Schwarz and symmetry of
\varphi
in the first inequality and
\varphi(x,u)\in\left[0,1\right]
in the second. We then use
(.1)
to bound this by the constant
C
. We use Lemma
F.3
to get the required bound for the remaining terms arising from (
F.3
) in Lemma
F.1
.

For
n\geq 3
, we once again bound the terms in the relevant expansion in (
F.3
). For
\left\lVert\varPhi^{n}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
we use
(.3)
to get
\left\lVert\varPhi^{n}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}=O\left(g(d)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)
. We then use Lemma
F.3
to bound the remaining terms.
∎

For our proofs of Lemma
5.16
and Lemma
5.21
, we adopt the following notation. Given
g(\overline{x};a,b)
and
h(\overline{x};a,b)
, we define

(gh)(\overline{x};a,b):=\int g(\overline{x}-\overline{y};a,c)h(\overline{y};c,b)\mathrm{d}\overline{y}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{d}c).

(F.25)

This notational convention is associative and therefore can generalise to three or more terms unambiguously.

See
5.16

Proof.

By applying (
F.1
) and (
F.2
), we can get

\tau_{\lambda}(\overline{x};a,b)\leq\varphi(\overline{x};a,b)+\lambda(\varphi\varphi)(\overline{x};a,b)+\lambda^{2}(\varphi\tau_{\lambda}\varphi)(\overline{x};a,b).

(F.26)

We will in fact prove the more general result for the convolution of
m\geq 2

\tau_{\lambda}
-functions in dimensions
d>2m
. By bounding
\lambda\leq f(\lambda)
, and using Lemma
F.2
and the Fourier inversion theorem we can realise that we only need to get further bounds for the following objects. For
\vec{j}\in\left\{1,2,3\right\}^{m}
, we want to bound

\int\prod^{m}_{i=1}P^{j_{i}}_{2i-1,2i}(k)\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}},

(F.27)

where

P^{j}_{p,q}(k):=\begin{cases}\left\lvert\left\langle g_{p},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{q}\right\rangle\right\rvert&:j=1\\
\left\lvert\left\langle g_{p},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{q}\right\rangle\right\rvert&:j=2\\
\left\lvert\left\langle g_{p},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{q}\right\rangle\right\rvert&:j=3,\end{cases}

(F.28)

where
\left\{g_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{2m}
are the functions of the form
g_{i}(a)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}(E_{i})}\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}(a)
arising from the application of Lemma
F.2
.

We now bound the following terms in the same way as (
F.12
):

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}

(F.29)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(k)\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq f\left(\lambda\right)\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(F.30)

In terms of pointwise uniform bounds for the
\widehat{\varPhi}
terms, we have
\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq C
and
\left\langle g,\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g\right\rangle\leq C^{2}
(the latter also having been used in the proof of Lemma
F.3
).

As in Lemma
F.3
, we will require various integral bounds for these
\widehat{\varPhi}
terms. Suppose we have
r\in\left\{1,\ldots,m-1\right\}
instances of
P^{2}_{p,q}(k)
and
P^{3}_{p,q}(k)
, and
m-r
instances of
P^{1}_{p,q}(k)
. Then by applying Cauchy-Schwarz and the definitions of the
g_{i}
we get

\displaystyle\int\prod^{r}_{i=1}\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i-1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2i},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod^{m}_{i^{\prime}=r+1}\left\lvert\left\langle g_{2i^{\prime}-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2i^{\prime}}\right\rangle\right\rvert\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left(\int\prod^{r}_{i=2}\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i-1}\right\rangle\left\langle g_{2i},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i}\right\rangle\prod^{m}_{i^{\prime}=r+1}\left\langle g_{2i^{\prime}-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2i^{\prime}}\right\rangle^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\esssup_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2})\star\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left(\esssup_{a_{5},\ldots,a_{2m}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{5},a_{6})\star\ldots\star\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{2r-1},a_{2r})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\left.\left.\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{2r+1},a_{2r+2})\star\ldots\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{2m-1},a_{2m})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C^{m+r}.

(F.31)

In this last inequality we have used
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq C
and
\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)^{2}\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}(0)\right\rVert_{2,\infty}^{2}\leq C^{2}
to extract off
\varphi
and
\varphi^{(2)}
via supremum bounds on the spatial position. We will also require integral bounds where there are
m
factors of
P^{2}_{p,q}(k)
and
P^{3}_{p,q}(k)
, and where there are
m
factors of
P^{1}_{p,q}(k)
:

\displaystyle\int\prod^{m}_{i=1}\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i-1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\langle g_{2i},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{1}\right\rangle\left\langle g_{2},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int\prod^{m}_{i=2}\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i-1}\right\rangle\left\langle g_{2i},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)^{2}g_{2i}\right\rangle\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\esssup_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2})\star\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\left(\esssup_{a_{5},\ldots,a_{2m}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{5},a_{6})\star\ldots\star\varphi^{(2)}(\cdot;a_{2m-1},a_{2m})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C^{2m}.

(F.32)

\displaystyle\int\prod^{m}_{i=1}\left\lvert\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2i}\right\rangle\right\rvert\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\int\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2}\right\rangle^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\prod^{m}_{i=2}\left\langle g_{2i-1},\widehat{\varPhi}(k)g_{2i}\right\rangle^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\left(\esssup_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{4}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi(\cdot;a_{1},a_{2})\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{3},a_{4})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\left(\esssup_{a_{5},\ldots,a_{2m}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\varphi(\cdot;a_{5},a_{6})\star\ldots\star\varphi(\cdot;a_{2m-1},a_{2m})\right)\left(\overline{0}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C^{m}.

(F.33)

Note that for
m\geq 3
, better bounds are available via
(.3)
, but will not be required here.

We can now proceed to bound each of the expressions of the form (
F.27
) in much the same way as we did in the proof of Lemma
F.3
. We set
\varepsilon>0
and partition the integral over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into one over
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
and one over
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
. For the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)
integral we use the uniform bounds on the
\widehat{\varPhi}
terms and perform the integral of the
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}
terms (if there are any) as before. Note that we require
d>6
for all of these to be finite. For the
B_{\varepsilon}(0)^{c}
integrals we uniformly bound
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(k)\leq 1/\left(C_{2}\varepsilon^{2}\right)
and use the integral bounds we calculated above. Since we are only asking for a constant bound, we don’t need to worry about having
\varepsilon\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.

∎

See
5.18

Proof.

The first two inequalities in (
5.53
) holds from the general inequalities that holds for these norms (on a probability space for the second inequality). Then we can remove the
l
-dependence by performing the following calculation:

\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(l+k\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left\lvert\text{e}^{il\cdot\overline{x}}\right\rvert\left\lvert\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\\
=\esssup_{a,b\in\mathcal{E}}\int\left\lvert\left(1-\cos\left(k\cdot\overline{x}\right)\right)\varphi\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\left\lVert\widehat{\varPhi}\left(0\right)-\widehat{\varPhi}\left(k\right)\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(F.34)

The final step relating the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
-norm to the difference of the spectral suprema is precisely that given by (
2.15
) in
(.1)
.
∎

We now address
\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
.

See
5.19

Proof.

First note that for
k=0
the kernel function
\tau_{\lambda,k}\left(x,y\right)=0
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{X}
, and so the required inequality holds trivially. In this proof we hereafter may assume
k\neq 0
.

We use (
F.1
) to write

\left\lVert\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}\leq\left\lVert\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}+\lambda\left\lVert\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}.

(F.35)

We first get the bound on
\left\lVert\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right\rVert_{\infty,\infty}
, using a similar approach to Lemma
F.3
. For each
\overline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define the integral linear operator
\left(\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)\colon L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\to L^{2}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)
as the integral operator with kernel function

\left(a,b\right)\mapsto\left(\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x};a,b\right)\\
:=\int\varphi^{(2)}\left(\overline{x}-\overline{u};a,c\right)\tau_{\lambda}\left(\overline{u}-\overline{u}^{\prime};c,c^{\prime}\right)\tau_{\lambda,k}\left(\overline{u}^{\prime}-\overline{u}^{\prime\prime};c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}\right)\varphi\left(\overline{u}^{\prime\prime};c^{\prime\prime},b\right)\mathrm{d}\overline{u}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\overline{u}^{\prime\prime}\mathcal{P}^{\otimes 3}\left(\mathrm{d}c,\mathrm{d}c^{\prime},\mathrm{d}c^{\prime\prime}\right).

(F.36)

Recall the notation
\varphi^{(2)}
used in (
F.8
). As in the proof of Lemma
F.3
, we let
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\mathcal{E}
be measurable and
\mathcal{P}
-positive sets, and aim to bound
\langle g_{1},\left(\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g_{2}\rangle
(where
g_{i}(c):=\frac{1}{\mathcal{P}\left(E_{i}\right)}\mathds{1}_{E_{i}}\left(c\right)
for
i=1,2
) independently of the choice of
E_{1},E_{2}
. If we do this we will have proven the result.

As before, we use the Fourier inversion theorem to write
\langle g_{1},\left(\varPhi\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g_{2}\rangle
as the integral of an inner product of Fourier transformed operators over the Fourier argument. Having
\lambda<\lambda_{O}
allows us to swap the integrals in this step. Then we can use Cauchy-Schwarz and the definitions of the operator norm and the bootstrap functions to extract factors of
\widehat{G}_{\mu_{\lambda}}(l)
and
\widehat{J}_{\mu_{\lambda}}\left(k,l\right)
from the inner product. The net result is

\displaystyle\left\langle g_{1},\left(\varPhi^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda}\mathcal{T}_{\lambda,k}\varPhi\right)\left(\overline{x}\right)g_{2}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\int\left\lvert\left\langle g_{1},\widehat{\varPhi}(l)^{2}\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda}(l)\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{\lambda,k}(l)\widehat{\varPhi}(l)g_{2}\right\rangle\right\rvert\frac{\mathrm{d}l}{\left(2\pi\right)^{d}}
